#341. iCloud and other Apple services are down in an apparent outage

The full extent of the disruption isn’t known yet.

A variety of Apple services, including iCloud, the App Store and Apple Pay, appear to be down in an apparent outage that has affected much of the company's online infrastructure. According to Downdetector, a handful of people began reporting connection issues around 11:30AM ET, before the number of outage reports jumped to the thousands around 12:29PM.

As of 2:34PM ET, Apple's System Status page currently lists a number of ongoing issues. It says iCloud account sign in "may be slow or unavailable," and that AppleCare on device is "temporarily unavailable" for all users. It notes the App Store and Apple Music are also down to due to an outage. Over on Downdetetor, users have also reported issues with the Apple Pay and device activation. "Unable to login to my Apple account across any devices," one person wrote on the website.

"Many of our systems are back up and running after an internal update caused some outages this morning. We're working to get the remaining systems online as soon as possible," an Apple spokesperson told Engadget.

Today's disruption follows last week's massive Microsoft outage, which took out many of the company's critical apps, including Outlook and Teams, for much of November 12. It was only the following morning that Microsoft was able to begin restoring its services. On Monday, the company blamed an old bug for the outage.

Update 2:35PM ET: Added comment from Apple.

#339. Yet another satellite company is jockeying for a rocket—but this one makes sense

“Governments need control of their critical space infrastructure, and Europe needs it most.”

Finnish satellite developer ICEYE announced Tuesday that it is seeking to buy Italian rocket-maker Avio for more than $2.2 billion in a transaction aimed at creating Europe’s first true vertically integrated end-to-end space company.

The announcement came as an all-cash voluntary tender offer from an Italian subsidiary of ICEYE to Avio’s shareholders, led by the Italian defense conglomerate Leonardo. Avio said its board of directors “has taken note of the notice” from ICEYE and is considering the purchase offer.

Avio builds the Vega C rocket, a solid-fueled launcher designed to carry up to 3.3 metric tons (7,300 pounds) of payload to low-Earth orbit. The Vega C has flown successfully seven times in eight attempts since 2022. It is a follow-on to an earlier version of the Vega rocket that amassed a 20-for-22 record since debuting in 2012.

ICEYE is, by far, the most successful space startup in Europe, where access to private capital, regulatory barriers, and fragmented markets often hinder startup growth, according to a recent report from the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development. ICEYE’s valuation stands at more than $12 billion after the company’s most recent fundraising round in June.

In a statement, ICEYE said it wants to join forces with Avio to meet Europe’s need for “sovereign space and defense capabilities.” ICEYE manufactures and operates its own satellites for radar surveillance and sells data to commercial customers and national governments, including Ukraine, which uses ICEYE imagery for targeting and tracking Russian troop movements.

“Governments need control of their critical space infrastructure, and Europe needs it most,” said Rafal Modrzewski, CEO and co-founder of ICEYE. “Avio has built the launch and propulsion capability Europe relies on. With ICEYE’s long-term capital behind it, we can integrate the supply chain, grow output and strengthen the European programs that already depend on it. Together we can build, launch and operate sovereign space systems end-to-end, made in Europe for governments across the world.”

Avio oversees the consortium of European companies that produces the Vega C rocket and conducts final assembly of Vega C rocket stages at its factory near Rome. For most of the Vega program’s history, Avio handed the rockets to the French launch provider Arianespace, which managed marketing, sales, and launch day operations in French Guiana. Avio recently took over those responsibilities to bring the entire Vega C operation in-house.

ICEYE has built and launched 84 radar reconnaissance satellites, providing routine overhead imagery of locations around the world, even during darkness or cloud cover. In addition to its commercial satellite fleet, ICEYE has sold turnkey satellites directly to national governments, including Finland, Poland, Germany, Sweden, Portugal, Greece, and the Netherlands.

Last month, ICEYE announced a partnership with Nokia, another Finnish company, to develop a low-Earth orbit satellite constellation to support European connectivity needs, particularly in the defense and security sectors. Ukraine’s Ministry of Defense announced a preliminary agreement, saying it intends to work with ICEYE and Nokia on the constellation, days after Ukraine reported new restrictions on its access to Starlink and Starshield broadband services offered by SpaceX.

“Modern warfare has proven that reliable communications are a matter of national security,” Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said. “We must all rely on capabilities that we can control.”

There’s a long way to go before ICEYE and Nokia can start deploying their constellation, but their goal is to begin launching 50 satellites per year starting in 2028. The companies have not disclosed expected figures for throughput and latency on the network, or a total number of satellites for global or regional coverage. Depending on altitude, it would likely require hundreds or thousands of satellites. SpaceX has more than 11,000 operating satellites in its Starlink constellation today.

What is clear, however, is that ICEYE’s future radar and broadband satellites will need rides to space, and Avio’s Vega C rocket needs business. The primary customer for Vega C is the European Space Agency, and the rest of the rocket’s future schedule is largely filled with satellites from European national governments. Vega C’s purely commercial business line is thin.

Through the proposed transaction, ICEYE says it would secure more access to launch services, and Avio would receive investment for higher production and more frequent flights of the Vega rocket family, which averages fewer than three per year.

The most recent data indicates a dedicated Vega C launch sells for approximately $55 million, just shy of the commercial price for a much more powerful dedicated SpaceX Falcon 9. Nearly all of ICEYE’s satellites, except for the first view demo spacecraft, have launched on Falcon 9 rideshare missions, which SpaceX intends to phase out after 2028. SpaceX sold rideshare launch slots for satellites of ICEYE’s size and mass for about $700,000. After accounting for potential savings after ICEYE’s potential purchase of Avio, an ICEYE satellite might be able to launch on Vega C for two to three times that price.

Avio and the European Space Agency are jointly funding the development of a new version of Vega, the Vega E, which replaces the top part of Vega C with a new higher-performing methane-fueled upper stage. ESA and Avio also signed a nearly $50 million contract last year to begin designing a reusable upper stage similar in concept and appearance to SpaceX’s Starship, although much smaller. The idea is to complete early design work on the reusable upper stage as a potential replacement for Vega E.

Business results seem to bear out the benefits of combining launch and satellite services under one roof. SpaceX’s Starlink network and Falcon 9 rocket would not have been as successful without the other. Rocket Lab, the No. 2 US launch company by launch rate, quickly branched out into satellite manufacturing after establishing the reliability of its Electron rocket. Pretty much every US launch provider has a role in satellite production or in-space services, or plans to, except for United Launch Alliance.

The coming launch crunch precipitated by SpaceX’s plans to retire the Falcon 9 has underscored the importance of assured access to space. SpaceX is prioritizing Starship launches for its own Starlink network and future Starmind AI satellites.

One of SpaceX’s competitors in the satellite broadband business, AST Space Mobile, announced last week that it is developing its own rocket to launch its communications network. It has launched most of its satellites to date on Falcon 9. Another company, Cowboy Space, plans to put orbiting data centers into space, bringing it into competition with SpaceX’s Starmind initiative. Cowboy Space, too, recently announced it was working on its own rocket.

There are reasons to disbelieve both companies’ launcher plans. Neither has any rocket development experience, and both are targeting first flights in 2028, a bewilderingly fast path to the launch pad. There are reasons to be skeptical of ICEYE and Avio, too, but if they do join forces, the combined company would boast established satellite and rocket production lines and backing from European governments seeking to reclaim sovereignty amid fraying ties with the United States.

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#338. What exactly are Flock cameras and how can you spot one?

Flock cameras are everywhere, and they’re easier to miss than you think.

Basic descriptions of Flock cameras fall woefully short of their true capabilities and potential uses. Most people are familiar with the automated license plate reader (ALPR) version, usually pole-mounted and paired with a solar panel. If that were all there was to it, this would be a very short article. But Flock Safety (Flock Group Inc.) has become deeply intertwined with the defining terminology of ALPR cameras.

In truth, there are more than a dozen manufacturers and vendors of ALPRs; their products just aren't called Flock cameras, and each has its own aesthetic characteristics. For all intents and purposes, however, they are identical. If you see Motorola on the side of one, it likely does much of what a Flock does: read plates and log vehicles.

Most are vertically oval, narrow in width and fairly small, roughly the size of your hand. They're designed to capture your license plate and the time and place you passed by. Most are also accompanied by a solar panel. Leonardo and Genetec make the most visually distinct versions. Leonardo's are blockier, rectangular in shape, with some elongated versions. Genetec's are horizontally oval, with the same narrow width as Flock's variations, only flipped. Motorola manufactures a mix of the two.

The problem is that some Flock cameras can easily be mistaken for a standard traffic camera and vice versa. Because of this, it's difficult to accept the absolute accuracy of a project like DeFlock, which uses volunteers to identify Flock cameras visually. There's no centralized or formal verification system to independently confirm the sightings.

Flock also sells more than ALPRs. Depending on what the customer wants, the system can log details like roof racks on cars and bumper stickers. Some setups have microphones and others can recognize things like clothes and beards.

Flock cameras usually turn up where traffic funnels. They're often mounted on poles at intersections and busy traffic areas. Since many homeowners associations (HOAs) are major Flock camera customers, it shouldn't be a surprise to find them at neighborhood entrances. Shopping centers aren't off the list of potential locations either. The aesthetics aren't irrelevant, but since several manufacturers make them, there are a few other telltale signs to look for as well.

Flock cameras are meant to capture license plates, so look at where they're aimed. Check for Flock's bird logo on the camera and a solar panel above it. Because most are self-powered, there's usually little to no additional equipment nearby. Red light and speed cameras are typically larger and wired to traffic signals or enforcement poles. White, elongated cameras are another lookalike, but they mostly help traffic signals adjust to traffic flow. Some states also mount signs next to speed cameras so they're easy to identify.

Keep in mind that there are other variations of Flock cameras out there. The Flock Condor is a mounted sphere that rotates 360 degrees and tracks people. Naturally, these are mounted near areas with heavy pedestrian traffic. Flock also makes a mobile security trailer, a gunshot detector and a drone-as-first-responder. Because companies are using video integration tools to convert standard IP cameras into plate readers, it will be increasingly difficult to identify Flock or Flock-type cameras.

As a rule of thumb, if it's pointed at you, it's potentially watching you. But for a broader view, check the Atlas of Surveillance, a searchable database from the Electronic Frontier Foundation that shows what technology law enforcement agencies use. Search your town or county to see whether police are using plate readers.

While some services like MapQuest can help you find Flock cameras in your neighborhood, be wary of crowdsourced maps. DeFlock uses OpenStreetMap data collected from volunteers, but there's no official public list, so a missing pin doesn't mean there isn't a Flock camera sitting there.

Pins can also be wrong, since it's easy to confuse a Flock camera with the countless security and traffic camera variations. A Flock camera doesn't have to belong to a public agency. Flock and Flock competitors also sell to HOAs and retailers.

If you want to find every Flock camera in your city, county or state, you have to put in the work. Submit a public records request, attend the next city council meeting or ask your HOA and local businesses.

#337. Can you use a keyboard and mouse with an Xbox Series X?

There's no question the PS5 has handed the Xbox Series X and S their collective rear ends from a sales perspective this console generation. That's a shame for Microsoft fans. Even though there are drawbacks you should understand before buying an Xbox Series X, for my money, it's still an excellent console. It's also one that supports a rich suite of accessibility options, as does its little sibling. One of the biggest? Fairly robust keyboard and mouse support.

Using traditional PC peripherals on a console might seem a bit outfield, but hey, the Dreamcast was doing it a quarter of a century ago, so there's definite precedent. There are actually a ton of Xbox games that offer at least partial mouse and keyboard support. Let's get into the mouse and keyboard options that should work with the Xbox Series X/S, and also discuss how to set them up. Plus, we'll examine just how many games support non-gamepad control schemes on Microsoft's current-gen consoles.

You sure can... but there are caveats. While Microsoft has done a commendable job at providing accessibility features for Xbox fans, mouse and keyboard support on Xbox Series X/S isn't universal across all games. Additionally, of the titles that do work with PC-based peripherals, not all of them offer native support. That means certain actions in non-native titles will require you to have a controller nearby to progress.

There are more restrictions, too. There's currently no way to connect a Bluetooth mouse or keyboard to either Xbox Series console. For those looking to hook up their favorite PC gizmos, you're looking at either wired peripherals or ones that come with 2.4GHz dongles.

That's slightly annoying. The best Xbox Series X/S accessories are generally wireless because you're likely sitting a decent distance away from your TV while playing your favorite games. Unless you keep your Xbox at a desk like a PC, wired mice and keyboards are probably out of the question for everyday living room play. Dongles it is, then.

Oh, and there's one more catch. You can't use a mouse to navigate around the Xbox dashboard, which is a bit of a bummer.

Setting up a keyboard and mouse on an Xbox Series X/S is relatively simple, but functionality beyond that is going to vary based on the specific game. The easiest and most practical way to get started with PC peripherals on your Xbox is to plug in a wireless keyboard dongle and a mouse dongle: You can use the two USB-A slots on the back of either console, or the one at the front.

From there, you can utilize the keyboard for typing on the Xbox dashboard (though as previously mentioned, mouse navigation is a no-go). Once you launch a game, things are a tad up in the air. The title may recognize your PC-style peripherals immediately, and you can start pointing and clicking. Or, you may well need your trusty Xbox controller to navigate into the settings menu, where you should be able to select mouse and keyboard inputs, if the title supports them. Microsoft provides the platform, but accessibility options are up to the individual developer, so this workflow will vary game to game.

Basically, prepare to always have your gamepad in reach if you want to use typical PC peripherals with an Xbox Series console. Just in case.

A surprising amount. We're not quite talking PC levels here, but the list of Xbox games with current mouse and keyboard support (as reported by Pure Xbox) shouldn't be sniffed at by WASD fans. Well over 100 titles feature levels of mouse and keyboard functionality at present, though native support isn't guaranteed in all of them. That means you'll occasionally have to break out your Xbox controller to navigate menus. If you hop on the Xbox Store, a game's listing page should tell you what level of mouse and keyboard support a title offers. Those that don't natively offer traditional PC controls will be marked as "Limited."

Looking for a few big hitters that support native mouse and keyboard controls on Xbox Series X/S? You'll find Microsoft Flight Simulator 2024, Halo Infinite and Gears of War: E-Day. Considering one of those games demands complex simulation controls that never quite felt at home on a pad, and the other two involve landing headshots on alien beasties from afar, mouse and keyboard sounds like a great way to experience all three. I always thought Master Chief's 2021 open world was overly criticized, and now I'm eager to replay it on Xbox, sniping hordes of Brutes with my favorite mouse, the Inphic F9 Mecha.

There are also competitive shooters like Overwatch that support cross-platform play, and will automatically pair you with fellow mouse and keyboard users if the game detects a PC-style control scheme on console. After all, in a fast-paced online shooter, bringing a gamepad to a mouse-and-keyboard match is like wielding a pillow in a lightsaber battle.

There are a number of benefits to using desktop peripherals with an Xbox Series X/S. Some aren't all that sexy — oooh, I can type my custom license plate in Forza Horizon 6 a teeny bit quicker — but others have far more practical use cases.

For simulation or strategy games with multiple mechanical layers and rich inventory systems, mouse and keyboard inputs are simply superior. I've had great experiences with these devices on my Xbox Series X playing Jurassic World Evolution 3, even though the dino-park management title does work pretty well with a controller. Keyboard support also gives you more quick buttons to assign should an Xbox title support it, which can definitely be useful in games that involve heavy inventory management, like Cyberpunk 2077.

The main reason you want keys and a mouse, though? For fans of first-person shooters, gamepads can never come close to the accuracy of blasting bullets with the utter precision of a mouse. Halo Infinite, Overwatch, the Call of Duty series and S.T.A.L.K.E.R. 2: Heart of Chornobyl are all well-situated for mouse and keyboard play, among many other headshot-heavy games.

#335. San Diego dumps both Flock cameras and smart streetlights

500+ cameras will go dark just 3 years after mayor urged approval.

On Monday evening, San Diego’s city council voted to end its contracts with two surveillance equipment vendors, Flock and Ubicquia. San Diego is one of the largest cities to end its relationship with Flock, the startup with an estimated valuation of $8.3 billion.

“We fucking won!” Lily Irani of the Transparent and Responsible Use of Surveillance Technology (TRUST) SD Coalition told Ars.

She and her colleagues have been among the loudest critics in recent years.

“The national energy creating left and right and center coalitions was the major cause [of the political victory],” she said, noting that it was only last month that one of the councilmembers revoked his support, creating an opening for a political majority to halt the surveillance.

That council member, Stephen Whitburn, said in an October press conference that he wanted his fellow citizens to “feel safe.”

“If Flock cameras are causing many San Diegans to feel unsafe, then they have lost effectiveness as a public safety tool,” he said at the time.

After the Monday vote, San Diego Mayor Todd Gloria said in a social media post that he “respect[ed] the Council’s decision” and would not veto the move to stop operation of the cameras across the city.

Andrew Smith, a spokesperson for Flock, told Ars in an email on Tuesday that the company also “respect[s] the city’s decision.” But Flock isn’t happy about it. “When a tool that is actively helping solve violent crimes is removed, public safety moves backward,” Smith wrote. “That has real consequences: cases will take longer to solve, organized retail theft crews will operate with fewer obstacles, a missing child may not be returned home, and victims may wait longer, or indefinitely, for justice.”

At Monday’s city council meeting, police representatives said there has been a 20 percent decline in vehicle theft since 2023, with 689 stolen vehicles recovered as a result of the Flock cameras.

The vote is a remarkable turnabout from just three years ago, when the mayor and other municipal leaders urged the city council to approve the program.

The city’s wholesale rejection of these devices included Ubicquia’s so-called “smart streetlights” that worked in concert with Flock’s AI-driven automatic license plate reader (ALPR) software. This cessation of smart streetlights marks the second time in less than a decade that San Diego has experimented with and then ultimately rejected this technology.

At one point, San Diego purchased thousands of smart streetlight systems from a different vendor, GE Current, but a previous mayoral administration shuttered them in 2020. After that, Ubicquia acquired the smart city platform and proposed a newer installation of 500 cameras in 2023 in partnership with Flock—and supported by police—which was installed in 2023.

“A reminder for every city, agency, and institution: if you create an advisory board of experts, listen to them!” she wrote. “Actually, earnestly listen to them. It’s cheaper than learning the hard way. Do better.”

That sentiment is echoed by Secure Justice, an Oakland-based advocacy group, which has been studying and monitoring Flock’s issues nationwide.

“We’ve documented at least 513 Flock contracts that have ended in red and blue states, in large and small cities,” Brian Hofer, the group’s executive director, emailed Ars. “It’s pretty clear that Americans do not consent to their government tracking them as we go about our daily lives.”

#334. 3 Body Problem finally returns to Netflix on January 14

It’s been nearly three years since the sci-fi adaptation first premiered.

The second season of Netflix's 3 Body Problem finally arrives on January 14, which will place it nearly three years after the series first premiered. The streamer has dropped an action-packed trailer that's filled to the brim with sci-fi setpieces and it looks pretty nifty.

The second season will continue to adapt Cixin Liu's Remembrance of Earth's Past novel trilogy.A third (and final season) has already been greenlit. The books have been an international phenomenon for decades and the first season was a modest hit for the platform.

For the uninitiated, 3 Body Problem was co-created by David Benioff and D.B. Weiss. Yes, those are the two guys who made Game of Thrones. Alexander Woo is also a co-creator and he wrote for programs like The Terror, True Blood and Wonderfalls.

The series is basically a hard sci-fi take on an alien invasion story spread across multiple generations. The first season certainly looked gorgeous and featured standout performances from Benedict Wong, Liam Cunningham and Rosalind Chao, among others.

We don't exactly know how the second season will handle some of the more narratively finicky aspects of the novel (no spoilers here.) Woo has always said that the "guiding principle" of the series is to "retain the spirit" of the source books, so we'll have to wait and see.

There is a spot of bad news here. The first season consisted of eight episodes, but the second includes just six. All six episodes were directed by Game of Thrones veterans Jeremy Podeswa and Miguel Sapochnik.

#333. Shania Twain calls for equal pay and more diversity in country music

Shania Twain is standing up for others in country music.

The legendary star in the genre used her acceptance speech for the Equal Play Award at Sunday’s CMT Awards to call for more equity and diversity in the country music industry.

“When I wrote the phrase ‘Man I feel like a woman’ I honestly had no idea at the time it would become an undercurrent of a decades-long career,” she said, referring to one of her biggest hits. “It’s amazing how one statement can empower so many.”

The phrase, Twain said, “has been adopted by an array of fantastic communities all around the world and become a genuine path of power…which brings me so much joy.”

“I believe in an all-inclusive country music. We’re family,” Twain said. “This is a genre of music with a rich history that raised and nurtured my own songwriting and performance and recording career from childhood. Currently, the industry standard does a real disservice to this.”

Twain, who was presented the award by rapper Megan Thee Stallion, shouted out some of the diverse acts she is spotlighting on her “Queen of Me” tour this year, including Lily Rose, Lindsay Ell, Hailey Whitters, Mickey Guyton and others.

“I will continue to do my best as a trailblazer,” Twain said. “Together, let’s ensure all our fellow artists get equal play regardless of gender, age or race.”

She also performed acappella with the African American singing group, The BoykinZ, while presenting an award onstage. Twain connected with the group, who are sisters and rising country stars, after Kelly Clarkson surprised them recently by bringing Twain out to meet them during an episode of Clarkson’s daytime talk show.

#331. Google Wallet is launching on iOS

In case you, for some reason, don’t want to use the nearly identical Apple Wallet.

Google and Apple have a long history of not launching first-party software for their rival's hardware, so it's usually a bit of a surprise when these companies manage to make nice. The latest instance is Google Wallet, which is available starting today on iOS for US users.

It's an odd integration considering Apple has its own Wallet app that's very similar to Google's offering. Both can handle digital payment tools, they store tickets and travel information and they safely store IDs and credentials.

The notable standout in today's announcement is the positioning of Google Wallet as a hub for agentic purchases made across Google tools. That seems like the only area where this could be a markedly different app experience, since Apple has been more cautious about agentic AI than its competitors to date. The iOS version of Google Wallet also will not support tap-to-pay, but the blog post didn't specify whether that feature might be added in the future.

#330. Instagram is helping creators crack down on copycat videos

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Meta will let creators decide to hide copies of their videos flagged by a new ‘content protection’ feature.

Meta will let creators decide to hide copies of their videos flagged by a new ‘content protection’ feature.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Instagram is beginning to roll out a “content protection” feature that lets creators see and review copies of their videos posted to the platform.

“For eligible creators who enroll, content protection is a tool that automatically looks out for visual matches of your original videos, alerts you when we find a match across Instagram and Facebook, and lets you decide what happens next,” Meta says in a blog post. Meta launched a similar tool for Facebook last year to help users “safeguard” their Reels and “maintain control” over their creative work.

When an Instagram creator is notified of a matched video, they can see how many views the match has and decide if they want to track the video, hide it from everyone on Instagram and Facebook, add a credit link, or release their claim on the match.

After a creator enrolls, every “original video” they post on Instagram is protected, and creators can retroactively protect older videos manually. Creators can also put accounts on an allow list that lets them use their videos.

In addition, Instagram will start flagging AI-generated videos that “match the spoken words” in a creator’s original video but have visuals replaced with AI. Initially, Instagram will test this with a “small group of creators” in “the coming weeks.” The platform says it’s exploring ways to limit the reach of content and profiles that “use AI tools to post this type of content repeatedly.”

#329. There's a chance your Xbox Series X is plugged into the wrong HDMI port

Make sure you're getting the most out of your Xbox Series X.

This sounds like an idiotic suggestion at first, right? Surely all you need to do is plug the HDMI cable that came with your Series X into your television, and you'll be ready to enjoy the best Xbox games, no? It's not always that simple.

Though your current TV may support HDMI 2.1, the input that ensures the Series X can display its maximum resolution and framerate, that doesn't mean all of your TV's ports are 2.1 compliant. This latest high-def standard really matters, as without it, cutting-edge visual features like Variable Refresh Rate are off the table.

It's important to know the specifics of your current TV. For instance, if it's an aging model that only supports HDMI 2.0, you can stop reading this article now, and content yourself with a top output of 1440p/120 fps or 4K/60 fps from your Series X. However, if you own a semi-modern budget flatscreen, there's a good chance only one or two of its ports will be compatible with HDMI 2.1, enabling 4K/120 fps and framerate-smoothing VRR from the Xbox.

Let's get to know your TV's HDMI sockets a little better, discuss whether you should upgrade the cable that came in the box with your Series X, and go over why HDMI 2.1 is so important for current-gen gaming.

Possibly, but it's entirely dependent on the model of TV you own. If you picked up your screen within the last five years and it wasn't an ultra-budget panel, it probably supports HDMI 2.1. But remember, even with a newish TV, there's no guarantee that all of its ports are HDMI 2.1 compatible.

Most modern high-end sets will have three to four HDMI 2.1 ports total, so if you've bought a premium OLED or MicroLED panel in the last couple of years, it's likely that all of its HDMI inputs will comply. If your set is a good few years old and was one of the first models to support HDMI 2.1 features, there's a decent chance that only one of its ports can take advantage of the Xbox Series X's 4K/120 max output.

To assess your TV's port situation without any awkward bending or flashlight usage, check out your model's online manual. Otherwise, individual inputs tend to be labeled on the backside of the TV itself. My 2023 LG OLED has four HDMI 2.1 ports, so thankfully I never have to worry about connection issues, but it's often the case that certain brands and less-recent models favor HDMI 2.1 in specific slots. Take Sony as an example: Older Bravia displays usually reserve their 2.1 configurations to ports three and four. Again, it entirely depends on your specific model. And when in doubt, reference your TV's online manual.

In the vast majority of circumstances, you should definitely not replace the Ultra High Speed HDMI cable that came in the box with your beloved Microsoft machine. That puppy is fully HDMI 2.1 compliant   and it's likely to have a far greater shelf life than any Xbox Series X. For most folks, there really is no reason to upgrade the Series X's standard cable. Of course, there are exceptions.

Depending on the logistics of your gaming setup, you might need a longer HDMI cable than the one Microsoft provides. It's 6 feet long, which will be plenty for most Xbox players. But if for some reason you can't place your Series X as close to the TV as you'd like, investing in a lengthier cable might be worthwhile. Just make sure it's HDMI 2.1 certified and you're good to go.

There's also the case for folks who wall-mount their TVs. I can personally attest that getting the Xbox's HDMI 2.1 cable into one of my LG G3 OLED's outputs was a nightmare, as my screen is flush to the wall. In these instances, it's definitely worth investing in an HDMI cable with an ultra-thin connector. Again, just check that it's HDMI 2.1 compliant.

Please, please never been suckered into buying a "premium" HDMI cable. That word is basically a money-grabbing marketing tool used to lure in impulsive AV enthusiasts. I should know: I still have a painful memory of being sweet-talked into purchasing a $75 HDMI cable back in 2015, right after picking up my first OLED TV. Trust me, the ultra-fast cable that came with your Xbox Series X will do just fine.

There are actually a surprising number of games that can hit 120 fps on the Xbox Series X (and even its smaller sibling, the Series S), as noted by Pure Xbox. The big caveat? Few Xbox games can run at such a high framerate while also outputting at 4K resolution. Last-gen titles that got Series X upgrades like Halo: The Master Chief Collection can output at a full-fat 4K/120, but even popular first-party games like Indiana Jones and the Great Circle, Forza Horizon 6 and Doom the Dark Ages are locked at 60 fps on the console.

Otherwise, the Series X often hits 120 fps with an associated drop in resolution. Pretty much every modern Call of Duty installment supports 120 fps, from Black Ops Cold War right up to this year's Modern Warfare 4. The catch? Dynamic resolution is usually deployed, often dropping in-game clarity to either 1080p or 1400p. If you want faster framerates on Series X, in most cases you're going to have to accept a dip in resolution.

There are drawbacks you should know about before buying an Xbox Series X, but when push comes to shove, its visual output really boils down to having a proper HDMI cable, and the age and manufacturer of the connected display. To get the most out of your Xbox Series X, start by making sure your HDMI 2.1 ports and cables are in order.

#328. Microsoft calls Copilot "the new OS for work" at Ignite keynote

Word, Excel, and PowerPoint now live inside Copilot; always-on agents are next.

Opening the keynote at its Ignite event, Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella outlined the company’s vision for its new “frontier ecosystem,” announcing Tuesday the general availability of Word, Excel, and PowerPoint inside Copilot, which he described as “the new OS for work.”

The announcement is a striking inversion of how we’ve traditionally used software. Rather than an app like Word being the attraction and Clippy the assistant to help you use it, Microsoft envisions the new Copilot integrations will see agents driving things, with the software packages riding shotgun. Applications will become plugin components of the agent’s work, backed by an organizational knowledge layer that draws context from emails, meetings, calendars, chats, and files. Users will now be able to create documents, spreadsheets, and presentations directly in Copilot, with the resulting files remaining editable in their respective Office applications.

The rollout marks a major step toward the Copilot redesign the company first announced in September. Parts of that vision are now becoming “generally available,” as Nadella puts it, with autonomous agents and new governance tools arriving alongside them.

Copilot Autopilot (not to be confused with Windows Autopilot) is a “persistent, proactive agent for personal productivity” according to Microsoft. In a nutshell, Autopilot is a cloud-based autonomous AI agent that users can delegate responsibilities to, not just single tasks. Autopilot can be authorized to work across services like email and calendars and complete tasks assigned to it as part of its role. Autopilot will use usage-based billing via Copilot Credits, but final pricing has not been announced ahead of general availability.

Also announced as generally available at the keynote speech was Microsoft IQ, an intelligence platform that houses Work IQ, a context layer that helps agents understand a company’s communications, relationships, and ongoing work. Nadella described Work IQ as perhaps a company’s most valuable database: “It has a deep understanding of the people, their relationships to other people, your emails, your meetings, chats, projects that make up all the fabric of work.” Whether companies want autonomous agents to have access to internal documents and messaging data will have to be determined by their own internal IT review.

Copilot Workspaces is a collaborative environment currently available through Microsoft’s Frontier early-access program, but coming soon will be the ability to bring personal Autopilot agents into Workspaces. Early in the keynote address, Nadella said that “Until now, AI interactions have mostly been single-player. Workspaces makes it a truly multiplayer experience, where people and agents drive towards a common goal, with shared context.”

Trust, the third pillar of Microsoft’s new frontier ecosystem, was something that Nadella continued returning to during his presentation, and not in a way that some might expect. “We find ourselves in a place where alignment is elusive. Mechanistic understanding of these models is elusive. The question is: How can you even trust all this powerful technology? The reality is, by definition, you can’t,” he said.

In a departure from the norms of “AI is magic” corporate hype sentiment, Nadella conceded that the internal workings of AI thought processes remain difficult to understand, and models cannot be trusted to govern themselves, regardless of whether their answers are accurate. Nadella described the solution as “an architectural imperative to separate the supply of intelligence” from the authority granted to it. Microsoft says each Autopilot agent will have a unique ID, and administrators can restrict permissions or block it entirely. All agent activities are logged for human review, and Autopilot can ask for human approval before engaging in certain actions.

The promised agent controls and admission that AI systems can’t always be trusted are meaningful, but Microsoft is asking businesses and users to place their trust in the company’s own safeguards instead, though the effectiveness of those safeguards against mistakes, rogue actions, or data exposure will still have to be demonstrated. In Microsoft’s view, the solution to untrustworthy intelligence is not to make AI agents trustworthy, but to control what they’re permitted to do. Businesses will have to decide how much autonomy they delegate to agents.

Along with the guardrails, Microsoft announced what it calls the “AI Transparency Initiative.” The effort has partnered with 40-plus major companies, including Adobe and Mastercard, to develop open, industrywide standards for AI governance and security. Nadella described the initiative as a way for industry to “help establish an open standard that makes AI safer, more transparent, and more accessible.”

Along with the other announced additions, Microsoft also announced expanding capabilities for Agent 365, its existing control plane for managing AI agents across enterprise environments. The new agent sheriff extends existing identity, security, and management systems to AI agents. Microsoft describes the objective as providing “observability at every layer of the stack.” Agent 365 offers governance controls over agent behavior in what Microsoft says is “autonomous in execution, but bounded and supervised.”

Along with Agent 365 comes Agent 365 Business Analytics: “You can simply think of this as an ERP system of AI usage. You can see exactly what’s driving the spend and connect the spend to the outcomes that drive business value,” said Nadella. Customers will be able to see real-world costs associated with AI token-spend, and compare those metrics to the value of work performed. Microsoft acknowledged that the estimations of assisted work hours are determined by a model, and not a direct measurement of time that may have been saved. This is a telling concession for Microsoft to make as businesses evaluate whether there will be an ROI on AI implementations, but according to Judson Althoff, CEO of Microsoft Commercial Business, “anything you can do with Claude or ChatGPT, you can do better with Copilot, for less.”

Nadella offered only a brief acknowledgment of last week’s outage that disrupted access to services like Exchange Online and Teams for users across the globe. “Last week was a reminder of how critical digital infrastructure is as the dial tone for the world,” he said. “We know the trust you place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously, and in that context I know we have work to do.”

That statement was all the time the elephant in the room was given, but Microsoft did seem to acknowledge the public’s growing concern over data center projects by highlighting the previously discussed “community-first AI infrastructure plan.” Not a surprise in a presentation that asks organizations to extend more autonomy to software agents, at a time when Microsoft’s existing infrastructure is under increased scrutiny.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#327. Not even Sam Altman is safe from AI deepfakes on Instagram

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Meta’s chief AI officer posted an AI video of a top competitor. It’s allowed under Meta’s AI policy.﻿

Meta’s chief AI officer posted an AI video of a top competitor. It’s allowed under Meta’s AI policy.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Instagram is filled with people posting deepfaked content — including, as of late, from one of Meta’s most prominent executives.

Earlier in November, Alexandr Wang, Meta’s chief AI officer, shared a short clip to promote Muse, the company’s consumer AI agent. In the AI-generated clip, Wang, Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, and Jolly, the anthropomorphic mascot of Muse, are on a four-way video call. When Altman joins the call he’s dressed like Jolly, wearing a furry hooded suit and face paint to look like the cutesy character. The clip is captioned “When the homies meet about openai product strategy,” a reference to the AI agent race and the recent popularity of Muse.

Just a few years ago, AI content portraying real people led to strong public statements rebuking the use of the technology to imitate humans. In some cases legal action was threatened. Now, AI content has become part of the texture of a new reality, especially on social media. Leading up to the midterm elections, for example, political groups ran millions of dollars’ worth of AI-generated ads attacking opponents.

Wang has been doing a lot of what some have described as “anti-PR” to promote Muse, tweeting hundreds of times and referring to more traditional marketers as “corposlops.” Still, a major tech executive posting an AI video of a competitor is notable, especially on a platform that’s been criticized recently over AI-generated content. After an AI-generated image of actress Mia Goth gained traction in October, the Pearl star’s representative told The Verge it was “incomprehensible” that Instagram allowed the image to spread instead of focusing on the “protection and integrity of a public figure’s likeness.” Other celebrities, including Scarlett Johansson, are regularly the subject of sexualized AI imagery; Meta has removed some accounts in the past that it found to be posing as someone else and using their likeness.

Meta requires AI-generated videos depicting people to be labeled as AI, like Wang’s post is. Static images, however, have no such requirement, meaning the AI image of Goth had no disclosure. To make matters worse, the image was shared by a fan account with a blue verification badge given by Instagram.

Reached for comment, Meta spokesperson Tracy Clayton noted that Wang’s post is labeled as AI content. Clayton also pointed out that Altman has made his likeness available for AI content generation, as when OpenAI launched its Sora app (and it was quickly flooded with fake Altman videos). OpenAI didn’t respond to requests for comment.

#325. 2023 March Madness: Will UConn or San Diego State triumph in the national championship game that 'means everything?'

The University of Connecticut won its fifth men’s basketball national title with a 76-59 victory over San Diego State University on Monday night at NRG Stadium in Houston.

Senior guard Tristen Newton led UConn (31-8) with 19 points and 10 rebounds while Final Four Most Outstanding Player Adama Sanogo, a junior forward, chipped in with 17 points and 10 rebounds.

“We weren’t ranked going into the year so we had the chip on our shoulder,” UConn head coach Dan Hurley told game broadcaster CBS. “We knew the level that we could play at, even through those dark times,” he added, referencing the team’s six losses in eight games during the regular season.

He said going into the tournament his group had confidence garnered during the season.

“And when you have the type of leaders like Andre Jackson (game-high six assists Monday) and Adama Sanogo, they kept this team together, got us back on track and we knew we were the best team in the tournament going in and we just had to play to our level,” he added.

San Diego State (32-7) was topped by Keshad Johnson who had 14 points.

UConn trailed very early but San Diego State was undone by an 11-minute, eight-second stretch in which they scored just five free throws and missed 12 consecutive shots from the field. The Huskies went from down 10-6 to up 36-24 at halftime.

The Aztecs made a run midway through the second half and narrowed the deficit to five at 60-55 with 5:19 to play but the Huskies scored the next nine to take a comfortable lead into the final two minutes.

“We battled. Battled back to five in the second half, but gave them too much separation,” San Diego State coach Brian Dutcher said. “We had to be at our best. We weren’t at our best. A lot had to do with UConn.”

Senior guard Adam Seiko told reporters they gave themselves a chance with their second half comeback but UConn “just made a little bit more plays” at the end.

“They have a lot of weapons. They were pretty good,” said Matt Bradley, also a senior guard. “To beat them, we had to make shots. I shot poorly. And you had to have a really good game to beat those dudes on the offensive end.”

UConn won each of its six tournament games by at least 10 points, with its closest game being a 13-point win over the University of Miami in the national semifinals.

“I just want to thank my teammates, my coaches who believed in me. If it were not for them I would not be here right now,” Sanogo told CBS.

Jordan Hawkins, who scored 16 points for UConn, talked about winning the crown one day after his cousin, Angel Reese of Louisiana State University, won the women’s title.

“I mean it’s absolutely amazing that we both get this opportunity and I mean the family reunion is going to be great so that’s all I know,” he said.

UConn enters rarefied air as only the sixth team to win five NCAA men’s basketball championships, joining UCLA (11), Kentucky (eight), North Carolina (six), Duke (five) and Indiana (five). All of UConn’s titles have come since 1999 with the most recent before Monday occurring in 2014.

UConn’s women’s teams have won 11 basketball national titles.

CNN’s Steve Almasy and Issy Ronald contributed to this report.

#324. Waymo is pausing all freeway rides after California DMV says the robotaxis are 'imminent hazard to public safety'

Restrictions were made after a driverless Waymo was caught trying to pass a Highway Patrol vehicle that was slowing traffic.

California's Department of Motor Vehicles has barred Waymo from operating its robotaxis on state freeways, saying in a statement to Engadget that the company's driverless operation poses an "imminent hazard to public safety." The restriction has been in place since November 11, according to the DMV. In response, Waymo says it has temporarily restricted freeway driving in all of its US markets.

The DMV cited an incident of three Waymo robotaxis entering a restricted construction zone near Burlingame, and a separate incident of a Waymo trying to pass a California Highway Patrol car that was slowing traffic as part of its justification. Past examples of Waymo robotaxis attempting to drive through freeway construction sites were part of the reason Waymo voluntarily paused freeway service in May and later recalled 3,800 vehicles in June. The company started letting its vehicles drive on highways again in July, but whatever fixes were introduced apparently didn't fully address the problem.

"Safety is at the foundation of everything we do," Waymo said in a statement to Engadget. "Following recent freeway construction zone encounters on U.S. 101 in California that did not involve any injuries or collisions, the California DMV has implemented an operating restriction on freeway operations in the state. In alignment with our safety-first principles, we have also temporarily restricted freeway operations across our other operating markets to evaluate our performance."

In a state like California, losing access to the freeway could significantly change the routes robotaxis are able to take, the length of rides and the cost of using Waymo over a service like Lyft or Uber. Waymo will have to prove to the DMV that it has "taken effective corrective actions to address the deficiencies that precipitated the restrictions" before the freeway ban is lifted.

The company says it will continue offering service on surface streets while it works with regulators. It may also test freeway driving in vehicles with human safety drivers outside of California. Waymo began running robotaxis on California freeways in November 2025, and expanded its service area across Sacramento, the Bay Area and San Diego in August 2026.

#323. NVIDIA's board chair says the company is not considering reinstating Jensen Huang as CEO

"The NVIDIA board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO."

During the call, Kress said that the company was prioritizing its employees. "We will take care of our employees during this transition to make sure that they are safe and making sure the work can be done in the current environment," she said. Mostly though, she tried to keep the call focused on NVIDIA's blockbuster quarter, saying that "from [a] business perspective, it's business as usual," for the chip giant.

Since Huang's resignation on November 12, NVIDIA's stock had fallen about 11 percent.

Ahead of Tuesday's earnings, Huang said he was willing to return to the company. "If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO," he wrote in a statement shared on X. Given the timing of the post, Huang's statement could be read as an attempt to pressure NVIDIA's board to give him his position back.

During the call, Jones said the company's board of directors is taking the search for a new CEO "very seriously" and was considering internal and external candidates. "The board appreciates that we're living in a world where the advanced silicon technologies and national security dynamics are making major changes in our marketplace," he said. "Our new CEO will have to navigate those those waters very carefully, so the the the search will be thorough, and we want to install the CEO, the permanent CEO, as quickly as possible. But we won't expedite the CEO before finding exactly the right person."

In addition to the search for a new leader, NVIDIA will have its hands full dealing with fallout from recent revelations. According to a report The Wall Street Journal published before market close, the US Securities and Exchange Commission recently began investigating NVIDIA. The outlet said it could not immediately learn the focus of the probe. The FBI is also looking into the matter, according to President Donald Trump.

If Kress is rattled by any of this, it wasn't immediately apparent in her remarks to analysts. "Our momentum is real," she said at the end of the call. "Our demand is accelerating. Our supply is growing. Our customers and our partners are with us, and employees are committed. We've got this."

#322. Nike’s new air sole technology can mix soft and firm cushioning

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The first shoes with Nike’s new Tuned Air technology will be available for runners starting in late 2027.﻿

The first shoes with Nike’s new Tuned Air technology will be available for runners starting in late 2027.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

In 2024, Nike announced its Air Max 1000 that were manufactured using a 3D-printing process resulting in a shoe that had a uniform silhouette but varying areas of support and flexibility throughout. The company is now doing something similar with its iconic air-filled soles using its new Tuned Air technology. While previously the level of cushioning in Nike Air shoes was uniform across the entire sole, a new manufacturing process can produce multiple air pockets with varying levels of cushioning and stability.

Nike has been putting air pockets into its shoes for nearly 50 years as a way to increase comfort and cushioning while making them lighter. Its air pockets have even found their way into apparel as a way to improve insulation by trapping body heat, but Nike plans to use its new Tuned Air technology as a way to customize the cushioning experience in its shoes and tailor them to specific athletes and sports. The first shoes featuring the technology are expected to arrive in late 2027 and will be targeted at runners.

While one of Nike’s earliest successes was track shoes with a grip design inspired by a waffle iron, the company credits the “vibrant colors on magazine covers” as the inspiration for its latest shoe technology. A Tuned Air sole is manufactured using a “proprietary digital print and selective lamination process” where an ink is applied in specific patterns that define where the layers bond to each other. This process can be used to create internal pockets in an unlimited combination of shapes and sizes, and by fine-tuning how much air is in each of those pockets, some parts of the sole can feel firmer while others feel softer.

The Tuned Air technology will not only allow Nike to highly customize the fit and feel of air sole shoes for different sports, it will also help keep the benefits of the custom tuning consistent across different sizes. The new manufacturing process will also help reduce the amount of time it takes Nike to go from a concept for a new shoe to a final design ready for production. “Prototypes that once took three weeks can now be made in three days,” the company claims in a release today. That gives Nike’s designers more time to refine new shoes, and more opportunity to experiment.

#321. The dangers of using your old, damaged charging cables

Don't let a damaged $5 cable cost you thousands in fire damage or medical bills.

A charging cable has two important jobs: keep your devices charged, and do so without causing you or the item any damage. You can't expect a cable to last forever, though. After being plugged in and disconnected countless times, it's going to degrade, bend, break down, or split apart. The end result is an unsafe cable that will need replacing.

If you don't replace a compromised charging cable, you're risking damage to your device or being burnt yourself every time you plug it in. For the sake of a few dollars, you're better off buying a new one than persevering with a cable that should have been recycled long ago.

Pulling a charging cable out by the wire puts strain on the connection behind the plug. And you're going to bend it sharply in roughly the same places, especially at the two ends. This wears down the fine strands of metal inside before there's any sign of outer damage.

The funny part is that even if the cable is damaged inside, it can still work, although the connection might keep cutting out. Even if it's to the point where you see a lot of physical damage, it may be able to provide a charge. The remaining strands are carrying the weight, letting the current charge through.

The problem with this is pure physics. With fewer intact strands carrying the current, you're going to see increased electrical resistance. More energy will turn into heat as the current passes through, with that heat concentrated in a smaller area. This is where a damaged cable can start to overheat, especially when it's carrying the higher current used for fast charging.

You might have a charger with safety features designed to protect against overheating or short-circuits, to name but a few. You can't rely on this, though. A damaged connection can still heat up, even if the current passing through your cable is within the safety limits. You might get a warning on your phone about this, if your device has that functionality.

The immediate danger is getting either burned or electrocuted. Heat will concentrate around a damaged connector, making it hot enough to burn your fingers and melt the plastic around it. While the US Consumer Product Safety Commission can recall poorly-made cables, like this heyday Lightning cable sold at Target in 2019, your cable might simply be damaged by overuse. Even with a good cable, charging your device underneath a pillow or blanket isn't a good idea, as this traps the heat.

Potential problems include damaging your device's battery or reducing its long-term health from the inconsistent power going into it. It's also possible for the compromised electrical signals to fry your device's USB port. There's also a risk of electrical shock if you're using a frayed cable. USB leads carry low voltage, but you don't want to chance it. Further shock dangers can occur if the cable is connected to an adapter, or if the adapter itself is exposed. Moisture adds to the risk, too.

The worst-case scenario is a fire. I'm not suggesting every frayed USB cable will lead to a fire, but it's a possibility. A properly functioning charger should have protections against short circuits and excessive current. If the equipment is poorly made, you're not going to have confidence that those protections work. Obviously, the consequences if a fire starts are much bigger than just a broken cable or device.

You can't expect your home's circuit breaker to protect from this. It responds to faults in the household circuit, but depending on how much current the faulty equipment draws, the breaker might not trip at all. A cable can still become dangerously hot without your electricity cutting out.

Electrical tape (or any sort of tape) is a common solution to wrap around a damaged section of cable. It'll cover the split, but those broken strands inside aren't getting fixed and you shouldn't pretend it's as safe as a new cable. As a temporary fix to charge your device until a replacement arrives tomorrow, it might be fine, but you shouldn't use this for any longer than you have to.

In most cases, you'll spot when a cable is in bad shape because your device flickers in and out of charging, or it might only charge when you're holding the cable at a particular angle. Propping it up or bending it can provide a quick fix but won't help in the long-term.

You might also wonder if the problem is with the cable or the port you're plugging it into. There's an easy answer to this: change the cable. If you've got a spare that works perfectly, it's the cable. If it doesn't, it's likely the port. Double-check using a different charger to be sure.

Shine a light inside the port to get a better look. If you see any debris, you might want to give the port a cleaning, although Google advises against putting anything into your phone's port. Lightly tapping your phone against your hand with the port facing down can help dislodge buildup inside.

You might think this is all somewhat hyperbolic. It's also a bit hypocritical, as I've used fraying charging cables on more than one occasion in a rush (and later regretted it). The cost for a new cable is far less than replacing a broken piece of equipment, covering a medical bill or dealing with fire damage. Unless you're an electrical engineer with the skills to strip and repair a wire and test it afterwards, and have the desire to do so, recycle your old cable or repurpose it.

#317. CFTC tells Kalshi to stop letting people bet on Trump's speeches

The prediction market platform's CEO has complied but opposed the move.

On November 13, the US Commodity Futures Trading Commission ordered prediction market platform Kalshi to halt mention markets centered on President Donald Trump. The contracts in question were a "TRUMPSAY" series and one titled "What nicknames will Trump say before 2027?" These contract types allowed users to bet on whether the president would say particular words in a given time frame.

The CFTC wants to launch "a proceeding to determine whether Kalshi's certification that the contracts comply with the Commodity Exchange Act and the Commission's regulations was false." The prediction market has complied with the order, although Kalshi CEO Tarek Mansour said he is opposed to pausing these bets.

The agency released a staff advisory on these predictions in September, noting that they "present a heightened risk of manipulation because their settlement turns on the discrete conduct of a person that may be neither independently generated nor externally verifiable."

Mention markets around presidential speeches, as well as predictions markets' possible ties to the current administration, have already stirred controversy. White House teleprompter operator Gabriel Perez was placed on administrative leave this summer after he reportedly made more than $100,000 betting on things that Trump would say. At that time, the CFTC said it would be willing to settle with Perez if he returned the money. Kalshi has also parted ways with Donald Trump Jr as one of its paid advisors; the move was dubbed a mutual decision based on Trump Jr's investment firm putting more funds into rival Polymarket.

The CFTC has mostly used a light touch in overseeing prediction markets, although the agency has been aggressive in staking its legal claim to be the sole regulator of these businesses. It has sued multiple states that tried to take prediction markets to court on charges that they constitute illegal gambling.

#316. Nvidia’s Q3 earnings hit the mark, without mentioning the whole Jensen Huang thing

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Nvidia’s Q3 revenue has more than doubled, with income from data centers up 110 percent from last year, to $107.6 billion.

Nvidia’s Q3 revenue has more than doubled, with income from data centers up 110 percent from last year, to $107.6 billion.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Interim Nvidia CEO Colette Kress announced the company’s Q3 earnings on Tuesday, without initially mentioning former CEO Jensen Huang or the details of his departure, or possible attempt at a return. On the earnings call accompanying the release, board chair Harvey Jones kicked things off by saying the board “does not intend to reinstate Huang as CEO,” and that “we will not be addressing or taking questions on today’s call regarding the circumstances of Jensen’s resignation and the ongoing investigation,” before Kress started with her comments on the results.

As it predicted in the last financial update, Nvidia is now on the list of companies that have pulled in over $100 billion in a single quarter, with revenues of $115.8 billion, up 103 percent from the same period last year and higher than its expectation of $108 billion. This quarter’s figures include data center revenues of $107.6 billion, which were up 110 percent, and edge computing (which includes the consumer gaming PC business and devices like the new RTX Spark laptops) revenue of $8.2 billion, up 42 percent.

The company reported $65.3 billion in profit, a new record.

“Our momentum is real. NVIDIA is an exceptional technology leader, powered by the relentless innovation of our people,” said Colette Kress, interim CEO and executive vice president and chief financial officer of NVIDIA. “Vera Rubin is off to an explosive start, our roadmap is on track and our ambition has never been greater.”

In the fourth quarter, Nvidia expects revenues of $130 billion.

Update, November 17th: Added comments from Nvidia’s earnings call.

#315. 5 old-school charging ports you probably forgot phones had before USB-C

Back when chargers were less standardized, we had some interesting connectors for phone power.

From its reversible design to improved power delivery and high-speed data transfers, USB-C is the connector to beat for smartphones today. A sweeping EU mandate cemented its pole position in the race toward a universal standard. Earlier ports, charming as they were, were often bulkier and not as feature-rich. The lack of a universal connector led to a significant amount of e-waste from switching chargers every time you tried a phone from a new manufacturer.

Despite their limited bandwidth and slower charging speeds, I can't help but look back fondly at these unique connectors from the old days. They came in all shapes and sizes based on what manufacturers thought their customers wanted and what was technically feasible at the time. Before features like Apple's AirDrop, you had to rely on a physical cable to transfer large files, and your collection might have been huge if you jumped between phone manufacturers.

Let's check out some of the old-school, sometimes forgotten or even bizarre, charging ports that made it into yesteryear's phones before the advent of USB-C.

First seen on Motorola phones in 2000, the 17-pin CE Bus connector was gigantic. While it wasn't reversible and had hooks that kept it in place, the connector was notable for managing power, data and audio together. It was used in the Motorola V60, one of the most recognizable models of the clamshell era. The first phone with iTunes support, the Motorola ROKR E1, also launched with the port.

Thinner phones like the Razr V3 switched over to Mini-USB, making the CE Bus connector obsolete. Other companies moving toward Mini-USB also helped lower costs, compared to having tons of proprietary solutions to transfer data or charge devices.

Nokia's Pop-Port connector was built with external accessories in mind, from earphones to data transfer cables. Owners of the famed Nokia 7210 from 2002 will remember its 14-pin port supporting camera accessory add-ons and car kits. It wasn't very secure, however, and its plastic clips wore down with time. This made listening to music a chore, especially when you went jogging.

While the Pop-Port could be used for charging, Nokia devices of the time usually relied on a separate 2.0 mm thin-tip barrel connector instead. As Mini-USB ports became common, the Pop-Port slowly faded from existence.

Apple's proprietary power connector first showed up in 2003 on the third-gen iPod. This flat and wide port was then used across the first iPhone and iPad models. Speaker systems like Bose's SoundDock and Apple's iPod Hi-Fi also relied on the port. All those pins in the connector helped ensure support for FireWire, Apple's legacy charging standard, in its early appearances.

Apple replaced it with the reversible and smaller 8-pin Lightning connector in 2012, another proprietary option that kept the Apple camp from using chargers compatible with Android devices. These connectors also housed small chips to regulate voltage, which let the company collect licensing fees when third-party manufacturers used it for accessories. In 2018, the iPad Pro made the switch to USB-C and the iPhone 15 lineup followed suit in 2023.

Built to handle data transfers, charging and audio output, the clunky FastPort showed up in 2005 on the Sony Ericsson K750i. With double-sided plastic hooks that snapped onto matching holes on the device, the flimsy 12-pin connection was reminiscent of a VGI port's screws. If a hook were to break, the plug would no longer snap into place, creating a huge pain.

Despite its flaws, the FastPort's versatility pointed in the direction the mobile industry would take as it hunted for a universal standard. It was replaced by the Micro-USB and 3.5 mm audio jack combo in 2010. A five-year run might not be much in tech time, but its unconventional shape remains iconic to this day.

The HTC Dream, the first commercially available Android phone, featured the chunky 11-pin ExtUSB port, also known as Extended USB. Recognizable by its slanted corner, it combined the 5-pin Mini-USB port with extra pins that granted it data, power and audio capabilities. Fortunately, that meant regular Mini-USB chargers were compatible with this legacy port.

The connector was exclusive to HTC's early Android devices and Windows Mobile phones before they switched to Micro-USB ports. For a time, phones with this port dropped the 3.5 mm headphone jack long before Apple removed it from the iPhone, forcing users to purchase ExtUSB adapters to connect their headphones.
