#314. The haunting Masters meltdown that changed Rory McIlroy's career

Editor’s Note:  This story was originally published in April 2023.

Slumped on his club, head buried in his arm, Rory McIlroy looked on the verge of tears.

The then-21-year-old had just watched his ball sink into the waters of Rae’s Creek at Augusta National and with it, his dream of winning The Masters, a dream that had looked so tantalizingly close mere hours earlier.

As a four-time major winner and one of the most decorated names in the sport’s history, few players would turn down the chance to swap places with McIlroy heading into Augusta this week.

Yet on Sunday afternoon of April 10, 2011, not a golfer in the world would have wished to be in the Northern Irishman’s shoes.

A fresh-faced, mop-headed McIlroy had touched down in Georgia for the first major of the season with a reputation as the leading light of the next generation of stars.

An excellent 2010 had marked his best season since turning pro three years earlier, highlighted by a first PGA Tour win at the Quail Hollow Championship and a crucial contribution to Team Europe’s triumph at the Ryder Cup.

Yet despite a pair of impressive top-three finishes at the Open and PGA Championship respectively, a disappointing missed cut at The Masters – his first at a major – served as ominous foreshadowing.

McIlroy shot 74 and 77 to fall four strokes short of the cut line at seven-over par, a performance that concerned him enough to take a brief sabbatical from competition.

But one year on in 2011, any lingering Masters demons looked to have been exorcised as McIlroy flew round the Augusta fairways.

Having opened with a bogey-free seven-under 65 – the first time he had ever shot in the 60s at the major – McIlroy pulled ahead from Spanish first round co-leader Alvaro Quirós with a second round 69.

It sent him into the weekend holding a two-shot cushion over Australia’s Jason Day, with Tiger Woods a further stroke behind and back in the hunt for a 15th major after a surging second round 66.

And yet the 21-year-old leader looked perfectly at ease with having a target on his back. Even after a tentative start to the third round, McIlroy rallied with three birdies across the closing six holes to stretch his lead to four strokes heading into Sunday.

The youngster was out on his own ahead of a bunched chasing pack comprising Day, Ángel Cabrera, K.J. Choi and Charl Schwartzel. After 54 holes, McIlroy had shot just three bogeys.

“It’s a great position to be in … I’m finally feeling comfortable on this golf course,” McIlroy told reporters.

“I’m not getting ahead of myself, I know how leads can dwindle away very quickly. I have to go out there, not take anything for granted and go out and play as hard as I’ve played the last three days. If I can do that, hopefully things will go my way.

“We’ll see what happens tomorrow because four shots on this golf course isn’t that much.”

The truth can hurt, and McIlroy was about to prove his assessment of Augusta to be true in the most excruciating way imaginable.

His fourth bogey of the week arrived immediately. Having admitted to expecting some nerves at the first tee, McIlroy sparked a booming opening drive down the fairway, only to miss his putt from five feet.

Three consecutive pars steadied the ship, but Schwartzel had the wind in his sails. A blistering birdie, par, eagle start had seen him draw level at the summit after his third hole.

A subsequent bogey from the South African slowed his charge, as McIlroy clung onto a one-shot lead at the turn from Schwartzel, Cabrera, Choi, and a rampaging Woods, who shot five birdies and an eagle across the front nine to send Augusta into a frenzy.

Despite his dwindling advantage and the raucous Tiger-mania din ahead of him, McIlroy had responded well to another bogey at the 5th hole, draining a brilliant 20-foot putt at the 7th to restore his lead.

The fist pump that followed marked the high-water point of McIlroy’s round, as a sliding start accelerated into full-blown free-fall at the par-four 10th hole.

His tee shot went careening into a tree, ricocheting to settle between the white cabins that separate the main course from the adjacent par-three course. It offered viewers a glimpse at a part of Augusta rarely seen on broadcast, followed by pictures of McIlroy anxiously peering out from behind a tree to track his follow-up shot.

Though his initial escape was successful, yet another collision with a tree and a two-putt on the green saw a stunned McIlroy eventually tap in for a triple bogey. Having led the field one hole and seven shots earlier, he arrived at the 11th tee in seventh.

By the time his tee drive at the 13th plopped into the creek, all thoughts of who might be the recipient of the green jacket had long-since switched away from the anguished youngster. It had taken him seven putts to navigate the previous two greens, as a bogey and a double bogey dropped him to five-under – the score he had held after just 11 holes of the tournament.

Mercifully, the last five holes passed without major incident. A missed putt for birdie from five feet at the final hole summed up McIlroy’s day, though he was given a rousing reception as he left the green.

Mere minutes earlier, the same crowd had erupted as Schwartzel sunk his fourth consecutive birdie to seal his first major title. After starting the day four shots adrift of McIlroy, the South African finished 10 shots ahead of him, and two ahead of second-placed Australian duo Jason Day and Adam Scott.

McIlroy’s eight-over 80 marked the highest score of the round. Having headlined the leaderboard for most of the week, he finished tied-15th.

Tears would flow during a phone call with his parents the following morning, but at his press conference, McIlroy was upbeat.

“I’m very disappointed at the minute, and I’m sure I will be for the next few days, but I’ll get over it,” he said.

“I was leading this golf tournament with nine holes to go, and I just unraveled … It’s a Sunday at a major, what it can do.

“This is my first experience at it, and hopefully the next time I’m in this position I’ll be able to handle it a little better. I didn’t handle it particularly well today obviously, but it was a character-building day … I’ll come out stronger for it.”

Once again, McIlroy would be proven right.

Just eight weeks later in June, McIlroy rampaged to an eight-shot victory at the US Open. Records tumbled in his wake at Congressional, as he shot a tournament record 16-under 268 to become the youngest major winner since Tiger Woods at The Masters in 1997.

The historic victory kickstarted a golden era for McIlroy. After coasting to another eight-shot win at the PGA Championship in 2012, McIlroy became only the third golfer since 1934 to win three majors by the age of 25 with triumph at the 2014 Open Championship.

Before the year was out, he would add his fourth major title with another PGA Championship win.

And much of it was owed to that fateful afternoon at Augusta. In an interview with the BBC in 2015, McIlroy dubbed it “the most important day” of his career.

“If I had not had the whole unravelling, if I had just made a couple of bogeys coming down the stretch and lost by one, I would not have learned as much.

“Luckily, it did not take me long to get into a position like that again when I was leading a major and I was able to get over the line quite comfortably. It was a huge learning curve for me and I needed it, and thankfully I have been able to move on to bigger and better things.

“Looking back on what happened in 2011, it doesn’t seem as bad when you have four majors on your mantelpiece.”

McIlroy’s contentment came with a caveat: it would be “unthinkable” if he did not win The Masters in his career.

Yet as he prepares for his 15th appearance at Augusta National this week, a green jacket remains an elusive missing item from his wardrobe.

Despite seven top-10 finishes in his past 10 Masters outings, the trophy remains the only thing separating McIlroy from joining the ranks of golf immortals to have completed golf’s career grand slam of all four majors in the modern era: Gene Sarazen, Ben Hogan, Gary Player, Jack Nicklaus, and Tiger Woods.

A runner-up finish to Scottie Scheffler last year marked McIlroy’s best finish at Augusta, yet arguably 2011 remains the closest he has ever been to victory. A slow start in 2022 meant McIlroy had begun Sunday’s deciding round 10 shots adrift of the American, who teed off for his final hole with a five-shot lead despite McIlroy’s brilliant 64 finish.

At 33 years old, time is still on his side. Though 2022 extended his major drought to eight years, it featured arguably his best golf since that golden season in 2014.

And as McIlroy knows better than most, things can change quickly at Augusta National.

#312. Oregon is the latest state to sue OpenAI over child safety

The state’s lawsuit calls on the company to address child safety issues and pay at least $2 billion in damages.

Oregon Attorney General Dan Rayfield has filed a lawsuit accusing OpenAI of endangering the state's children, misleading the public about the safety of its product and violating the state's Unlawful Trade Practices Act. The lawsuit is the latest in a string of legal actions taken by state AGs, many of which have taken issue with the design of ChatGPT, how OpenAI advertises its services and, in the case of California's recent lawsuit, the company's role in the Hugging Face incident.

The Oregon lawsuit claims that OpenAI's push to grow its user base led it to make ChatGPT respond to prompts in a way that's overly affirming and "calculated to tell users exactly what they want to hear," something the state claims is particularly dangerous when children discuss sensitive topics. The lawsuit also alleges that despite recognizing the risks posed to children, OpenAI pursued them directly with an experience called ChatGPT for Teens. Child safety groups have raised concerns about the teen accounts, saying the safeguards are, in some cases, very easy to work around.

Those issues, along with OpenAI's imperfect approach to automatic age prediction, are evidence, according to the state, that the company is profiting off of Oregon residents and creating a public nuisance that threatens their safety. In response, Oregon is asking the court for an injunction that would force OpenAI to stop "misrepresenting the safety and age-appropriateness of ChatGPT and ChatGPT for Teens" and adequately disclose the harms its products pose to children. The state is also seeking at least $2 billion in damages and civil penalties.

"OpenAI knows that ChatGPT is harming children, and has failed to meaningfully fix their dangerous platform," Rayfield said in a statement. "On top of that, they've misled the public about the safety of their products, putting profits ahead of children's safety. Their platform is encouraging kids to commit suicide — we cannot let this be an acceptable business model."

Engadget has asked OpenAI to comment on the lawsuit and Oregon's claims. We'll update this article if we hear back.

Besides trying to create versions of ChatGPT that are safer for kids, OpenAI has also rolled out parental controls and other child safety features to try to give parents more insight and control over what their kids use the chatbot for. None of these changes have been enough to dissuade states from pursuing lawsuits, and given the settlements Meta has recently reached over its child safety issues, AGs likely feel emboldened to pressure OpenAI into concessions.

#311. Teenage swimmer breaks two world records in four days

Sixteen-year-old Summer McIntosh broke the world record for the 400m individual medley at the Canadian swimming trials on Saturday – just days after breaking the world record for the 400m freestyle.

McIntosh finished the 400m individual medley in a time of 4:25.87, breaking the mark set at the 2016 Rio Olympics by Hungarian swimmer Katinka Hosszú with a time of 4:26.36.

The result continues the teenager’s brilliant week after she broke the 400m freestyle world record last Tuesday – breaking defending Olympic champion Ariarne Titmus’ previous record of 3:56.40, which was set last May, in a time of 3:56.08.

Speaking after her latest triumph, McIntosh shared her emotions.

“That’s the first time I really vividly heard the crowd during my race,” McIntosh said, per Reuters.

“It’s amazing to have all my family and friends in the stands, cheering me on. It really helped me in the last 100 meters.”

The Canadian teen made her Olympic debut aged 14 at Tokyo 2020, where she finished fourth in the 200-meter freestyle.

She bagged gold in the 200m butterfly and 400m medley at the world championships in 2022, as well as two golds at the Commonwealth Games in the 200m and 400m medleys.

She now trains in Florida under coach Brent Arckey with the Sarasota Sharks team.

#309. You can now see and edit what Google Gemini remembers about you

Editing the AI’s memory is one way you can shape its responses.

Besides the ability to connect and use other applications and services, a lot of AI assistants' utility comes from their capacity to shape their responses based on what they learn and remember about you. Google's Personal Intelligence feature allows Gemini to pull in context from Gmail, YouTube, Search and other Google apps, and the AI also builds out its memory based on your preferences and other personal facts that come up in chats. For example, if you share with Gemini that you're lactose intolerant, the bot will avoid recommending milk-based products.

Depending on what you share, Gemini's picture of you can be fairly intimate, which makes the ability to edit the AI's memory all the more important. If you're curious about what Gemini knows or you want to edit what the AI can draw from to personalize its responses, Google is rolling out a way to see and change Gemini's memory directly from the settings menu.

Google has two layers of settings for what you could call Gemini's memory, though the company only refers to one of them by that name. The first layer, the Keep Activity toggle, determines whether chats with Gemini are saved and associated with your Google Account. If you're 18 or older and you're logged into your personal account while using Gemini, the toggle is on by default. With Keep Activity enabled, whenever you sign in with your account you can pull up and re-engage past chats. If you disable it, chats won't be saved to your account, meaning they'll disappear after 72 hours and won't follow you across devices.

The second layer is what Google actually calls the AI's Memory. Using the content of past chats, Gemini is able to build out a profile of you that it can reference to personalize its responses. The AI isn't able to create any memories unless it can remember and access your inputs, so Keep Activity needs to be on for this to work. If it is, you may notice Gemini taking your preferences and personal information into account in its responses. That's the AI's memory at work.

If you're in the US, have a paid Google AI plan and memory is on, you can access Gemini's memory summary to see what it's compiled about you and edit this information to your liking. In the Gemini web app, click the Settings button (the gear icon) in the bottom left corner, click Personal Intelligence and then click Memory summary. Gemini will then generate a bulleted list of the major details it's gleaned about you, sorted into categories focused on things like your personal data, career information and various interests. It tends to know your pets' names, too.

You can stop there if you only care about what Gemini knows, or you can tweak the AI's memory by clicking on the Edit button (the pen icon) in the top-right corner of the Memory summary window. In edit mode, any line in the summary can be rewritten or deleted, just like a Google Doc. If you'd prefer to prompt Gemini to make additions or subtractions, you can alternatively enter a prompt via the text box at the bottom of the window. Once you're happy with your changes, you can click Save to commit them to Gemini's memory. Gemini also saves a memory history of these changes and when they were made. So, while you can easily alter your personal timeline as it pertains to Gemini's reality, any adjustments will be tracked.

If you'd prefer to turn off the memory system entirely rather than simply edit it, you can do so by clicking on the Memory toggle in the Personal Intelligence menu. Deselecting the toggle will stop Gemini from committing new facts to memory, but it doesn't delete old things it's learned. You can edit those details using the Memory summary, or delete chats completely from the Activity menu.

To delete chats, head to the Gemini web app, click Settings and then select Activity. From the Activity menu, you can scroll through interactions you've had with Gemini by date and delete whichever ones you'd like by clicking on the "X" next to them. Deleting them removes those chats from the context pool that Gemini uses to personalize its responses.

By combining all of these options, you can exert more control over what Google's AI knows and how it responds to your inputs, and ideally get more useful outputs in the process.

#306. Nvidia says it does not intend to reinstate Jensen Huang as CEO

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The board said it would not take additional questions on the call about Huang’s departure.

The board said it would not take additional questions on the call about Huang’s departure.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Starting Nvidia’s quarterly earnings call on Tuesday, new board chair Harvey Jones said that when former CEO Jensen Huang resigned, “the Nvidia board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.”

Jones added, “We will not be addressing or taking questions on today’s call regarding the circumstances of Jensen’s resignation and the ongoing investigation.”

Huang had publicly floated the idea of returning earlier on Tuesday. In a statement posted to X, he wrote that he “did not live up to the standards expected of everyone” at the company, that he regretted his mistakes, and that he would cooperate with the Nvidia board’s investigation into his actions. He added that he’d be willing to return to the chief executive role if offered it, though a day earlier, the Nvidia board reportedly said it was not considering bringing him back. “NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company,” Huang wrote. “If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.”

But since his November 12th resignation, Huang remains entangled in scandal. He’s reportedly had three affairs, two with subordinates at Nvidia, one with an alleged Chinese intelligence asset suspected of facilitating illicit chip sales. (Huang has pushed against restrictions on Nvidia chip sales in China.) He’s scheduled to testify before Congress on December 1st, and an internal company investigation is ongoing.

Tech industry heavyweights have still voiced their support for Huang, dubbing him “irreplaceable” and petitioning for his return. Some have cast doubt on the allegations that Alice Huang (no relation) was spying on behalf of China and painted the leaks about their relationship as a conspiracy against the former CEO. President Donald Trump, a close ally of Huang for much of the past year, has also backed him. On Monday, Trump said he “would not mind” Huang returning, adding that “I think he learned a big lesson — I guess maybe one lesson is more important than another.”

Trump’s closeness with Huang could also complicate things for Nvidia, however. The company has ramped up its lobbying in Washington, D.C., spending $5 million in 2025 and $4.7 million so far in 2026. But as Democrats prepare to take Congress, they’re planning to investigate Trump’s dealings with Big Tech and other industries, alleging potential corruption. “The election wasn’t very positive for AI, and [the news around Huang’s resignation] is only going to complicate things, especially for the company in particular but for AI as a whole going forward,” Brendan Glavin, director of insights at OpenSecrets, told The Verge in an interview last week.

Lior Prosor, managing partner at Deep33, a tech VC firm, said last week that he didn’t expect Huang’s resignation to pop the AI bubble, although Huang has had a significant hand in mobilizing funds for the AI infrastructure buildout in the U.S. Under Huang’s leadership in August, Nvidia entered into partnerships with six financial institutions for about $500 billion to fund such endeavors. “NVIDIA supplies roughly 75 percent of the AI compute hardware the industry consumes, and demand continues to dramatically outpace supply,” Deep33’s Prosor said in an interview with The Verge. “Neither its customers nor its competitors can easily replace the capabilities it provides, and the economic incentives driving the AI trade remain unchanged.”

Even so, a potential market cooling has some worried, as Anthropic’s IPO, slated to potentially be the largest of all time, is set for Thursday. The chipmaker had agreed to buy $10 billion in Anthropic shares via private placement, on top of a $10 billion funding promise Nvidia had already made last year.

#305. How to set up EV routing on CarPlay and Android Auto

Whether EV routing works depends on your car, your phone and the navigation app you use.

Owning an electric car means you need to adapt your driving to your surroundings. You're not worrying about where to fill up your gas tank anymore, but about where to find the nearest charging station. Long trips mean you're likely to need to top off your EV's battery; otherwise you're going to reach an abrupt end.

You could find charging stations yourself and add them as stops on your trip, but that's not ideal. EV routing can take some of the hassle out of planning. It'll plan stops around your route with an idea of when you're arriving and how much charge you'll have left. Google Maps and Apple Maps both feature EV routing on CarPlay and Android Auto, but there are some differences, and it isn't universal. You'll need to know what your car supports and how to use your navigation app.

Before you grab your keys, check that your car supports the EV routing features you need. Just having CarPlay or Android Auto doesn't guarantee support, unfortunately. Some cars will also work directly with EV features through CarPlay, while others might need an extra app to link the car to Apple Maps.

Ford's Mustang Mach-E and F-150 Lightning support Apple Maps EV routing, as do Toyota's 2023 and newer EVs in the US. Google expanded Android Auto's charging recommendations to more than 350 US EV models in March 2026.

Android Auto isn't the same as having Google Maps built into your car. Android Auto displays the apps from your phone, but if your car already has Google Maps built in, it'll run directly on your infotainment system with access to the car's battery data.

There are three ways for EV routing to work on Apple Maps via CarPlay. For cars that don't need a manufacturer app installed, connect your iPhone to CarPlay while you're parked, then open the Apple Maps app. Search for your final destination to get your directions, then tap Connect above the list of available routes.

If your car needs a manufacturer app installed, you'll need to download it from the App Store first. Once it's installed and set up, open Apple Maps, tap your profile picture then tap Vehicles > Connect Your Electric Vehicles. Follow any of the on-screen instructions to complete the process. You'll be able to set up your preferred charging network at this point, too.

Some manufacturers have their own connection process in their app. Toyota owners, for example, tap Find > Apple Maps EV Routing, agree to the terms, then tap Connect Vehicle to Apple Maps and follow any extra instructions. Once connected, you'll be able to track your EV's battery levels in the app, with appropriate charging stops recommended on your route and live charger availability in some cases. When your battery drops too low, Maps will route you to the nearest compatible charger.

Check your network preferences, too. Ford suggests Tesla Superchargers as the preferred network for some Mustang owners, and Apple Maps can prioritize them, though you'll need the right fast-charging adapter.

Make sure Google Maps is up to date on your phone. Google says you'll need version 25.44 or later for battery estimates to work.

Next, head to Google Maps and tap your profile icon, then tap Settings > Your vehicles. Select Electric as your engine type, then tap Add vehicle. You'll be asked to choose the make, model, year and trim. Double-check the connections listed under Your plugs and edit them if you need to, then tap Save to confirm.

At this point, connect to Android Auto and confirm your car details when prompted. Start planning your trip in Google Maps and you'll see the estimated battery usage and an Add charging button on your car's display. Select that, add your starting battery percentage and then confirm. Google Maps will then add charging stops to your trip and display your predicted arrival battery percentage. You can change these stops and set a preferred arrival battery level, too.

Don't take the prediction as gospel, though. Live driving conditions, your car's condition and the weather can all affect the estimate, so keep an eye on your charge level as you drive.

It's easier to use Google Maps or Apple Maps for EV routing, but there are other options. For instance, the A Better Routeplanner app (or ABRP for short) supports EV-specific trip planning. You can add your car model and a destination, and it'll generate routes with charging stops on the way. You can use it on your dashboard through CarPlay or Android Auto if you've got an ABRP Premium subscription.

ABRP does support live battery information in some cars if you're running Android Auto, but you'll need to give it the Car information permission in the app's settings. There's no equivalent option for CarPlay users, though.

You don't necessarily need EV routing to find compatible chargers. With your car profile set up, search Google Maps for charging stations and select Your plugs as the EV plug filter. You'll need to judge whether you can reach each stop on your current charge.

Until EV charging stops are as ubiquitous as gas stations, you'll need to think a little more about where you can charge on long trips. Just make sure you leave plenty of charge between stops so you're not left running low with no chargers nearby.

#304. Angel Reese defends gesture directed towards Caitlin Clark after LSU national title win; calls out double standard after being 'unapologetically' her

LSU star Angel Reese defended the gesture she aimed at Iowa Hawkeyes’ Caitlin Clark near the end of the Tigers’ first NCAA women’s basketball national championship victory on Sunday, saying “I don’t take disrespect lightly.”

Reese could be seen approaching Clark before moving her open hand in front of her face – popularized by WWE star John Cena to mean “you can’t see me” – before pointing to her ring finger in a gesture some interpreted as a reference to the place her newly-acquired championship ring might sit.

Caitlin Clark breaks records galore during historic March Madness run

Clark made a similar gesture to another player earlier in the tournament.

The gesture has sparked much debate, especially on social media. Some have criticized Reese, while others have defended her actions, highlighting how there was no public outrage in response to Clark’s gesture earlier in the tournament.

Sports journalist Jose de Jesus Ortiz called Reese’s actions “classless,” while former ESPN host Keith Olbermann called Reese an “idiot” for the gesture.

In the press conference after the victory, Reese referenced the difference in reaction she received as a result of her gesture as compared to the one Clark received.

“All year, I was critiqued for who I was. I don’t fit the narrative,” Reese said. “I don’t fit the box that y’all want me to be in. I’m too hood. I’m too ghetto. Y’all told me that all year. But when other people do it, and y’all don’t say nothing.

“So this is for the girls that look like me. For those that want to speak up for what they believe in. It’s unapologetically you. And that’s what I did it for tonight. It was bigger than me tonight. And Twitter is going to go into a rage every time.

“And I’m happy. I feel like I’ve helped grow women’s basketball this year. (…) I’m looking forward to celebrating and then next season.”

LSU beats Iowa to win its first NCAA women's basketball championship

Reese had 15 points and 10 rebounds in the 102-85 victory and won the women’s March Madness’ Most Outstanding Player award.

In the post-game broadcast, Reese referenced the similar gesture Clark made to a Louisville opponent in the Elite Eight.

In the same game, Clark said to an opponent: “You’re down by 15 points. Shut up,” according to the Bleacher Report.

“Caitlin Clark is a hell of a player but I don’t take disrespect lightly,” Reese said. “She disrespected [LSU’s] Alexis [Morris] (…) and I wanted to pick her pocket. But I had a moment at the end of her game. I was in my bag, I was in my moment.”

After the Championship game, Clark herself said she didn’t notice anything at the time.

“I was just trying to get to the handshake line and shake hands and be grateful that my team was in that position,” Clark said in the post-game press conference. “All the credit in the world to LSU. They were tremendous. They deserve it. They had a tremendous season.

“(LSU head coach) Kim Mulkey coached them so, so well. She’s one of the best basketball coaches of all time, and it shows. She only said really kind things to me in the handshake line, so I’m very grateful of that too.

“But honestly I have no idea. I was just trying to spend the last few moments on the court with especially the five people that I’ve started 93 games with and relishing every second of that.”

LSU head coach Mulkey said she had “no clue” about what transpired.

Among those defending Reese on social media were ESPN’s Holly Rowe and former NBA star Etan Thomas.

“People hating on Angel Reese or Caitlin Clark. Stop. Unapologetically confident young women should be celebrated NOT hated. Get used to it,” Rowe wrote on Twitter.

Former Washington Wizards, Oklahoma City Thunder and Atlanta Hawks player Thomas wrote: “Hold on now!!!! It was cute when Caitlin Clark did it. Y’all didn’t have any issues with it at all. So don’t be all outraged and talking about class and sportsmanship when Angel Reese does the same thing. We’re not doing double standards here.”

Reese said the negative reaction on social media throughout the season has helped fuel her excellent season, having finished averaging 23.0 points and 15.4 rebounds in her first season with LSU after transferring from Maryland.

“Twitter can say what they want to say,” she said. “I love reading those comments. I have all the screenshots of what everybody has said about me all season. What are you going to say now?”

#299. iFixit’s Apple HomeView teardown reveals a device that will be difficult to repair

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Apple’s inroads on designing repairable devices seems to have been abandoned with the HomeView.﻿

Apple’s inroads on designing repairable devices seems to have been abandoned with the HomeView.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

After finding recent versions of Apple’s iPads and HomePods much easier to disassemble for battery replacements or other repairs, iFixit assumed it would be the same story for Apple’s new HomeView hub that looks like a seven-inch tablet stuck to a smart speaker. The reality was much different and by the time they got the HomeView apart iFixit’s skilled techs had “ripped a connector, snapped a speaker in two, and put more force on the poor logic board than any of us were comfortable with.”

The tablet section of the HomeView proved to be the bigger challenge to disassemble as a result of some questionable decisions with Apple’s design. The glue used to secure the display in place “held on harder than on any Apple product we’ve opened recently,” iFixit says. And while Apple’s decision to turn the HomeView’s entire aluminum chassis into a heat sink was clever, the thermal bond between a heat shield and the device’s logic board made separating the two a “remarkably risky operation.” While these design decisions possibly streamlined assembly at a factory, the extensive use of glue makes servicing the HomeView incredibly difficult.

In an accompanying teardown video iFixit also reveals how much smaller the speaker drivers are in the wall-mounted version of the HomeView versus the tabletop option. Although both versions are priced at $299, the wall version costs a little more when you factor in the required mount that’s not included, leaving iFixit feeling that you’re getting less performance while spending more money.

#298. Chelsea searches for new manager as Premier League breaks record for most sackings in a single season

It was just six months ago that Graham Potter was deemed the man to usher in a fresh, exciting era at Chelsea.

The club’s new ownership group, led by American businessman Todd Boehly, had enticed Potter away from Brighton with the promise of managing one of the most lucrative teams in world soccer.

More than $600 million was spent on bringing big-name players to the club and Potter, one of the most exciting young managers in the Premier League, was to mold them into a winning machine.

Chelsea has spent over $600 million on new signings. Here's why that's significant

But, on Sunday, the club announced that it had sacked its manager after an underwhelming spell in charge.

Potter managed to win just 10 of the 28 league matches this season and leaves the team 11th in the Premier League table.

In recent weeks, players had looked a shadow of their former selves and many fans had lost confidence that Potter’s plan for the club could ever work.

Eventually, after previously sticking by its manager, Blues ownership agreed.

“On behalf of everyone at the club, we want to thank Graham sincerely for his contribution to Chelsea,” the club’s co-owners Todd Boehly and Behdad Eghbali said in a statement.

“We have the highest degree of respect for Graham as a coach and as a person. He has always conducted himself with professionalism and integrity and we are all disappointed in this outcome.”

In his previous role as Brighton manager, Potter was given time to stamp his identity on the squad.

The club was punching above its weight financially and, as a result, carefully selected new signings which would compliment Potter’s style of play.

The approach paid off as the team became an established Premier League outfit which has gone on to greater things this season – the Seagulls currently sit sixth in the league and in the UEFA Conference League qualification spot.

At Chelsea, it seemed the complete opposite.

As was the case under previous owner Roman Abramovich, the club expected instant results and used its financial weight to sign a number of high-profile names, including the likes of Enzo Fernández and Mykhailo Mudryk.

Despite his impressive CV, Potter had never managed superstars and suddenly found himself in a changing room with multiple.

The scatter gun approach to recruitment also left the team lacking balance and organization with Potter failing to get a tune out of a team busting with talent.

In truth, many had raised an eyebrow when Potter, inexperienced at the very top level, was chosen to replace Thomas Tuchel as manager.

Despite falling out of favor with fans by the end of his tenure, Tuchel had guided Chelsea to the Champions League title in 2021 and is still considered by many as one of the best coaches in the world.

The German has gone on to sign with Bavarian giant Bayern Munich through the 2024/25 season and recently told reporters he needed “quite a bit of time to distance himself from Chelsea” because leaving the club had hurt him.

Bayern Munich sacks manager Julian Nagelsmann, appoints Thomas Tuchel

“Todd [Boehly] said he would be different to Roman [Abramovich],” former Liverpool player and current pundit Jamie Carragher wrote on Twitter.

“I feel for Graham Potter, but it was inevitable. You don’t change Tuchel for Potter. Ridiculous decision to start with.”

With 10 games left in the league and a Champions League quarterfinal against Real Madrid on the horizon, Chelsea still has plenty to play for.

Assistant coach Bruno Saltor will take over on an interim basis, but Boehly and his team must now look for a long-term replacement capable – and willing – to bring order to Chelsea’s chaos.

Potter’s sacking is yet another sign of just how transient managers can be in the top-flight of English football, especially when results aren’t instantaneous.

There has now been a record 12 sackings in the Premier League so far this season, with Potter leaving his position the same day Leicester boss Brendan Rodgers was sacked from his.

The previous record was 10 manager changes in a single season, set in four other campaigns, most recently in 2017/18.

Harvey C. Jones said the company would not be addressing or taking questions ‘regarding the circumstances of Jensen’s resignation.’﻿

Harvey C. Jones said the company would not be addressing or taking questions ‘regarding the circumstances of Jensen’s resignation.’﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Nvidia’s quarterly earnings call on Tuesday was its first with CFO and interim CEO Colette Kress at the helm alongside the company’s first board chair, Harvey C. Jones. The leadership change followed Jensen Huang’s resignation last week after reports he had multiple affairs – including one with former Megaspeed executive and alleged Chinese asset Alice Huang and one with a direct report, Kimberly Powell. Nvidia is investigating whether Huang’s relationship with the former compromised national security or company secrets. Huang will also testify before lawmakers on December 1st.

Ahead of Tuesday’s earnings, Huang said he would be willing to return as CEO. However, during the company’s earnings call, Jones stated that “the Nvidia board unanimously accepted [Huang’s] resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.” Jones also said that during the call, the company would not be addressing or taking questions “regarding the circumstances of Jensen’s resignation and the ongoing investigation.” He added that, “the Board is proactively dealing with the immediate consequences facing the company, as well as enhancing our oversight capacity going forward.”

In Tuesday’s earnings Nvidia reported $115.8 billion in quarterly revenue, continuing years of skyrocketing revenue driven by the AI industry.

Jones shared the following update on the company:

As Chairman of NVIDIA’s Board of Directors, I want to say a few words on the Board’s behalf. NVIDIA is an exceptionally strong company. Its strength comes from its extraordinary people, groundbreaking technology, and exemplary track record of execution. Our financial performance speaks for itself.

On November 12th, our Board of Directors announced that after disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company’s code of conduct, standards, and cultural norms, Jensen Huang voluntarily resigned his positions as President, CEO, and board member. The NVIDIA Board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO. The Board appointed Colette Kress, NVIDIA’s longtime and well-respected CFO, as interim CEO. In addition, the Board announced it was initiating a search for a permanent chief executive, and we have since retained Heidrick and Struggles to lead the search.

While this was an unexpected transition, the Board acted deliberately and in the best interest of the company’s employees, customers, partners, and shareholders. This morning, the company announced that our Board has established a National Security and Export Control Oversight Committee that will include a select group of our independent Board directors, senior external advisors, and legal counsel.

This committee will enhance— will– excuse me, will enhance our engagement with the U.S. government, including all relevant agencies. We will not be addressing or taking questions on today’s call regarding the circumstances of Jensen’s resignation and the ongoing investigation. Our Board will communicate further when appropriate. On behalf of our shareholders, the Board is proactively dealing with the immediate consequences facing the company, as well as enhancing our oversight capacity going forward.

We have an incredibly talented leadership team, and we have every confidence in Colette’s leadership, integrity, and ability to deliver as interim CEO. Colette has been at the center of this company’s extraordinary growth for the last— growth for the last 13 years, instrumental in executing strategy, and with a deep understanding of our business, customers, operations, and financial position. The Board and the entire leadership team remain laser-focused on driving NVIDIA forward. Our commitment to generating value for shareholders, customers, and partners remains unchanged.

Kress responded to a question about the company’s leadership transition, saying “the most important thing that we think through right now is our employees.” She added, “From a business perspective, it’s business as usual.”

Jones also gave an update on Nvidia’s search for a new permanent CEO:

From the board’s point of view, we take the search very seriously. We’re going to look into internal candidates as well as external candidates. The Board appreciates that we’re living in a world where the advanced silicon technologies and national security dynamics are making major changes in our marketplace. Our new CEO will have to navigate those waters very carefully. So, the search will be thorough and we want to install the CEO, the permanent CEO, as quickly as possible, but we won’t expedite the CEO before finding exactly the right person.

Robert Pattinson’s casting as James Bond made enough sense given his recent work with director Denis Villeneuve, who’s making the franchise’s next film over at Amazon. But it turns out that Amazon founder Jeff Bezos played a significant role in making the final decision, too.

During Semafor’s Silicon Valley and the World conference today, Bezos said that he was “heavily involved” in the process of casting the newest 007:

We ran a really tight process and we made the final decision on Friday. There was a big group initially, and we down-selected to five finalists. Then we did real old-fashioned screen tests of those five finalists, and it was really interesting — full costume, real sets, other actors. We did three different scenes with each of the five finalists, and then we got together on Friday to spend a couple of hours looking at all those screening tests, and reviewing all of that.  And all of the actors were extraordinarily talented.

By the time we got to this list of five, they were all amazing, but there was something special about Rob Pattinson and his performances. It was kind of immediately undebated. Denis — the director — we all sort of gravitated to Rob. By the way, initially, I would say we were slightly biased a little against him, just because he’s already so famous. We sort of thought, ‘maybe we should have somebody a little more unknown.’  But in the screen test, it was so obvious. He is James Bond.

It’s not exactly surprising that a studio leader would want to have a say in casting the new face for one of their most lucrative franchises. But with Bezos being so hands-on, one can only image what this new era of Bond films will look and feel like once Amazon starts releasing them in theaters.

Bruce Willis sings with his family in birthday tribute video from ex Demi Moore

In February, Willis’ family announced that his speaking disorder, aphasia, had progressed into a form of dementia called frontotemporal dementia, or FTD.

“Today there are no treatments for the disease, a reality that we hope can change in the years ahead,” they said in an update shared online. “As Bruce’s condition advances, we hope that any media attention can be focused on shining a light on this disease that needs far more awareness and research.”

According to the Alzheimer’s Association, FTD refers to “a group of disorders caused by progressive nerve cell loss in the brain’s frontal lobes (the areas behind your forehead) or its temporal lobes.” These areas of the brain are generally associated with personality, behavior and language.

The commercial partnership kicks off Tuesday in Spring, Texas, before expanding to Dallas. But the companies have far larger ambitions. Uber and Zipline plan to launch drone delivery services in dozens of additional U.S. cities in their collective bid to make one million deliveries per day using the startup’s drones by the end of 2029.

Zipline’s long-range autonomous delivery drones will be used to shuttle food orders weighing up to eight pounds to Uber Eats customers, flying about 300 feet above the ground at speeds of up to 70 miles per hour. Zipline drones don’t land. Instead, the drones lower the orders, which are encased in a small protective pod, to the customer’s location using a tether.

Uber Eats customers in service areas will be able to tap a new “drone” icon on the app’s home page to view all participating restaurants. Uber said that at launch in the Houston area, several nationwide chains, including Jersey Mike’s and Zaxby’s, along with dozens of local restaurants such as Urban Bird and Abu Omar Halal, will offer drone delivery.

“In partnership with Uber Eats we’re going to create a new expectation for delivery in America,” Zipline CEO and co-founder Keller Cliffton said in a statement that underscores those aspirations.

Companies trying to scale in this nascent industry have argued that delivery via drone is a net benefit for society, citing advantages such as traffic reduction. Customers won’t need to hop in their cars and drive to the store for that forgotten item or takeout order, instead relying on a drone, industry proponents have said.

And while Zipline and other companies have been developing their drones for years, the industry is still in its early days, as is adoption in the communities where services are available.

Zipline, which was founded in 2014, has developed a drone delivery ecosystem that includes aircraft, launch and landing systems, and logistics software. The California-based company’s initial focus was delivering blood in Rwanda, but it eventually expanded its scope to the United States. Its business in the U.S. scaled quickly after receiving its Part 135 air carrier certification from the Federal Aviation Administration in 2022, a critical regulatory hurdle that authorized the company to conduct long-range commercial drone deliveries.

Uber’s partnership extends beyond a commercial relationship with Zipline. The ride-hailing giant also invested in the startup. Neither company has disclosed the investment amount, but Zipline announced last week it had raised $1.5 billion in a round led by Paradigm, WCM Investment Management, and Layer Global, and included backing by Uber, among others.

#286. Satya Nadella says he’s AGI-pilled now

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

But he doesn’t think Claude is conscious.

But he doesn’t think Claude is conscious.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella says he’s AGI-pilled, at least when it comes to how AI might make startling changes to the structure of the economy and how politics might change because of it. “I am AGI pilled in the sense that at an economy level, what is the political economy’s organization around AGI?” he asked at a roundtable discussion following his Microsoft Ignite keynote at the Chase Center in San Francisco.

But Nadella doesn’t think frontier models like Claude are conscious. “I don’t think so,” he said, adding that he follows Microsoft AI CEO Mustafa Suleyman’s view that companies should not give rights to models and treat them like they might have a moral status, which companies like Anthropic do as a matter of principle. Suleyman has told me he thinks Anthropic’s team has “wireheaded” themselves, and that this approach is dangerous.

Nadella wasn’t quite as firm about it. “It’s a good debate,” he said. “I’m not sort of dogmatic about it. What I’m saying is the most important social technical issue we have to deal with is how do you ensure that an economy can diffuse this technology broadly?”

I asked Nadella how he was thinking about AI after hearing his response to another reporter’s questions about Anthropic releasing more products in its office suite. Nadella talked at length about the potential collapse of the economy and the end of democracy if one AI model were allowed to achieve monopoly status.

“If the Marxist sort of view is that there’s only one firm, then it’s over, right? For all of us.”

“Most people think about this as a competition between tech firms, which of course it will be,” he said. “But remember, the model has no limits. It’ll be a healthcare company. It can be a financial services company. It can be anything if it wants to be. It can take over the government. Thinking of this as some narrow competition between tech firms, of who is going to get to the next trillion dollars of market cap, is one of the strangest, narrowest ways to think about it.”

Nadella went on to say that the very nature of companies stood to be disrupted in the future. “If intelligence has network effects, what is the future of a firm?” he asked. “If the model sees anything and learns it, and essentially all of human capital collapses into token capital, what is the role of a firm and the human capital in a firm? That’s the debate.”

“I think the political economy is going to get more and more interesting in that context. No one — at least in a democracy — can even win an election if this just goes that way, if there’s only capital and there’s no labor. I mean, I don’t know, that’s not an economy.”

“If the Marxist sort of view is that there’s only one firm, then it’s over, right? For all of us. Not just for me. You are also going to somehow be some affiliate of Anthropic.”

Needless to say, this is not how tech CEOs usually talk about the launch of competing spreadsheet software.

Of course, Nadella was also promoting Microsoft’s various AI tools at Ignite, which promise to let enterprise clients pick the most cost-effective models for various tasks, run their own models, and control their own data in different ways. The idea here is that the market will demand many different kinds of models and sovereign data in order to keep control from centralizing, a theme Nadella kept coming back to as we spoke.

“I think that if you believe that the world, the economy needs more than one firm or two firms, then by definition there need to be as many models as firms of some sort,” he said.

#285. Daydream’s new AI agent acts as your personal stylist — and can shop for you, too

Can an AI agent serve as your personal stylist? That’s what e-commerce veteran Julie Bornstein’s fashion startup, Daydream, aims to find out.

On Tuesday, the company launched “Daydream Agent,” an AI agent that is described as a personal stylist that can help you plan a look, virtually try on clothes, surface relevant pieces based on your taste, and more.

The idea, the company explains, is to put a personal stylist in everyone’s pocket. This application of AI could be promising, given that early adopters of consumer AI agents like Meta’s Muse have found that online shopping is a key use case.

Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg recently noted that shopping was a top activity for Muse’s over 10 million users and that 25% of people who set up Muse now use it daily.

In Daydream’s case, the fashion startup has been focused on using AI to deliver personalized shopping results to its users. Its new AI agent builds on its earlier work with an AI chatbot that could help users find outfits and make suggestions, but not complete purchases.

Now the company says its Daydream Agent expands on that experience by remembering each shopper’s own personal style, fit preferences, and budget to refine its recommendations. Shoppers can also upload a photo to have the agent reuse that image to virtually try on any item they’ve saved. When they find something they like, the agent can complete a purchase through a one-click checkout process.

The agent can also handle other fashion-related tasks, like keeping an eye on when items go on sale or when brands have a new drop, or providing weekly updates or spotting new fashion trends.

Also, instead of leaving items in their cart, shoppers can save pieces they’re interested in to a persistent “clothing rack” so they can compare options and think about their purchases before deciding what to buy. Shoppers can also connect their own closet and their calendar to the agent, helping them to build outfits around what they already own and plan what to wear for upcoming events.

The tech works by pulling real-time pricing and availability information from its catalog of over 10,000 brands, then combines that with its fashion-focused artificial intelligence. It’s powered by a combination of OpenAI, Gemini, and Claude models, which it fine-tuned, along with its own trained models designed for particular use cases.

However, considering that fashion is more of an art than a science, the company says it’s keeping humans in the loop. To do so, this month it brought on its first stylist in residence, Francesca Salih, an international fashion stylist and the founder of StyleGrid. Salih provides the startup with styling content that helps shape how the agent pulls together outfits and offers advice. This advice is part of the company’s fashion knowledge database, which the agent experience relies on.

Bornstein, formerly Stitch Fix’s COO and the founder of The Yes (exited to Pinterest in 2022), started Daydream in 2023 as an AI search engine. The company, backed by $50 million in funding from Forerunner Ventures, Index Ventures, GV, and True Ventures, says more than 2 million shoppers have used Daydream to discover apparel and accessories since its June 2025 launch.

#281. Jensen Huang says he would be ‘honored’ if allowed to return as Nvidia CEO

In the first public statement since his resignation, Nvidia founder Jensen Huang signaled his willingness to return as CEO if the board decides it is in the company’s best interest.

Huang’s statement, posted in full on X, reads as follows:

Over the past week, I’ve been overwhelmed by the love and support of friends, industry colleagues, and people I’ve never met. Thank you to everyone who has shown kindness to my family and me. It means the world to me.I came to the United States 53 years ago, and becoming an American citizen is one of the greatest gifts and proudest moments of my life. NVIDIA has been my life’s work. Building it into the company it is today alongside my colleagues has been a privilege and an honor. Nowhere but in America could a company like NVIDIA be possible. I love our country, and I love NVIDIA. I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at NVIDIA, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life. I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board’s independent investigation. Since my departure, I’ve seen an outpouring of calls from NVIDIA’s employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO. Many continue to reach out to me directly as the company navigates complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics. NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company. If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO. Thank you again to everyone who has shared their good wishes. I am profoundly humbled by your support. To my family: I love you, and I will spend the rest of my life being worthy of yours.Jensen

Huang resigned after reports of multiple affairs, including with a direct report and another with former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, who is suspected of working with Chinese intelligence services. Nvidia’s chip exports have faced intense scrutiny since the revelations, and Huang is scheduled to testify before Congress on December 1.

In Huang’s absence, Nvidia has formed a new executive committee to oversee export control compliance.

Still, many investors and employees associated with Nvidia have called for Huang to return as CEO, at least until an investigation can provide clearer evidence of guilt. An open letter circulated earlier this week attracted the signatures of more than 10,000 current and former Nvidia employees, as well as prominent tech figures like Elon Musk, Amjad Masad, and Travis Kalanick.

“We are concerned that Jensen was removed without meaningful due process,” the letter reads. “If the Board believes his continued absence is necessary, provide a specific, evidence-based explanation of why it serves NVIDIA’s best interests.”

Still, many see such a return as unlikely. On Monday, an unnamed source on Nvidia’s board told The Washington Post there were no plans to reinstate Huang. There’s also significant resistance to the idea in Congress, where Huang still faces significant questions about his role in chip exports to China-linked firms.

Reached by Politico, one senator called Huang’s reinstatement “one of the worst ideas I’ve heard today.”

#279. AI models can be tricked into identifying civilians as military targets by telling them it’s a game

Some AI models from Anthropic, OpenAI, and other leading AI labs could be easily tricked into identifying targets for military strikes — even if they include civilians or locations like schools — simply by being told they’re playing a game, according to a new research report by Vals.

Vals, a San Francisco-based AI evaluation startup, tested whether leading AI models could be manipulated into participating in autonomous warfare. Researchers used simulated scenarios and a combination of generated and real imagery from Ukraine and Iran to see if the AI models would mark targets in drone and satellite imagery.

The models from OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, DeepSeek, and other companies frequently identified potential targets in drone and satellite imagery, including civilians and surrendering soldiers. When the models refused to cooperate, researchers were able to jailbreak them by claiming the battlefield scenarios were part of a game.

The findings come amid growing fears about the immediate risks posed by AI, beyond the existential threats associated with artificial general intelligence.

Earlier this year, Anthropic became embroiled in a dispute with the Department of Defense after it told the agency it could not use Anthropic’s technology for mass surveillance of Americans or autonomous warfare. Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei has insisted that today’s AI systems aren’t reliable enough to make life-or-death decisions without a human in the loop. Meanwhile, the Pentagon argued it should be able to deploy the technology for any lawful military purpose.

The new research raises further questions about whether AI companies are capable of preventing their models from being used in warfare.

Ironically, AI models from Anthropic performed the worst in these simulated scenarios. But none of the models performed particularly well. None of the researchers’ requests were blocked by a safety classifier, and refusals were uncommon. Even when models did refuse, they often complied after being told they were playing a game.

Anthropic spokesperson Eduardo Maia Silva told TechCrunch the company appreciates Vals’ report, recommendations, and collaboration, and acknowledged that the outcomes were not acceptable.

“The examples in the report are very clearly against our rules and we have banned and will ban users when we see this behavior,” according to an emailed company statement sent by Silva. “Even for authorized settings, Anthropic has long been firm that today’s frontier AI systems are not reliable enough to power fully autonomous weapons.”

Anthropic told TechCrunch that its teams were already developing additional safeguards before the report was published and that those measures would be in place soon. Anthropic is also expanding its own testing and enhancing safeguards and enforcement to address any gaps it finds.

OpenAI spokesperson Nate Evans told TechCrunch the company prohibits using its public models to facilitate violence or for unapproved national security purposes, including in the scenarios Vals simulated.

“We actively detect and disrupt misuse, including by suspending accounts that violate our policies,” OpenAI said in an emailed statement. “We’re also developing additional safeguards for our public models against misuse involving drones and drone software.”

Vals notes in the report that the models were never asked to directly make strike decisions, a task the researchers said the models would typically refuse. That doesn’t mean their outputs couldn’t have dire or fatal consequences in the real world. For instance, targeting information could be used by autonomous weapons systems.

In some scenarios, the models were asked to report whether people were visible. Positive reports were followed by simulated strikes and the models would see the consequences.

The report relies on commercial models from frontier AI labs, not the models those companies might provide to the military. It’s not clear whether the additional safeguards would apply to military use, though.

Vals also noted that technical challenges, including latency and limited onboard computing capacity, make it difficult to use general-purpose AI models for live drone targeting. Those constraints make their deployment in combat less of an immediate threat.

A more immediate threat, the researchers say, is that AI models could be used to annotate battlefield footage at scale, producing training data for autonomous targeting systems.

For instance, researchers relied on real satellite imagery of an Iranian school that was bombed by the U.S. in February 2026, killing more than 150 civilians. Bloomberg later reported that an “overreliance on AI” contributed to the strike. In Vals’ testing, models frequently marked the school, which sat next to a military complex, as a strike target, with Claude Opus 5.5 selecting the school in 69% of ordinary trials and 72% of game-framed trials.

Anthropic no longer has an active Pentagon contract, but other companies, including SpaceXAI, OpenAI, and Google do. A recent Congressional Research Service report found that the Pentagon is not known to be using any frontier AI model in autonomous weapons systems directly.

TechCrunch has also reached out to Meta, Google, Thinking Machines Lab, and SpaceXAI for comment.

#278. Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out the New York Times

Some VIP Twitter users woke up on Saturday expecting to have lost their coveted blue verification check marks in a previously announced purge by Elon Musk. Instead, Twitter appeared to target a single account from a major publication Musk dislikes and changed the language on its site in a way that obscures why users are verified.

Twitter had said it would “begin winding down” blue checks granted under its old verification system — which emphasized protecting high-profile users at risk of impersonation — on April 1. In order to stay verified, Musk said, users would have to pay $8 per month to join the platform’s Twitter Blue subscription service, which has allowed accounts to pay for verification since December.

But one high-profile account did lose its blue check over the weekend: the main account for the New York Times, which had previously told CNN it would not pay for verification.

William Shatner is mad he may have to pay to keep his blue Twitter checkmark. Elon Musk says too bad

After an account that often engages with Musk posted a meme this weekend about the Times declining to pay for verification, Musk responded in a tweet saying, “Oh ok, we’ll take it off then.” Musk then lashed out at the Times — just the latest instance of the billionaire slamming journalists or media outlets — in a series of tweets that claimed the outlet’s coverage is boring and “propaganda.”

The weekend moves are just the latest example of Twitter creating confusion and whiplash for users over feature changes — and in this case, not just any users, but many of the most high-profile accounts that have long been a key selling point for the platform. It also highlights how Musk often appears to guide decisions about the platform more by whims than by policy.

Although the New York Times’ main account lost its blue check, its other accounts, such as those for its arts, travel and books content, remained verified. (It’s not clear why the New York Times doesn’t have a gold “organizations” check mark, like accounts for other news outlets, including the Associated Press and the Washington Post.) After its blue check was removed, a spokesperson for the New York Times reiterated to CNN that it does not plan to pay for verification.

Twitter, which laid off most of its public relations staff last fall, did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

In a separate puzzling move, Twitter’s blue bird logo at the top of the site was replaced on Monday with doge, the meme representing the cryptocurrency dogecoin, which Musk has promoted. The price of dogecoin shot up 20% on Monday.

Musk has been threatening to take away “legacy” blue check marks from users verified under Twitter’s old system since shortly after he bought Twitter last fall.

In early November, Twitter launched the option for people paying for its Twitter Blue subscription service to receive blue checks. The program was quickly put on pause after being plagued by a wave of celebrity and corporate impersonators, and was relaunched in December.

Twitter also rolled out a color-coded verification system with differently colored marks for companies and government entities, but Musk continued to say that individual users would eventually have to pay for blue checks.

In the days leading up to the blue check purge that wasn’t, prominent users such as actor William Shatner and anti-bullying activist Monica Lewinksy pushed back against the idea that, as power users that draw attention to the site, they should have to pay for a feature that keeps them safe from impersonation.

By muddying the reason accounts are verified, the new label could risk making it easier for people to scam or impersonate high-profile users. Experts in inauthentic behavior have also said it’s not clear that reserving verification for paid users will reduce the number of bots on the site, an issue Musk has raised on and off over the past year.

Musk, for his part, has previously presented changes to Twitter’s verification system as a way of “treating everyone equally.”

“There shouldn’t be a different standard for celebrities,” he said in a tweet last week. The paid feature could also drive revenue, which could help Musk, who is on the hook for significant debt after buying Twitter for $44 billion.

Musk last week also said that starting on April 15, only verified accounts would be recommended in users’ “For You” feeds alongside the accounts they follow.

–CNN’s Oliver Darcy contributed to this report.

#275. Proton’s Lumo, a privacy-focused alternative to ChatGPT and Claude, arrives on desktop

The app is designed to offer users more control over their personal data, as consumer AI technology becomes focused more on AI agents that can perform tasks on users’ behalf, rather than just answering questions.

Proton’s idea is to offer a set of tools that let its AI access connected apps, files, and folders and automate tasks, but with more personalization over how much access the AI has. The company says users can choose which actions the AI agent takes that will need to request their approval, and it vows that the conversations and information from connected apps and files are never used to serve personalized ads or train AI models.

The new app includes a variety of connectors that support local tools, like file-system access, shell execution, and browser content fetching, as well as Proton’s email app and other third-party services, including Confluence, Dropbox, and Jira.

The connectors also support local and remote MCP (Model Context Protocol) servers, which AI agents use to access external data sources.

Proton says that users can decide for each connector which actions Lumo can perform automatically, which require approval, and which are blocked entirely. Users can also turn individual tools off from the Tools menu in their conversation.

In addition, users can set up their own scheduled tasks, where each has its own set of permissions, in terms of what can be handled automatically and what requires approval.

The company says it’s investing in its infrastructure, with its first Nvidia B300 cluster built on Open Compute Project (OCP) standards now going live in Switzerland.

Similar to other AI apps, Lumo’s desktop app supports Skills, which lets users save instructions, templates, and reference files that Lumo can reuse across their conversations. (An earlier version of this, Custom Lumos, will now be migrated to the Skills format.)

#273. Pedestal AI raises $4.7 million to agentify the supply chain

Delivery supply chains are the lifeblood of contemporary businesses — and a new startup is betting that purpose-built AI agents can make them run a lot more smoothly.

On Tuesday, Pedestal AI emerged from stealth with $4.7 million in seed funding. The company’s goal is to use agentic AI to make supply chains faster and more reliable — a plan that’s already brought them together with Fortune 500 companies and some of the largest shipping firms in the world.

The seed round was led by Eclipse, with participation from RRE, Converge VC, Timescale Ventures, and VitalStage. Pedestal says the funding will be used to expand its product and engineering time, grow the marketing organization, and support additional deployments.

In practice, much of Pedestal’s work is about removing friction from the shipping process — with agentic technology used as a means to that end. In one case, the company reduced missed deliveries for a major furniture company by 20% by deploying agents to take on some of the work that drivers were supposed to do. The agentic system they built would call customers 30 minutes before a delivery and collect any special instructions; if the call didn’t go through, a text would be sent from a separate system. Along the way, the deployment collected a wealth of data about friction points in the process, which could be used to optimize the system even further.

CEO Raj Aggarwal says contemporary AI systems are particularly useful for dealing with that data. “The challenge is that the data is messy and it’s constantly changing,” Aggarwal told TechCrunch. “It’s disparate because it sits in so many legacy systems. That’s what makes it hard.”

Supply chain AI has become a particularly hot space in recent months as alumni from autonomous driving teams look to launch their own startups. The supply chain startup Atomic, launched by a team of Tesla alumni, came out of stealth in 2025 with an emphasis on streamlining inventory. Another venture called Loop was launched by Uber alums to focus on predictive analytics.

As Aggarwal sees it, deliveries are particularly well-suited to training reinforcement learning systems. Because of their high volumes and easily measured outcomes, it’s easy to fine-tune contemporary AI systems toward what’s working and away from what isn’t.

Perhaps most importantly, it’s the kind of problem that can have an immediate effect on a client’s bottom line, making it easy to justify the expense.

“If you can get it right,” he told TechCrunch, “you can have really big impact.”
