#271. This boiled bag of offal is banned in the US. In Scotland it's a fine-dining treat

Anthony Bourdain loved haggis. But even the late, great American chef, writer and television host recognized that Scotland’s national dish, with its “sinister sheep parts” wrapped in a shroud of mystery and half-invented history, could be a hard sell.

“Don’t let them tell you otherwise, that’s really one of life’s great pleasures,” Bourdain said on one of his gastro-curious pilgrimages to Glasgow. “There is no more unfairly reviled food on Earth than the haggis.”

A mash-up of diced lung, liver and heart mixed with oatmeal, beef suet, onion and assorted spices, haggis was traditionally made by stuffing these raw ingredients into the stomach of a recently slain sheep and boiling the lot to a state of palatability.

Instagrammable is not the word that immediately comes to mind. In our 21st-century world, where “clean” eating and processed pap overlap, haggis can seem like an “Outlander”-style outlier from another age.

Yet, by some alchemy, once cooked to its required “warm-reekin’ (steaming)” state, it adds up to much more than the sum of its modest parts. It’s offaly charm has kept nose-to-tail eating alive among a younger generation of Scots that has largely turned its back on the tripe, liver and kidneys their predecessors enjoyed (or endured).

Carefully prepared, haggis tastes both oaty and meaty; it is dark and crumbly, a little crispy at the edges but still moist; earthy but also savory and spicy; deep-tasting and profoundly warming, the perfect foil for its traditional garnish of floury mashed potatoes and orange bashed turnip.

“It’s like a cuddle for the stomach,” says Nicola Turner, a 35-year-old office administrator from Helensburgh, a town on western Scotland’s Firth of Clyde.

For children of the 1960s and ’70s, like crime novelist Ian Rankin, haggis meals were a choice between the classic meat-and-two-veg plate and the battered and deep-fried, chip-shop iteration loved by both his friend Bourdain and his quintessentially Scottish detective character, Inspector John Rebus.

Now myriad other treatments have blossomed.

“I’m pretty sure the first time I dined with AB in Edinburgh we had haggis in filo pastry with a jam-style – maybe blackcurrant – sauce,” Rankin recalled. “He was a big fan of haggis and of chip shops. Rebus will have enjoyed the occasional haggis supper from his local chip shop. He was definitely a fan, as am I.”

“It is all about the spicing and the texture,” says the Scottish food writer, novelist and cook Sue Lawrence, a champion of haggis’ adaptability for use in other dishes. “If you didn’t know what was in it, you wouldn’t think ‘oh that tastes of liver or whatever.’ It is all nicely chopped up and the oatmeal gives it a lovely texture. It could easily be a nice, big mince dish.”

These US restaurants and chefs are James Beard Award finalists

Lawrence uses haggis as an alternative to beef and pork ragù in lasagna and in her pastilla, a version of the North African dish in which a hand-made haggis from the Isle of Mull substitutes for the traditional poultry or seafood filling. The filo pastry savory is flavored with the spice blend ras el hanout, apricots, chile, orange zest and almonds before being sprinkled with cinnamon and icing sugar.

Such cultural crossovers serve as a reminder that haggis could easily be a dish with nothing specifically Scottish about it at all. Records of similar quick and portable preparations of the fast-perishing innards of sheep and other animals date back to ancient Rome and Greece.

Haggis-like combinations of offal and grains are part of the culinary history of several countries. Spain has chireta, Romania drob and Sweden polsa, while chaudin, or ponce, is a rice and meat-stuffed pig stomach that is a staple of Cajun cooking.

In neighboring England, recipes for “hagese,” “hagws of a schepe,” “haggas” or “haggus” pop up in recipe books published between the 15th and 17th centuries, probably preceding written records north of the border.

Etymological evidence points to the term “haggis” having its roots in Old Norse, suggesting an early version of an oat-and-offal sausage might have arrived in Britain and Ireland on a Viking longboat.

But ever since it was first optioned by the poet Robert Burns in the late 1700s, the haggis backstory has been monopolized by Scotland and the Scots, sometimes mischievously.

It is, according to the kind of lore that Burns engendered, the dish a doughty Highlander would carry with him as he drove cattle through the glens to the markets of the central belt or the perfect picnic for a whisky smuggler plying his illicit trade by moonlight.

From such romantic notions it was a short step to turning the haggis into a wee wild beastie, one with longer legs on one side that was thus condemned to run round and round whichever hill it lived on. In 2003, a poll of American tourists in Scotland found that one in three of them believed they might encounter such a confused creature on a Caledonian vacation.

Bourdain, a native New Yorker, may have qualified as haggis’ biggest admirer since Burns, but his compatriots at the US Department of Agriculture remain unconverted to offal-filled paunch. Haggis imports into the United States were prohibited in 1971 as part of a ban on the consumption of all livestock lungs. Authentic versions of old school haggis remain culinary contraband in the US, as hard to lay your hands on as Cuban cigars.

Across the rest of the world, it’s a different story. According to leading producer Simon Howie, haggis is more widely appreciated and consumed now than it has been since Burns improvised his “Address to a Haggis” for the entertainment of well-to-do Edinburgh acquaintances.

Firmly tongue-in-cheek, the poem lauds the “Great chieftain o’ the pudding race,” as exactly the kind of unpretentious, hearty fare required to nourish a nation of braveheart warriors.

In comparison to the enfeebling foreign muck enjoyed by the capital’s claret-quaffing elites of the time – the olio, fricassée or ragoût that would “sicken a sow” – Burns urges his readers to wonder at the magical impact of haggis on his fellow sons of Scotland’s soil.

As the English translation of the original Scots language version puts it:

These days synthetic casings have largely replaced stomach but ovine and porcine innards remain at the core of most of the haggis produced in its homeland, said Howie, who estimates that his company Simon Howie Butchers, accounts for around 60% of the roughly two million haggises produced every year.

For Howie, versatility, value for money and convenience explain why this staple of the Scottish larder is thriving. Typically haggis retails in Scotland, which accounts for half of global consumption by volume, for around £6, or $7.70 per kilogram ($3.36/pound). That’s around half the price of less expensive cuts of beef or a third of the price of Scotch lamb while enjoying a fairly similar nutritional and calorific profile.

“You can give your kids a meal that is not full of things you don’t want to feed them – for a few pounds you can feed three strapping lads,” Howie said.

“From a kitchen perspective, it is very simple because when it leaves our factory it is already cooked. So when you or a restaurant owner gets it into the kitchen all you have to do is heat it up to be piping hot. It couldn’t be more basic: a student with no cooking skills or a Michelin-starred chef do exactly the same thing to put it out on the plate.”

Its texture means haggis can also be usefully deployed in fine dining alongside leaner meat like venison or as a stuffing for poultry and game birds. Its spicy intensity means it is also finding uses in canapés and as a crouton-borne garnish for soups.

Buoyant sales are also underpinned by the increasing consumption of haggis in forms inspired by Scotland’s ethnic minorities.

Glasgow’s Sikh community pioneered haggis pakora in the 1990s and samosas, spring rolls and quesadillas have followed in its wake, often using a vegetarian version of the protein in which the offal is replaced by a mix of vegetables, pulses and mushrooms.

Such dishes are more than culinary twists. They are badges of belonging, and an indication that, two centuries after Burns grabbed it for the nation, haggis is as intimately entwined with Scots identity as ever.

Just ask Ross O’Cinneide, a promising 14-year-old fly-half in the junior section of Stirling County rugby club.

“Most of my friends and I like haggis,” he says. “Mum makes it for us sometimes after rugby and it’s got a very nice warming feeling. And it’s nice because it’s purely Scottish.”

#268. As decision model use skyrockets, is customization the next frontier?

In October, more than one in four requests on Vercel’s AI model router went to decision models, not LLMs — an enormous share of work for a kind of computerized intelligence that was invented only a month earlier.

Decision models are LLMs that don’t produce output tokens; instead, they’re tuned to use their general intelligence to quickly and cheaply decide between preset options, while registering its level of confidence in the choice. They’re useful in production environments, where fast decisions can be more important than the reasoning provided by a slower and more expensive frontier model.

TypeSafe became a $7.5 billion company after releasing its Jev family of models, which continues to dominate usage (at least where we can see metrics from services like OpenRouter, which handled more than 1.5 billion queries to TypeSafe’s models last week).

However, it’s easy to make a decision model, and dozens were released in the wake of Jev. One Amazon engineer made one in a weekend, and soon every hyperscaler had its own option. On Tuesday, Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella said that his company is rolling out its decision model, Microsoft-Decision-1.1, for wide use on Azure immediately.

The action is happening on both ends of the ecosystem: Sqwish, a U.K.-based AI startup behind a decision model called kettei fat, announced a $3 million pre-seed round today. Co-founders Ushnish Sengupta and Federica Freddi, who met as Cambridge grad students, had been developing small LLMs when Jev arrived on the scene, and they realized their architectures could be repurposed as a powerful decision model.

Data appears to be the key to a successful model in this category — TypeSafe CEO Diogo Almeida told TechCrunch that the hardest part of building Jev was finely calibrating its judgement using statistically-adjusted synthetic data. Sqwish, too, has been working with synthetic data, but its unique offering is built-in post-training tools that use a customer’s own data to improve the model’s judgement.

“What makes us not a commodity, I think, is the fact that we are offering our customers the same power…they can keep uploading their production data, and just the same way that we trained our models, their models can also keep getting better at the tasks that they care about,” CEO Ushnish Sengupta told TechCrunch.

Attempting to rank decision models is difficult; Sqwish’s “kettei fat” model has performed well on some benchmarks, but most builders of the technology say that the real proof is in the pudding: It’s generally simple for developers to sub in one model for another and see how it does in a specific context.

“The benchmark is more of an invitation to try the model out, and then the continual tuning is the reason why they should stick to us,” Sengupta said.

The Microsoft-Decision-1.1 model is also the result of the company carefully balancing across cost, latency, intelligence and calibration. Achint Srivastava, the engineering VP who led the project, agreed that too much focus on benchmarks is a mistake.

“What we see is that ‘benchmaxed’ models fall flat under real enterprise usage very quickly,” Srivastava wrote in an email. “Our testing underscores just how challenging it is to deliver accuracy, reliability, speed and cost efficiency in a single model. Microsoft-Decision-1.1 and Jev are among the few that perform strongly across all four dimensions, even when evaluated on ‘held out’ or unseen benchmarks reflective of real use cases.”

For startups like Sqwish, competition with hyperscalers and frontier labs is an opportunity. Freddi predicts that future systems will see so-called System One intelligence, like decision models, working in concert with System Two intelligence, like frontier LLMs.

“Even in that future, System One is maybe a $10 billion, $50 billion [opportunity], and OpenAI is doing this IPO with you know $1 trillion, $2 trillion market value in mind,” she told TechCrunch. “This is all we care about; whereas for them, this is just a side project.”

#265. Google to keep Gemini chats for 2 months, up from 72 hours, even with history off

Google is extending how long it keeps temporary Gemini chats and conversations made with chat history turned off to around two months, saying the change aims to improve safety and security as AI systems become more capable.

OpenAI, by comparison, says it may keep temporary chats in ChatGPT for up to 30 days, whereas Anthropic retains incognito chats in Claude for 30 days by default. However, companies may keep some data longer for legal or safety reasons, or to investigate policy violations.

The longer retention period comes as Google introduces separate settings for Gemini’s chat history and AI training. Until now, both were tied to a single “Keep Activity” setting. Users will now be able to keep their conversations in their chat history without using them to train Google’s generative AI models.

Google says both new settings will be turned off by default for users who previously disabled Keep Activity. For those who had Keep Activity turned on, their chat history will remain enabled. They can, though, decide separately whether Google can use their conversations to train AI models.

#263. Kwame Brathwaite, photographer of 'Black is Beautiful' movement, dies at 85

Editor’s Note:  This article was originally published by The Art Newspaper, an editorial partner of CNN Style.

Kwame Brathwaite, the pioneering activist and photographer whose work helped define the aesthetics of the “Black is Beautiful” movement of the 1960s and beyond, died on April 1, aged 85.

His son, Kwame Brathwaite, Jr, announced his father’s death in an Instagram post that read in part, “I am deeply saddened to share that my Baba, the patriarch of our family, our rock and my hero has transitioned.”

Brathwaite’s work has been the subject of resurgent interest from curators, historians and collectors in recent years, and his first major institutional retrospective, which was organized by the Aperture Foundation, made its debut in 2019 at the Skirball Cultural Center in Los Angeles before touring the country.

Brathwaite was born in 1938 to Barbadian immigrants, in what he referred to as “the People’s Republic of Brooklyn” in New York, though his family moved from there to Harlem and then to the South Bronx when Brathwaite was 5 years old. He attended the School of Industrial Art (now the High School of Art and Design) and, according to profiles of Brathwaite in T Magazine and Vice, was drawn to photography by two moments. The first was in August of 1955, when a 17-year-old Brathwaite encountered David Jackson’s haunting photograph of a brutalized Emmett Till in his open casket. The second was in 1956, when — after he and his brother Elombe co-founded the African Jazz Arts Society and Studios (AJASS) — Brathwaite saw a young man taking photos in a dark jazz club without the use of a flash, and his mind became alight with possibility.

Using a Hasselblad medium-format camera, Brathwaite attempted to do the same, learning to work with limited light in a manner that enhanced the visual narrative of his imagery. He would soon also develop a darkroom technique that enriched and deepened how Black skin would appear in his photography, honing the practice in a small darkroom in his Harlem apartment. Brathwaite went on to photograph jazz legends performing throughout the 1950s and ’60s, including Miles Davis, John Coltrane, Thelonious Monk and others.

“You want to get the feeling, the mood that you’re experiencing when they’re playing,” Brathwaite told Aperture Magazine in 2017. “That’s the thing. You want to capture that.”

A 106-year-old from the Philippines is Vogue's oldest ever cover model

By the early 1960s, alongside the rest of AJASS, Brathwaite began using his photography and organizing prowess to consciously push back against whitewashed, Eurocentric beauty standards. The group came up with the concept of the Grandassa Models, young Black women whom Brathwaite would photograph, celebrating and accentuating their features. In 1962, AJASS organized “Naturally ’62”, a fashion show held in a Harlem club called the Purple Manor and featuring the models. The show would go on to be held regularly until 1992. In 1966, Brathwaite married his wife Sikolo, a Grandassa Model whom he had met on the street the year prior when he asked if he could take her portrait. The two remained married for the rest of Brathwaite’s life.

By the 1970s, Brathwaite’s focus on jazz shifted to other forms of popular Black music. In 1974, he traveled to Africa with the Jackson Five to document their tour, also photographing the historic “Rumble in the Jungle” boxing match between Muhammad Ali and George Foreman in what’s now the Democratic Republic of Congo that same year. Commissions in this era also saw Brathwaite photographing Nina Simone, Stevie Wonder, Sly and the Family Stone, Bob Marley and other music legends.

Creating for community: The Black designers placing identity and heritage at the center of their brands

Throughout the ensuing decades, Brathwaite continued to explore and develop his mode of photography, all through the lens of the “Black is Beautiful” ethos. In 2016, Brathwaite joined the roster of Philip Martin Gallery in Los Angeles, and he was continuing to photograph commissions as recently as 2018, when he shot artist and stylist Joanne Petit-Frère for The New Yorker.

T Magazine’s 2021 profile, published on the occasion of Brathwaite’s retrospective traveling to the Blanton Museum of Art in Austin, Texas, noted that the photographer’s health was failing such that he was unable to be interviewed for the article. A separate exhibition, “Kwame Brathwaite: Things Well Worth Waiting For,” is currently on view at the Art Institute of Chicago, where it will remain until July 24.

Top image: Kwame Brathwaite, “Untitled (Sikolo Brathwaite, Orange Portrait),” 1968

#260. Apple services, including iCloud, App Store, Apple Music, and more, hit by outage

After a recent slew of tech outages, service disruptions, and flight chaos this week, it’s now Apple customers who are facing technical issues.

The iPhone maker’s System Status page indicates that there are issues with iCloud’s Account & Sign In services affecting “all users,” which could make the service slow or unavailable. The App Store, AppleCare on Device, and Apple Music are also experiencing issues, the page indicates.

Like all major tech companies, Apple faces outages and other technical issues affecting its cloud services at times. However, today’s issues are likely more noticeable to end users because problems with iCloud can lead to account sign-in problems. This could cause your iPhone to ask for your Apple ID and password, and you could see other attempts to sign in to services with your Apple account information fail.

On Reddit, some folks also noted that they were unable to activate a new iPhone, which is likely related to these ongoing issues.

Given the recent increase in AI-driven cyberattacks, users across social media have already begun to speculate about the cause. In particular, users are expressing concerns about the Apple outage, as it arrives only days after technical failures at Microsoft led to its cloud systems that power flagship services like Outlook and Teams failing due to cascading software issues.

Similarly, a brief outage of Chase’s banking app this week garnered attention, as did an ongoing partial outage (at the time of publication) of the National Security Agency’s internal classified network, both of which were blamed on software glitches.

So far, none of the outages in the past week have been attributed to cyberattacks, but rather to technical problems.

Apple responded to TechCrunch’s request for comment (which it initially gave to Bloomberg), noting that the outages were caused by an internal update and not a cyberattack. “Many of our systems are back up and running after an internal update caused some outages this morning. We’re working to get the remaining systems online as soon as possible,” the statement read.

After the initial outages were reported, Apple’s status page was updated to include other outages impacting Apple School Manager, iCloud Mail, iCloud Web Apps (iCloud.com), Schoolwork, and the Volume Purchase Program.

By 6 PM ET on Tuesday, the status page showed all the outages were resolved.

This article was updated after publication with the status of the outages.

#257. Study finds slightly higher risk of autism diagnosis in areas with more lithium in drinking water, but experts say more research is needed

A new study found a moderately higher risk of autism spectrum disorder in children born to pregnant people exposed to tap water with higher levels of lithium, but experts caution that this association does not show a direct link between the two.

About 1 in 36 children in the US is diagnosed with autism spectrum disorder (ASD) each year, according to data from the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention.

Scientists still don’t know the exact cause of autism, a developmental disorder. Genetics may be a factor, but some have been looking at potential environmental causes, too.

Autism diagnosis has become more common, but pandemic disrupted early detection, CDC says

Cases may be on the rise, but that is also unclear. One study published this year on cases in the New York-New Jersey area found that autism diagnosis rates tripled among certain age groups between 2000 and 2016. A 2021 report found similar increases in cases, but the CDC says the increased number of cases is most likely linked to more doctors screening for the condition.

Lithium is an alkali metal that can be found naturally in some food and ground water. It’s used in batteries, grease and air conditioners, as well as in the treatment of bipolar disorder and some blood disorders. Its levels in US drinking water are not regulated, according to the US Geological Survey.

A new study, published Monday in the journal JAMA Pediatrics, found a small association between lithium and autism diagnosis in Denmark, where the researchers say the level of lithium in drinking water is similar to that in American water systems.

The researchers checked a database of people with psychiatric disorders for children born between 2000 and 2013 to find information on 8,842 cases of ASD and 43,864 participants who did not have ASD. They then measured the concentration of lithium in 151 public waterworks that served more than half of the Danish population and mapped out where pregnant people lived in relation.

For many neurodiverse people, 'The Sims' has been a lifelong comfort

As lithium levels in water increased, there was a modest increased risk of an ASD diagnosis. Specifically, compared with people at the lowest exposure level, those who had the second and third highest exposure during pregnancy had a 24% to 26% higher risk of ASD diagnosed in children. The group with the highest exposure had a 46% higher risk than those at the lowest level of exposure.

The researchers could not tell how much water the pregnant people drank, but they picked Denmark in part because residents there consume some of the lowest amounts of bottled water in Europe.

Experts say it’s important to note that the research can’t show that lithium exposure leads directly to an autism diagnosis.

Further study is required, said study co-author Dr. Beate Ritz, a professor of neurology in the David Geffen School of Medicine at UCLA, and a professor of epidemiology and environmental health at the UCLA Fielding School of Public Health.

“Any drinking water contaminants that may affect the developing human brain deserve intense scrutiny,” Ritz said in a news release. She added that the research would need to be replicated in other countries to look for a similar connection.

The implications of the findings are complex as far as public health policy is concerned, according to an editorial published alongside the study. Lithium levels in water, at concentrations that the study associated with a potential ASD risk, have also been linked with health benefits such as lower rates of hospitalization for psychiatric disorders and suicide.

“If all these of associations are valid, the wisdom of Solomon will be required to develop guidelines for lithium in drinking water that are maximally protective of the entire population,” wrote Dr. David C. Bellinger, a professor of neurology and psychology at Harvard Medical School. “Until the basic biology of ASD is better understood, it will be difficult to distinguish causal from spurious associations.”

'Heartbreaking' delays in autism diagnosis and treatment got even worse during pandemic

Dr. Max Wiznitzer, director of the Rainbow Autism Center at University Hospitals Rainbow Babies and Children’s Hospital in Cleveland, points to other research on the effects of lithium on pregnant people who take it for mental health disorders. Those studies – which look at people exposed to much higher levels than are found in drinking water – do not show a connection with autism spectrum disorder.

“It’s an interesting association, but causation is definitely not proven,” said Wiznitzer, who was not involved in the new research. “We have to see if there’s a viable and biologically plausible mechanism by which a small amount of lithium in the water supply can somehow do this, yet pharmacologic dosing of lithium in women with bipolar disorder has not been reported to be causing increased risk of ASD.”

Other studies have also suggested connections between ASD and environmental exposures to things like pesticides, air pollution and phthalates. But none of them points to any of these factors as a direct cause of the disorder.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

A link between environmental exposure and ASD is hard to prove, Wiznitzer said. With research showing that increased exposure to air pollution raises the risk of giving birth to a child with ASD, for example, he often wonders whether pollution is the determining factor or if it’s just the populations who live in more polluted areas.

“There’s a lot of speculation about about environmental factors, but how many of them are truly causally associated?” Wiznitzer said. “We are bombarded with a variety of environmental stressors in our everyday lives. We have to figure out how to basically safely navigate them, and this is probably not one that’s high on our list.”

#254. Google expands access to Gemini Tasks, which can help with calls, purchases

Google is expanding the rollout of Tasks, a toggle in Gemini that converts the chatbot into a more active helper.

Unlike Gemini’s Chat, which merely allows users to talk to Google’s AI assistant for informational purposes, Tasks empowers the app to complete basic secretarial functions, deputizing it as a kind of personal assistant.

How, specifically, can you use it? Google says that the app can call businesses and book appointments on users’ behalf; it can also complete purchases for them via Google Pay, and even sign into websites securely if prompted to do so.

In a blog post, Josh Woodword, VP of Gemini, claims to have recently used the feature to call local car dealerships to schedule a service appointment for his family’s minivan. He says he also used it to analyze his electrical bill.

Getting the function up and running is easy. Users merely flip a toggle within the app from Chat to Tasks, which enables the feature. Users can also send texts to Gemini or call the app to give it commands.

#252. Model ML raises $250M to automate investment banking grunt work

Model ML, a London-based startup that uses AI to automate work traditionally performed by junior bankers and consultants, including building financial models, preparing pitch decks, and writing due diligence reports, has raised $250 million in Series B funding at a $1.5 billion valuation. The new round, which comes a year after its $75 million Series A round was announced, was co-led by General Atlantic and earlier backer QED Investors.

Brothers Chaz and Arnie Englander co-founded the three-year-old startup after previously co-founding two other companies, including Fat Llama, a peer-to-peer rental marketplace acquired by Swedish rival Hygglo for $41.5 million in 2022.

Model ML develops AI agents that integrate with clients’ systems, data, and tools, including Excel and PowerPoint. The startup says its agents understand the full context of deals that bankers and consultants are working on and can automatically update financial models and presentations when new developments affect their analysis.

Other startups automating the routine, repetitive tasks for investment bankers and management consultants include Hebbia and Rogo, which raised $160 million at a $2 billion valuation in April.

Anthropic and OpenAI have also built tools for financial professionals, but Model ML says it runs on open source models and that doing so allows it to operate at about 2% of the cost of frontier models while delivering faster results.

#250. Syrian refugee elected mayor of German town, years after fleeing war

A Syrian who arrived in Germany as a refugee in 2015 has won a mayoral election in the southwestern German state of Baden-Württemberg.

Ryyan Alshebl, who left his hometown of As Suwayda in Syria eight years ago, ran as an independent in the municipality of Ostelsheim. He won 55.41% of the votes on Sunday, beating two German candidates, Marco Strauss and Mathias Fey.

Locals cheered the 29-year-old when he welcomed his win, a victory he described as “sensational,” German local broadcaster SWR reported Monday.

“Today, Ostelsheim sent an example for broad-mindedness and cosmopolitanism for the whole of Germany,” he said, according to to German public broadcaster ZDF. “That’s not something that can be taken for granted in a conservative, rural area.”

Alshebl’s first call after his victory was to his mother in Syria, who was thrilled with the news, SWR reported.

The Association of Municipalities of Baden-Württemberg said Alshebl is the first man with Syrian roots to run for and win a mayor’s office. He will start his role in June.

'You are not a refugee.' Roma refugees fleeing war in Ukraine say they are suffering discrimination and prejudice

Ostelsheim residents have welcomed their incoming mayor. “The fairy tale has come true, and the right man has become our mayor,” Annette Keck, who lives in the village, told SWR.

Strauss, one of his opponents, congratulated Alshebl. “I wish you good luck and at the same time ask for support for Mr. Alshebl, for our shared Ostelsheim,” he said on Facebook.

The state’s Integration Minister Manne Lucha said that Alshebl’s victory showed that diversity is a natural part of Baden-Württemberg. “I would be very pleased if Ryyan Alshebl’s election encourages more people with a migration history to run for political office,” he said.

Not everyone has been so warm to the 29-year-old. ZDF reported the Syrian received hateful comments on the campaign trail.

The young politician went from house to house, promoting his election program, and “the experiences were predominantly positive,” but there was also a minority of far-right fringe voters in Ostelsheim that did not want to accept him due to his Syrian roots, Alshebl told ZDF.

Born to a schoolteacher and agricultural engineer in Syria, Alshebl described his life as carefree until the age of 20, according to his campaign website.

At the time, protests against the Syrian government that began in 2011 soon devolved into chaotic war. The fighting and later rise of ISIS forced 10.6 million people from home by late 2015 – about half of Syria’s pre-war population.

Alshebl faced the dilemma of being drafted for military service with the Syrian army or leaving the country, according to his website.

While many Syrians were displaced internally or fled to countries in the region, others like Alshebl made the dangerous journey to Europe. He was 21 years old at the time, and said he crossed from Turkey to the Greek island of Lesbos in a rubber dinghy.

Former German Chancellor Angela Merkel had implemented a brief open-door policy in 2015 that saw the country take in about 1.2 million asylum seekers in the following years, including Alshebl.

They were welcomed into British homes. Celebrating their first Christmas together, Ukrainians wonder if that hospitality will last

The move sparked a backlash in Germany and the sudden growth of the far-right, anti-immigration Alternative for Germany (AfD) in the wake of summer 2015.

Once in Germany, Alshebl lived close to Ostelsheim and said at the time he felt “there is only one thing you can do: get back on your feet quickly and start investing in your own future quickly.”

For the last seven years he worked in the administration of Althengstett town hall, in a neighboring town. He drew from his experience, he said in his campaign, and made digital access to to public administration services one of priorities. Flexible childcare and climate protections are also on his agenda.

Alshebl, who is a member of the Green Party and now has German citizenship, pledged during his campaign that once elected as mayor he would move to Ostelsheim.

#247. Waymo suspends freeway rides nationwide after California DMV order

Waymo robotaxis are no longer allowed to operate on California freeways after the state’s Department of Motor Vehicles restricted the Alphabet-owned company’s operations following several incidents that raised safety concerns.

California’s DMV, which regulates the testing and deployment of autonomous vehicles in the state, issued the operational restrictions on Waymo on November 11. However, the restrictions weren’t made public until Tuesday. San Francisco Business Times was the first to report the order.

The DMV confirmed the new restrictions to TechCrunch, stating that the department took action after three Waymo autonomous vehicles entered an active construction closure on U.S. Route 101 near Burlingame, and another separate incident involving California Highway Patrol vehicles on Highway 280 in San Francisco.

Waymo has complied and is preventing its robotaxis from accessing freeways in any of the markets it operates in, including those outside of California. Waymo currently has commercial operations in 15 U.S. cities.

The DMV said Waymo’s freeway operations pose an imminent hazard to public safety.

“The Department determined that Waymo’s driverless freeway operations pose an imminent hazard to public safety due to recurring incursions by its vehicles into active construction zones where construction workers, vehicles and equipment are present, despite a prior restriction and corrective actions implemented following similar incidents that occurred on May 18, 2026,” according to an emailed statement sent to TechCrunch.

The DMV said the restrictions will remain in place until Waymo can demonstrate that it has taken “effective corrective actions” to address the deficiencies.

Waymo spokesperson Dana Gallagher told TechCrunch the company has temporarily restricted freeway operations across its other operating markets to evaluate its performance.

“Safety is at the foundation of everything we do,” Gallagher said in a statement. “Following recent freeway construction zone encounters on U.S. 101 in California that did not involve any injuries or collisions, the California DMV has implemented an operating restriction on freeway operations in the state. In alignment with our safety-first principles, we have also temporarily restricted freeway operations across our other operating markets to evaluate our performance. Our ride-hailing service continues to operate safely on surface streets, serving over half a million trips each week. We are working with the California DMV and other state officials diligently and responsibly to resume freeway operations.”

Waymo began offering freeway rides in late 2025, a commercial milestone that has been central to its expansion in major metro areas. Freeway access allows Waymo to connect riders to airports and other destinations more efficiently, slashing travel times by avoiding slower surface streets.

That has been particularly important in the San Francisco Bay Area because Waymo’s service area stretches from the city south to Silicon Valley. Freeway access has dramatically shortened trips across the Peninsula that previously took 45 minutes or more than an hour.

In May, Waymo abruptly stopped operating on highways. The company didn’t cite a specific reason at the time, but several social media posts showed Waymo robotaxis driving through traffic cones and struggling to navigate construction zones.

Waymo robotaxis returned to freeways in July after the company said it updated the self-driving software system to improve performance around freeway construction zones.

#245. Google joins the tech outage trend by announcing it’s not having one

Was Google feeling a little left out of the recent wave of tech outages? The company took to X on Tuesday to announce that its Gmail service is actually running just fine. In a post, the company said it was not currently seeing evidence of a broad Gmail service disruption and reminded users they could always check Gmail’s real-time status on its online dashboard.

As it turns out, the company was trying to head off reports of a Gmail outage that began circulating on X on Tuesday around the same time that Apple was seeing a number of verified outages of its own. While some posts on the social network suggested Gmail was experiencing a disruption, others said they were not having any problems.

The concerns seemed to stem from an X post by Downdetector, a crowdsourced website where users can report service disruptions themselves and see if others are having problems. Downdetector is not an official source for verifying or understanding the true scope of internet outages, and as people become more concerned about possible cyberattacks, the site’s data may become less valuable over time if every minor glitch and hiccup is reported as an “outage” by non-technical users.

The fact that Google had to announce it was not having an outage speaks to the growing anxiety around the reliability of major tech services. Over the past week, Apple and Microsoft experienced outages, an FAA network failure led to a nationwide ground stop, and some banks and airlines also reported disruptions.

However, Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton said on Fox Business Network’s “The Claman Countdown” on Tuesday that the U.S. is not currently facing coordinated cyberattacks. He told the outlet, “We do not see the indicia of a coordinated cyberattack — let me just be clear about that — and we do not see the indicia of state-sponsored activity here.”

Instead, Clayton attributed the rise in issues to businesses patching their systems. The patching may be a downstream effect of AI, as newer models have become increasingly adept at uncovering previously unknown vulnerabilities. “They’re upgrading them, they’re putting patches in place,” he said, adding that this is happening at “a much larger scale” than before.

As a result, some of the systems are “not coming back as expected in all cases,” he added.

#244. These are the animals people most want to see photographed

Editor’s Note:  Call to Earth is a CNN editorial series committed to reporting on the environmental challenges facing our planet, together with the solutions. Rolex’s Perpetual Planet initiative has partnered with CNN to drive awareness and education around key sustainability issues and to inspire positive action.

Originally a term used by trophy hunters in Africa, the “Big Five” described the animals most challenging to shoot and kill: the lion, elephant, leopard, rhino, and buffalo. Nowadays, it is more loosely used to refer to some of Africa’s largest and most iconic animals.

However, British photographer Graeme Green has reclaimed the narrative, creating a global “New Big Five” for wildlife photography. In 2021, 50,000 people around the world voted for the five animals they would most like to photograph, or see photographed, in the wild. Five animals were crowned the winners: the elephant, polar bear, lion, gorilla and tiger.

This week, sees the publication of “The New Big 5” photography book, which features images of those animals and other at-risk wildlife, from photography legends such as Ami Vitale, Steve McCurry and Paul Nicklen, and essays from famous conservationists and activists including like Jane Goodall and Paula Kahumbu.

Green says that the book celebrates wildlife and is a global call to action on issues impacting wildlife, including habitat loss, poaching, pollution and climate change.

Green was on assignment in Botswana at least a decade ago when he came up with the idea for a project to encourage people to “shoot with a camera, not a gun,” he says.

“I thought this would be a way to get people really focusing on wildlife, thinking about the wildlife they love, thinking about the animals that are at risk.”

Ten photographs that made the world wake up to climate change

In total, the book includes the work of 144 globally renowned wildlife photographers from Ecuador to India. Curating the images took nearly two years of work, says Green.

“I think these are some of the most beautiful and creative images that I’ve seen put together in one book,” says Green. “These are the species that we are at risk of losing.”

According to the UN, nature is declining at an unprecedented rate, with around one million of the planet’s animal and plant species facing extinction. The “New Big 5,” all of which are threatened, act as ambassadors for what is happening in the natural world, says Green.

As well as being a powerful reminder of what we stand to lose, the book also points people towards potential solutions. Featured essays explore the benefits of rewilding and the importance of indigenous communities in conservation.

A chapter on endangered species from bees to blue whales illustrates the alarming threat climate change poses to animals outside of the “New Big 5.” “That’s only the tip of the iceberg – I could have included thousands of pictures because that’s how serious the situation is,” says Green.

Why a wildlife photographer took 40,000 photos to capture these extraordinary birds

Jane Goodall, a leading conservationist who also wrote the afterword to the book, said in a press release that “we have a window of time during which we can start to heal some of the harm we have inflicted on the natural world, but only if we get together and take action now.

“I hope the photos will lead people into the wonderful worlds of these iconic species. Then, perhaps, other people will become involved in helping to create a world where wildlife can flourish for future generations to enjoy,” she said.

“The New Big 5: A Global Photography Project For Endangered Wildlife,” by Graeme Green, published by Earth Aware Editions, is on sale from April 4, 2023.

This story has been updated to reflect the range of issues covered by the book.

#241. Why did some Apollo missions carry a grenade launcher?

Where Readers Share Great Links. Join Free

Some Apollo missions carried a grenade launcher and grenades as part of an active seismic experiment. The idea was to create controlled surface explosions near deployed seismometers so scientists could study how shockwaves traveled through the lunar subsurface and infer its structure.

#240. SEC reportedly investigating Nvidia amid Huang exit

The Securities and Exchange Commission is opening a civil probe into Nvidia over whether the company’s disclosures misled investors, according to The Wall Street Journal.

The investigation adds to Nvidia’s stack of drama after CEO Jensen Huang resigned following news of his extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, Alice Huang, an executive at Singapore-based data company Megaspeed.

The news of the SEC probe also comes as Nvidia reports earnings to a market that has seen its stock fall more than 10% since Huang departed. The chipmaker reported revenue of $115.8 billion, up 103% from a year ago.

#237. Amazon and Google will together own nearly 30% of Anthropic after its IPO

Anthropic on Tuesday disclosed more details about the biggest investment agreements with major cloud providers Amazon and Google.

While we already knew that Amazon stood to make a killing on its Anthropic investments from the IPO, Anthropic revealed that Amazon’s total stake will come in at just shy of 15% (14.7% to be exact).

Even more interesting is that Amazon could have, if it wished, owned an even bigger share of the company. When Amazon invested billions in Anthropic in 2023 and 2024 (long before Claude Code took the enterprise world by storm, back in the days when Anthropic looked like it would forever be a speck in OpenAI’s shadow), the cloud giant obtained promissory notes that could convert into equity.

These notes carry conversion prices of about $2.09 or $5.05 per share, depending on the year of investment. Amazon has opted to convert a chunk of them as part of the IPO into a special class of non-voting shares that will give it about 15% ownership. (The shares can receive dividends and convert to standard voting Class A shares when sold, the filing says).

But it has also opted to hold on to billions of dollars’ worth of the notes for now. If it converted them all right now, it would own just under 24% of Anthropic (23.8% to be precise).

Google, meanwhile, will own just under 14% (13.7%), also in non-voting shares, which come from the conversion of notes, as well as the “private placement” sale of shares as part of this IPO. A group of major tech companies (AMD, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia, and TSMC Partners) have agreed to buy $26 billion worth of this non-voting stock. Google and Nvidia each agreed to buy $10 billion worth.

So while a lot of investors stand to make billions of dollars in Anthropic’s IPO, Amazon is one of the biggest winners, and that doesn’t even count that AWS stands to earn from Anthropic’s commitment to spend more than $100 billion on its cloud services over the next decade.

#236. Giving Marjorie Taylor Greene a platform isn't good for America

Editor’s Note:  Dean Obeidallah, a former attorney, is the host of SiriusXM radio’s daily program “The Dean Obeidallah Show.” Follow him @DeanObeidallah@masto.ai. The opinions expressed in this commentary are his own. View more opinion on CNN.

Last year, GOP Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene of Georgia spoke at a white nationalist event organized by Holocaust denier Nick Fuentes that caused Republican leaders to denounce her.

Last week, Greene’s Twitter account was temporarily suspended by the Elon Musk-headed platform over a tweet with a graphic referring to a “Trans Day of Vengeance,” as she denounced a planned transgender rights rally.

And come Tuesday, Greene has announced plans to protest in New York City when former President Donald Trump is expected to be arraigned on an indictment of more than 30 counts, calling the proceedings against him an “unconstitutional WITCH HUNT!”

But on Sunday, Greene was featured on CBS’ “60 Minutes” in an interview the long-running show promoted on Twitter with the tease: “Republican Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene, nicknamed MTG, isn’t afraid to share her opinions, no matter how intense and in-your-face they are. She sits down with Lesley Stahl this Sunday on 60 Minutes.” The images attached to this tweet by “60 Minutes” include Greene and Stahl walking through the US Capitol, taking a stroll outside and Greene showing Stahl something on her phone.

It's time for Marjorie Taylor Greene to go

In the segment that aired Sunday night, Stahl noted the congresswoman had moved from the fringe to the GOP’s front row in two years despite a “sharp tongue” and “some pretty radical views” as well as “over the top” comments such as “the Democrats are a party of pedophiles.” Stahl also referred to video of Greene chasing a Parkland, Florida, school shooting survivor, still maintaining that the 2020 election was stolen and failing to criticize Trump over spending. (The interview was conducted before news of his indictment.)

But Stahl didn’t mention Greene spoke at a white nationalist event a year ago while a member of Congress or her extreme anti-Muslim views and her defense of January 6 rioters.

Criticism of CBS for amplifying Greene has been swift and well-deserved even before the program aired. Former GOP Rep. Adam Kinzinger of Illinois tweeted beforehand: “Wow. Insane that 60 min would do this.” (Kinzinger is a CNN senior political commentator.)

Journalist Molly Jong-Fast also slammed “60 Minutes” with the tweet: “Attention is currency and 60 minutes is spending its currency on the Jewish space lasers woman.” (Jong-Fast was apparently referring to Greene’s past claim that a massive California wildfire was started by “a laser” beamed from space controlled by a prominent Jewish banking family.)

David Hogg, who survived the 2018 horrific school shooting at Marjory Stoneman Douglas High School in Parkland and has since become an activist against gun violence, responded, “I look forward to your questions about why she thinks school shootings are fake and why she’s supported QAnon.”

Greene amplified the conspiracy theory — two years before being elected to Congress — that the Parkland shooting that took 17 lives was staged. And in 2019, Greene appeared on video confronting Hogg outside the Capitol when he was championing laws to save lives from gun violence, screaming that the then-teenager was a “coward.” She also called Hogg “#littleHitler” on social media.

Greene’s own tweet promoting Sunday’s segment was not filled with her typical smears of “fake news” when she doesn’t like the coverage. Rather, Greene urged people to tune in as she praised “60 Minutes” host Stahl (and misspelled her first name): “It was an honor to spend a few days with the legendary icon Leslie Stahl and talented crew @60Minutes.” Greene added, “Leslie is a trailblazer for women in journalism. And while we may disagree on some issues, I respect her greatly.”

Now, it is true that “60 Minutes” over its 50-plus-year history has featured what the show has dubbed “controversial” guests. These include a 2000 interview with Oklahoma City bomber Timothy McVeigh, whose 1995 terrorist attack left 168 dead, including 19 children. And the show did a 1979 interview with Iran’s Ayatollah Ruhollah Khomeini.

Perhaps “60 Minutes” views Greene — who has repeatedly downplayed and even defended the January 6, 2021, attack plus called for the GOP to become the party of “Christian nationalism” — in the vein of its long list of controversial guests. (CBS had not responded to a request from CNN for comment at time of publishing.)

However, the choice of Greene as a guest instantly recalls the comments of then-CBS CEO Les Moonves during Trump’s 2016 presidential campaign when he admitted that Trump’s candidacy “may not be good for America, but it’s damn good for CBS.” Moonves added, “The money’s rolling in and this is fun. … (T)his is going to be a very good year for us,” concluding, “Sorry. It’s a terrible thing to say. But bring it on, Donald. Keep going.”

Moonves may be gone from CBS, but it appears his mindset continues at “60 Minutes.” And that may be good for “60 Minutes,” but it’s definitely not good for America.

#232. Nvidia says it doesn’t plan to reappoint Jensen Huang as CEO

Despite Nvidia founder and former CEO Jensen Huang’s recent offer to return to the chip company he helped build from the ground up, the company is not looking to take him back.

During the company’s earnings call on Tuesday, Nvidia chairman Harvey C. Jones told listeners that Huang’s return was not something that company leadership was considering.

“On November 12, our board of directors announced that after disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company’s code of conduct, standards, and cultural norms,” Jones said, “Jensen Huang voluntarily resigned his positions as president, CEO, and board member. The Nvidia board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.”

On Tuesday, Huang had posted on X that since he left the company, he has “seen an outpouring of calls from Nvidia’s employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO,” and added that “if the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.”

Huang resigned last week in dramatic fashion following revelations of a number of extramarital affairs, including one with a woman who has been accused of being a Chinese intelligence agent. That woman, former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, has maintained close ties to the former Nvidia CEO for years. Critics worry that the relationship may have influenced Nvidia’s dealings with China, and U.S. intelligence officials have expressed suspicions that she works for China’s Ministry of State Security.

The development has sent shockwaves throughout the tech industry at a moment when the future of the AI business, in which Nvidia plays a pivotal role, hangs in the balance. It has also spurred investigations, including an independent investigation into Huang’s activities by Nvidia’s board and an SEC probe into Nvidia. Huang has been replaced by interim CEO Colette Kress, who is also the chipmaker’s chief financial officer and executive vice president, while the board searches for a permanent replacement. Jones added Tuesday that the company was looking at both internal and external candidates.

#231. Nvidia says its open source AI strategy remains intact after Jensen Huang’s exit

Nvidia interim CEO Colette Kress reaffirmed the company’s commitment to open source AI during Tuesday’s earnings call with analysts — the first since CEO Jensen Huang’s resignation.

“At Nvidia, we are committed to fostering a vibrant open model ecosystem, so every country, company, and industry can build its own intelligence,” Kress said in her opening remarks. She also noted that open models were accelerating AI adoption, citing a 35-fold year-over-year surge in weekly token volume on OpenRouter, including a 3.5-fold increase over the past three months.

Kress also spoke about Nvidia’s plans for Hugging Face, a platform for open source AI models that the company agreed to acquire for $12.9 billion in September, in what would be the second-largest acquisition in Nvidia’s history.

The platform, which serves more than 118 million developers and 200,000 companies, is still on track to join Nvidia, despite Huang’s ouster. Huang had heavily advocated for open source AI technology. His departure had raised questions about the company’s future approach to open source AI models, with Hugging Face’s CEO calling Huang’s departure “a sad day,” and promising to “keep fighting for open-source AI, harder than ever.”

So far, Nvidia’s plans for Hugging Face appear unchanged. The acquisition was not directly addressed during the Q&A portion of the earnings call.

“Together, we will scale its platform and make intelligence more open, more capable, and more accessible,” said Kress of Nvidia’s plans for Hugging Face.

Last week, Nvidia founder Huang resigned from his position as CEO following news about his extramarital affairs, including one with Alice Huang, an executive at the Singapore-based company Megaspeed who is suspected of being a Chinese spy. He’s also accused of having extramarital affairs with two direct reports and is now set to testify before Congress by Thanksgiving. Meanwhile, the SEC is investigating Nvidia over whether the company’s disclosures misled investors, The WSJ reported.

Within Nvidia, Huang pushed for the development of the Nemotron family of open-weight AI models built for long-running agentic AI systems and reasoning workloads. On today’s call, Kress noted that Nemotron is now the “most widely used U.S. open model on OpenRouter,” and it’s “where developers start to build their own intelligence.”

“That foundation is empowering the ecosystem,” she said.

#227. Nvidia is abandoning China after Jensen Huang’s ouster

Nvidia will no longer sell its H200 chips in China, acting CEO Colette Kress said Tuesday, ending the company’s participation in a controversial loophole in the U.S. ban on the sale of advanced chips to the country.

China’s access to leading AI accelerators was an important topic during the company’s first earnings call since CEO and co-founder Jensen Huang stepped down last week. The disclosure comes the same day Nvidia announced it was creating a board-level committee to oversee national security policies, export control compliance, and global trade risks.

Nvidia’s deal to sell H200 chips in China was brokered by Huang and President Donald Trump. The arrangement required the chips to be shipped through the U.S., allowing the government to collect a 25% tariff.

The pathway angered China hawks who wanted to deny Chinese labs computing power and maintain the United States’ lead in AI, but Huang long argued that it was more important for the U.S. to compete in China. In April, he told podcaster Dwarkesh Patel that “to concede that market for the United States technology industry is a disservice to our country.”

Huang’s ouster — in part over an affair with the CEO of a company under investigation for smuggling chips into China — has coincided with new rules, issued Monday, barring advanced U.S. chips from foreign data centers that serve Chinese customers remotely.

Investment bank Jefferies forecast that those rules might trim between $44 billion and $62 billion from Nvidia’s data center revenue next year. Referencing the new export controls, Kress said that the backlog of demand for advanced chips like Vera Rubin means the rules would have no impact on the company’s top-line revenue.

In the long term, the company may face the loss of potential customers in Singapore, Malaysia, Thailand, and Indonesia. Critics of Nvidia’s dependence on China claim that as much as 20% of its revenue comes from demand linked to the country — annual revenue at its Singapore subsidiary hit $104 billion in 2025 before Nvidia stopped disclosing those figures — but that’s clearly going to change.

Even before Nvidia decided to stop shipping H200s, China had largely refused to accept their delivery. The government would prefer to generate demand for the country’s domestic chip industry, even if major AI compute users want the still-superior Nvidia equipment, according to Jacob Feldgoise, a researcher at Georgetown’s Center for Security and Emerging Technology.

Meanwhile, Chinese frontier labs are finding ways to access Nvidia chips elsewhere. All of China’s leading AI models show evidence of having been trained on Nvidia Grace Blackwell chips that are prohibited from being exported to China.

That training may have taken place at data centers outside of China, but sales to those facilities will be barred under the new rules. If large data centers suited for training AI models want to buy Nvidia chips, they will have to certify they aren’t serving any Chinese customers, export control attorney Dan Fisher-Owens told TechCrunch.

If they can’t provide that certification, the new rules may force them toward other chip options and further eat into Nvidia’s market share.

#224. Stripe backer Atomico calls for Europe to own its success stories

Europe is home to many of the world’s fastest growing companies of all time, veteran VC firm Atomico highlighted in its latest annual State of European Tech report.

The report, for instance, notes that the likes of Black Forest Labs and Microagi have reached $100 million in revenue in months, not years. “I think we can put to bed the idea that there’s a fundamental reason or constraint on the ability to scale a breakout company from here in Europe,” Atomico partner and head of intelligence Tom Wehmeier (pictured) told TechCrunch.

Yet, there’s still more that the VC firm founded by Skype’s Niklas Zennström would like to see — and its call this year is for Europe to “build it” and “scale it,” but also “own it.” That’s a way of wishing these companies will remain European longer. But there’s also a sense that this success hasn’t been acknowledged enough, perhaps obscured by louder calls for improvements.

Wehmeier, the report’s lead author, was quick to opine that “policy, procurement and capital are not moving at the same speed as our founders.” But he said the tech sector should advocate for itself in an inspiring way.

“When it comes to policy, I think the language needs to be, ‘Hey, this is not us complaining. This is us saying here is an opportunity to make the magnitude of impact even greater,’” Wehmeier said. “When we frame it as problems, that becomes harder.”

This reframing may also help European tech improve its image abroad, where complaints around overregulation may have overshadowed Europe’s potential to leverage its deep tech talent and growing pool of repeat entrepreneurs to have even more of success stories.

Plenty could be still done to fully unlock this potential, and Wehmeier said that “the next few weeks are going to be a really important test, with EU Inc.” Also known as the 28th regime, this initiative would create a unified European corporate regime akin to the Delaware C Corp.

If the last stretch of EU Inc’s grassroots campaign achieves it goal, it will become much easier to create and operate businesses across the EU. It would also show that the tech system got itself heard, which could open the door to the kind of endorsement Atomico is hoping to see more of.

Politicians have strong incentives to give the tech sector what it wants. If it is Europe’s second largest sector, biggest contributor to growth, and biggest job creator, “Do we not want more of that?” Wehmeier asked rhetorically. But it is unclear whether it will win them any votes.

To make the answer more straightforward, the tech sector will have to work on having public opinion on its side. For Wehmeier, one key is to “demonstrate that this is translating into value for citizens.”

The report is supporting this endeavor with a mix of high-level data and examples from its portfolio; for instance, it notes that ICEYE’s satellites monitor severe weather, flooding, and other hazards; and Daniel Ek’s Neko Health has now done more than 100,000 body scans, some of which helped identify severe or life-threatening health conditions.

One limit to this argument is that Neko Health’s scans will seem pricey to most Europeans, who largely rely on collective, public health insurance. But Atomico seems to hope that its report will give readers FOMO not just as potential customers, but also as potential shareholders.

There’s more to be done for this to happen. Beyond joining the sector’s push for an EU capital markets union, he hopes more of Europe’s institutional investors will invest in the regions own tech companies. “We must get to the place where Europe and European beneficiaries are the biggest shareholders in the success that we create,” Wehmeier said.

Before that can happen, the industry must overcome anti-industry beliefs like fears of AI-induced job destruction. But Wehmeier is a believer in the power of proof. “I think that trust is going to be earned through showing results to the public — be that lower energy bills, better healthcare, more homes built for cheaper and faster, and having infrastructure that we can rely on.”

#221. What OPEC's surprise oil cut means for gas prices

OPEC and its allies’ surprise move to slash oil production will soon be felt at US gas pumps.

The group known as OPEC+ announced Sunday it would cut oil production by more than 1.6 million barrels a day starting in May, running through the end of the year. The news sent both Brent crude futures, the global oil benchmark, and WTI, the US benchmark, up about 6% in trading Monday.

The production cut announcement also had an immediate impact on gasoline futures, which will be passed onto US drivers much more quickly than the spike in oil prices. RBOB, the most closely watched wholesale gasoline price, was up about 8 cents a gallon, or about 3%, in morning trading.

“I think OPEC is reawakening the inflation monster,” said Tom Kloza, global head of energy analysis for OPIS, which tracks gas prices for AAA. “The White House has to be shocked and major-time pissed. It certainly alters the calculus for a while.”

The national average for US gas prices stood at $3.51, on Monday, according to AAA. Kloza said he could see it getting up to $3.80 to $3.90 in relatively short order thanks to the move by OPEC.

“We’re not going to get back to $5 a gallon. I don’t think we’re even going as high as $4,” he said. But he said by the end of the summer US drivers could be back above year-earlier prices, especially if there is a hurricane or other storms affecting production along the Gulf Coast.

The average US regular gas price a year ago stood at $4.19 a gallon in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and the disruption that caused to world’s energy markets. Prices eventually reached a record $5.02 a gallon on June 14, before starting a slow but steady decline over the course of more than three months during which the average price fell every day. The decline was partly driven by the release of oil from the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve, and partly by concerns that there could be a US or global recession that reduced the demand for gasoline.

Oil prices surge after OPEC+ producers announce surprise cuts

Even at $3.51, US gas prices were just below the $3.53 average on Feb. 23, 2022, the day before Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

Kloza said one thing keeping prices from getting anywhere near the record levels of 2022 is that the US plans additional releases from the SPR, and US oil production and refining capacity are both up. But a cut of 1 million barrels a day of oil by OPEC+ will not be easy to make up.

“They have ability to cut production and they seem motivated to do so,” he said.

#216. Scientists film deepest ever fish on seabed off Japan

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Cruising at a depth of 8,336 meters (over 27,000 feet) just above the seabed, a young snailfish has become the deepest fish ever filmed by scientists during a probe into the abyss of the northern Pacific Ocean.

Scientists from University of Western Australia and Tokyo University of Marine Science and Technology released footage of the snailfish on Sunday filmed last September by sea robots in deep trenches off Japan.

Along with the filming the deepest snailfish, the scientists physically caught two other specimens at 8,022 meters and set another record for the deepest catch.

Previously, the deepest snailfish ever spotted was at 7,703 meters in 2008, while scientists had never been able to collect fish from anywhere below 8,000 meters.

“What is significant is that it shows how far a particular type of fish will descend in the ocean,” said marine biologist Alan Jamieson, founder of the Minderoo-UWA Deep Sea Research Centre, who led the expedition.

Scientists are filming in the trenches off Japan as part of a 10-year study into the deepest fish populations in the world. Snailfish are members of Liparidae family, and while most snailfish live in shallow water, others survive at some of the greatest depths ever recorded, Jamieson said.

During the two-month survey last year, three “landers” – automatic sea robots fitted with high-resolution cameras – were dropped into three trenches – the Japan, Izu-Ogasawara and Ryukyu trenches – at varying depths.

In the Izu-Ogasawara trench, footage showed the deepest snailfish hovering calmly alongside other crustaceans on the seabed.

Jamieson classified the fish as a juvenile and said younger deep sea snailfish often stay as deep as possible to avoid being eaten by bigger predators that swim at shallower depths.

Another clip shot at between 7,500 and 8,200 meters in the same trench showed a colony of fish and crustaceans munching at bait tied to an undersea robot.

Images of the two captured snailfish – identified as Pseudoliparis belyaevi – provide a rare glimpse of the unique features that help the deep sea species survive the extreme environment.

They have tiny eyes, a translucent body, and their lack of swim bladder, which helps other fish float, works to their advantage, Jamieson said.

The professor said the Pacific Ocean is particularly conducive to vibrant activity due to its warm southern current, which encourages sea creatures to go deeper, while its abundant marine life provides a good source of food for bottom feeders.

Scientists would like to know more about creatures living at extreme depths, but cost is the constraint, Jamieson said, adding that each lander alone costs them $200,000 to assemble and operate.

“The challenges are that technology has been expensive and scientists don’t have a lot of money,” he said.

#215. AI in classrooms: Fighting the great 'dumbing down'

Research suggests that students who aren't careful about how they use ChatGPT and other AI tools risk poorer academic outcomes. It's a huge challenge for teachers.

Jakob is 15 years old and attends a secondary school for more academic students, known in Germany as a gymnasium. He and his classmates have already been using artificial intelligence-enabled tools, or AI, for the last two and a half years.

"I had a French test today, and yesterday I had ChatGPT explain a lot of the topics to me again," the student told DW. Jakob isn't his real name, because he doesn't want to publicly admit he and many others of his age use AI to do their homework.

Math, German, English, chemistry and even physics: In one click, the solution or the path to a solution is available thanks to AI.

In science, this is called "cognitive outsourcing." Basically, the required thinking is shifted to another device, whether that's a calculator, notepad or a large language model (LLM) like ChatGPT.

There's a difference with AI, though. A notepad or calculator can take over a step or two but rarely the whole thought process. That's not the same for an LLM, which can handle the entire problem-solving process.

Jakob admitted he's also had an AI help him with his homework. "But only when I was really stuck," he insisted. And he's also aware that it can become a problem when AI does all your homework. "The students who get good grades tend not to do that," he explained.

"They can't even think for themselves anymore," she told DW, complaining about some of her classes.

Even in ordinary classes, some of the students type their questions into their tablets or phones, asking for AI assistance. And should they be called upon in class, she knows that they're reading out the answers that the AI has prepared for them and hoping nobody notices.

Müller believes students these days are less able to comprehend text. "Because they read less and let AI summarize almost everything for them," she said.

Several studies have reached very similar conclusions.

That includes the latest PISA (Program for International Student Assessment) study from September. PISA, which is run by the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development, regularly evaluates the abilities of 15-year-old students around the world.

"Students who report using AI chatbots get lower scores in science, on average, than those who don't use AI," Andreas Schleicher, the OECD director for education and skills, wrote in his report in October. "This includes using AI to summarize texts they had to read, conduct preliminary research on a new topic, or draft texts for writing assignments."

With participants from more than 90 countries, PISA is considered the largest international comparative study of 15-year-olds worldwide. This year, German 15-year-olds posted their lowest PISA scores in the 25-year history of the study.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"The connections are complex and depend heavily on how generative AI is used, but the central insight is simple: Just as we don't get fit by watching sports but by doing sports ourselves, learning doesn't come from consuming content, but from the mind's productive cognitive engagement with new material," Schleicher explained.

Put simply, those who don't exercise their brains risk becoming less intelligent.

US think tank, the Brookings Institution, has already argued that the risks of using AI in education outweigh the benefits.

Its own research, which included young participants from around the world, speaks of students losing "fundamental cognitive and durable skills as they increasingly rely on AI to perform learning's intellectual work," as well as the decline of critical thinking, memory, perseverance and independent judgment.

But what can be done against the power of ChatGPT, Claude, Grok, Gemini and Meta AI?

New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani has banned the use of AI in public schools up to the eighth grade for the 2026 and 2027 school years — so that's up to about the age of 14 or 15.

Not everyone agrees this is the right thing to do. Julian Hinn, who teaches German to students aged between 15 and 18 in Germany, said class members can still use AI apps on their mobile phones. "And anyone who thinks they won't use it at home is ignoring reality," Hinn told DW.

Germany's annual JIM study, which tracks teenagers' media use and device ownership, supports that view.

Ninety-one percent of young people surveyed in Germany, between the ages of 12 and 19, stated that they use at least one AI application. The study also reports that 74% use AI for homework and learning.

The last JIM study actually dates back to 2024 so those percentages might be even higher now. Data on AI use in Asia, Latin America and the US from the Brookings study show similarly high figures.

German politicians have recognized the problem. Although education in Germany is the responsibility of the individual federal states, the so-called Standing Conference of the Ministers of Education and Cultural Affairs, or KMK, has been issuing statements on the use of AI since 2024.

The KMK's statements have been rather vague though, saying only that students should be enabled to engage with AI in a critical, reflective and responsible manner.

Teachers and students alike are aware that AI can be a powerful support in the classroom.

"The AIs simply explain things much better than textbooks," said Jakob, adding that he'll often get an AI to explain basic concepts to him.

Sometimes teachers can't respond like an AI, Hinn conceded. "The AI ​​can respond much better to the language and needs of the students."

That's why he thinks it's important to include AI in classes, starting from the 10th grade when students are around 15 or 16. Hinn doesn't think it should be introduced before that, when children's basic skills are still being developed.

In more senior classes, Hinn will encourage his students to question the AI critically. "We look at the quality of the solutions," he explained.

German teacher Müller has also integrated some use of AI into her lessons for older students. But in some aspects of the class, she also bans it altogether so that her students can focus on thinking for themselves.

"I understand very well that it's tempting to use AI," she said.

There are also other problems with AI in education. According to the Brookings study, students with basic skills can use AI to deepen their learning and progress faster.

But for children with gaps in their knowledge, they tend to adopt AI's errors, outsource their thinking and then fall even further behind. In this way, AI can exacerbate preexisting inequalities.

The PISA study notes that children from more affluent backgrounds often have a better start, and AI can widen that gap.

This is called the Matthew effect — the name comes from the biblical verse Matthew 25:29, "for unto everyone that hath shall be given." It basically means that early success in learning leads to better progress in the future, while early struggles equal a growing lag over time.

In the age of AI, teachers have become more motivated to challenge students to think independently and to consider their own personal autonomy.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Hinn, who also supervises the school newspaper, said: "I always tell everyone, you can use AI but the feeling of having written an article with your own pen is spectacular."

Both teachers also stress the idea of personal autonomy and responsibility. They believe this affects the students, especially because when they sit exams, they aren't allowed to use any aids or AI. And if they aren't able to solve their own problems, they will fail.

It's during exam time that the students really find out if they can solve those math, English or science problems by themselves.

Müller said one of her students recently confessed they were upset after a poor exam result. "He felt he simply couldn't think for himself anymore because of AI," she said.

This article was originally written in German.
