#162. Here’s the latest.

President Trump’s 48-hour trip started with a meeting with Southeast Asian leaders in Manila. He will later meet Xi Jinping of China and Vladimir Putin of Russia in Shenzhen, a Chinese economic hub.

President Trump landed in Manila on Tuesday to begin a 48-hour Asia trip in which he will meet with Xi Jinping of China and other world leaders, with an unpredictable agenda that could include the future of American tariffs, nuclear arms control, the war in Ukraine and U.S. restrictions on advanced A.I. chips.

Mr. Trump was scheduled to meet with President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines and attend a gathering of Southeast Asian leaders. After less than 24 hours in Manila, he will head to the APEC economic summit in Shenzhen, China, where he was expected to meet with Mr. Xi and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

The brief itinerary appears to reflect Mr. Trump’s preferences. He dislikes big summits where he often is not the sole subject of attention. And he enjoys meeting with leaders of powerful nations — especially Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin, two authoritarian leaders he often talks about in admiring terms.

Mr. Trump will be meeting Mr. Xi for the third time this year, and a three-way meeting between China, Russia and the United States would be the first of its kind.

On Tuesday, Mr. Trump caught the end of a dayslong meeting of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations, or ASEAN, after issuing tariffs on countries across the region, even allies. Those moves added to a sentiment in Asia that the United States was not a reliable partner but rather one that treasured its commercial interests over its security interests. The only event of substance on the president’s schedule on Tuesday — signing what he had called a “trade deal” with Thailand — did not happen because Mr. Trump said it was still being finalized.

Mr. Trump met with leaders from the so-called Quad — the United States, India, Japan and Australia — soon after his arrival. All three partners are smarting from high energy prices triggered by the Iran war, a conflict Mr. Trump never discussed with them before launching it in late February.

In Shenzhen — the heart of China’s robotics and artificial intelligence industries — Mr. Trump will pursue détente with Mr. Xi. He needs to extend a soon-expiring trade truce that limited tariffs on Chinese goods entering the United States in return for a Chinese commitment not to cut off rare earths and magnets.

Then comes the main event: the meeting with Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin. Referring to the trilateral meeting, Mr. Trump told reporters before leaving Washington: “We are going to be talking about denuclearization, a tough subject because it is very hard for any of us to say we’ll get rid of our nukes.” Chinese officials have made clear they are not interested in nuclear talks until their arsenal is roughly the size of the American one.

Mr. Trump also said, “We will talk about Iran a little,” insisting the situation was in “very good shape.” Analysts say it is unlikely that the meeting will make any progress on Iran, with Russia happy to see energy prices elevated since the war began, and China eager to see them rapidly decline.

Protests: Thousands of demonstrators assembled in downtown Manila on Tuesday in opposition to Mr. Trump’s visit and to a U.S.-led initiative called Pax Silica, which aims to unite partners around A.I. supply chains independent of China. Read more ›

North Korea: Mr. Putin could meet with Kim Jong-un if the North Korean leader also travels to China for the APEC summit, Russian state news agencies reported, citing the Kremlin’s spokesman. It was not clear whether Mr. Kim would attend the summit.

Ukraine: Mr. Trump also said he would talk about Ukraine, on which Mr. Putin’s aides are engaging in talks with the U.S. and Ukrainian officials, but also renewing demands that NATO pull back its troops to where they were before the alliance expanded toward Russia.

President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia could meet with Kim Jong-un if the North Korean leader travels to China for the APEC summit this week, the Kremlin said, reflecting the deepening of ties between their countries in recent years.

Asked about the possibility of a meeting, Dmitri S. Peskov, the Kremlin spokesman, said on Monday that it would “of course” happen if Mr. Kim went to Shenzhen, the Chinese economic hub and host city, according to Russian state news agencies. North Korea has not confirmed whether Mr. Kim plans to attend the summit.

Mr. Putin and Mr. Kim have publicly met three times since 2022, and North Korea has been a key supporter of Russia’s military actions in Ukraine.

As the war in Ukraine nears its fifth anniversary, with no end in sight, the Kremlin is expected to use Mr. Putin’s many meetings at the APEC summit to showcase how Russia is regarded as one of the major international powers alongside the United States and China.

A visit by Mr. Kim would be a rare foray outside North Korea for the leader, and it would further raise the prospect that he could meet with President Trump in China. On the first day of his 48-hour trip to Asia, Mr. Trump told reporters that Mr. Kim “would like” to come to the APEC summit. The president has repeatedly said he would like to meet with Mr. Kim again.

On Wednesday, Mr. Putin is scheduled to meet with Xi Jinping, China’s leader. On Thursday, he is set to take part in a trilateral summit with Mr. Xi and Mr. Trump, the first meeting of its kind. The Kremlin said on Monday that it was working to arrange a bilateral meeting between Mr. Putin and Mr. Trump.

Before his departure for China, Mr. Putin met on Monday in the Kremlin with Jo Yong-won, a secretary of North Korea’s ruling Workers’ Party. During the meeting, Mr. Putin said that North Korea’s help in driving Ukrainian forces from the Kursk region of Russia last year “marked a new chapter in the history of military cooperation between our countries.”

Moscow planned to unveil a monument in Kursk by the end of the year to honor the North Korean troops, Mr. Putin said.

During the final meeting of the day, with ASEAN leaders, President Trump told President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines:  “I don’t want to date myself, but I knew your parents very well. They were great. I believe in genetics. They did a good job. You have very good genetics, I can tell you that.”

It was not the first time Mr. Trump has praised the family of Mr. Marcos, who is the son of the dictator Ferdinand E. Marcos, who ruled the Philippines for 20 years, imposed martial law and was ousted in 1986.

The only event of substance on President Trump’s schedule today, the signing of a trade deal between the United States and Thailand, did not happen because it was still being finalized. Trump told reporters that “Thailand will eliminate all tariffs on U.S. imports” and that the United States would provide tariff relief, but the two sides signed only a memorandum about the agreement. The president said the deal would be done in the coming weeks.

During an address to Quad leaders, President Trump called Prime Minister Narendra Modi of India, which is a member of the group, “a great leader and a friend of mine, highly respected by everybody all over the world.” The president also praised Modi as he stood with other leaders and took a few questions, calling him a “very tough man.”

India and the United States are navigating a rocky patch in their relationship, largely tied to trade and India’s purchases of Russian oil. Although Modi and Trump signed an interim framework for a trade deal in February, talks have dragged on, frustrating both parties.

The Nvidia turmoil and its implications for the U.S.-China relationship is a backdrop to President Trump’s visit. On Tuesday, shares of technology companies in Asia tumbled after the Trump administration announced rules to restrict overseas access to advanced A.I. chips like those sold by Nvidia. In Hong Kong trading, Alibaba fell 5 percent, while Tencent’s stock dropped nearly 4 percent. The Chinese A.I. firms MiniMax Group fell 7 percent, and Z.AI Co. declined 5 percent.

In the United States, tech stocks stabilized from a sell-off the day before. Nvidia shares were up about 1 percent before the start of regular trading in New York. The company is set to report its latest financial results later on Tuesday.

President Trump is expected to meet with Prime Minister Anutin Charnvirakul of Thailand on Tuesday in Manila to sign what the White House has described as a “trade deal.”

But Thailand’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs said in a statement on Tuesday that Bangkok was expecting to agree only on a “joint statement marking progress in negotiations.”

The dissonance comes amid growing signs within ASEAN, the Association of Southeast Asian Nations, that the United States is not a reliable partner. The meeting between Mr. Trump and Mr. Anutin is taking place on the sidelines of an ASEAN summit.

For more than a year, Washington and Bangkok have been negotiating a new trade agreement that covers items like tariffs, food imports and labor rights.

Last year, the United States battered Thailand with a 36 percent tariff, one of the steepest rates levied by Mr. Trump after his return to office. The United States is one of Thailand’s largest export markets, including for goods like pet food.

Mr. Trump later reduced the tariffs to 19 percent, before the U.S. Supreme Court struck them down in February. The administration eventually found a way to still impose 12.5 percent tariffs on Thai goods.

The signing of a U.S.-Thai trade deal might still be far off, requiring Thailand’s parliament to pass a law, but the meeting delivers an easy publicity win for Mr. Anutin and Mr. Trump, who is known to revel even in perceived success.

Pavida Pananond, an economist at Thammasat Business School in Bangkok, said, “The level of trust Thailand has in the U.S. is not at the same level as before all this economic weaponization of tariffs.”

After handing over the chairmanship of ASEAN to Singapore, President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines held a news conference. “President Trump has just arrived,” he said. “He is waiting for us to go to him and to invite him to this really very informal kind of summit.”

Mr. Marcos ended his comments by apologizing for not taking more questions from journalists. “I cannot keep the president of the United States waiting,” he said. “Sorry.”

At his hotel, President Trump sat down for a meeting with Quad leaders and took a few questions. He said that he was “not worried” about bank outages and that the United States would be “looking into” reports of plans by the Iranians to drop a dirty bomb in Washington. He also said that Kim Jong-un, the North Korean leader, would “like to come” to the APEC summit in Shenzhen.

Several hundred activists massed on Tuesday in central Manila to march to the Philippine International Convention Center, the venue for the Association of Southeast Asian Nations summit.

By the time President Trump landed in the city, the ranks of the protesters had swelled to thousands.

Most of the demonstrators were university students who said that they were fed up with the government, which they said was more focused on preserving its relationship with the United States than helping impoverished Filipinos.

Cinna Sison, 18, who studies European languages at the University of the Philippines, said they were inspired by the recent mass protests in France.

“I love the French,” she said. “They know how to protest.”

Ms. Sison said she had worn colorful makeup to mark her first time attending a demonstration. “I am here to show my solidarity with fellow young activists. We are here to show our disgust to the fascist Trump and to Marcos for kowtowing to his every wish,” she said, referring to President Ferdinand Marcos Jr. of the Philippines.

Mr. Trump is expected to hold a bilateral meeting with Mr. Marcos on Wednesday before he heads to China for an Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit.

The Philippine president, who earlier this year described Washington as a “steadfast partner,” has said little publicly about meeting Mr. Trump. His administration has hinted that the two sides are working on a military aid agreement.

In Manila, Ms. Sison wore a red scarf on her head and a red shirt with the words “Silicone not Silica” — a reference to Pax Silica, a U.S.-led consortium of countries aiming to create artificial intelligence supply chains independent of China.

Ms. Sison added that Pax Silica was a symbolic return of the Americans to the Philippines, the site of former U.S. military bases, as Washington leaned on a regional ally to counter China.

“Neighborliness is important, but it is not important to be subservient to U.S. interests,” she said.

Nearby, Raja Bausing, a 22-year-old visual artist, was busy putting the finishing touches on a papier-mâché model of Mr. Trump holding Mr. Marcos.

“We want to show how Trump is a warmonger, while Marcos is a lap dog,” she said. “They are very disrespectful to Filipinos. Trump was welcomed in a grand way and yet we Filipinos are maltreated.”

President Trump landed in Manila on Tuesday, beginning a trip to Asia in which he will attend an ASEAN summit, greet members of the Quad, then travel to China for an APEC meeting.

Here’s what those acronyms mean, as well as some highlights from previous summits — including gold crowns and awkward handshakes.

The Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum is a group of 21 member economies, most of which are from that region. Its members also include Canada, Mexico, Australia, Russia and the United States. It promotes trade in Asia, but is not a free trade bloc. Its leaders meet annually, with the location rotating each year.

Meetings between world leaders on the sidelines of the summit can often overshadow the main proceedings. This year, in the Chinese tech hub of Shenzhen, Mr. Trump is expected to meet with President Xi Jinping of China and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia, as well as with Mr. Xi separately.

Last year, when Mr. Trump visited the South Korean city of Gyeongju during the week of the APEC meeting, President Lee Jae Myung of South Korea gave him a replica of a gold crown that researchers believe was worn by an ancient Korean ruler. Mr. Trump also met with Mr. Xi in the nearby city of Busan, where the two struck a truce in their countries’ trade war.

A notable incident happened in Sydney, Australia, in 2007, when a group of local television comedians brought an Osama bin Laden look-alike through security. The team came within yards of a hotel where President George W. Bush was staying.

The Association of Southeast Asian Nations is a grouping of 11 countries — Brunei, Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, Malaysia, Myanmar, the Philippines, Singapore, Thailand, East Timor and Vietnam — which promotes political cooperation and stability across the region. Its leaders meet twice annually.

The bloc was formed in 1967 and represents nearly 700 million people. Its economy and its role in global markets have strengthened in recent years. The United States also sees ASEAN as a geostrategic bulwark against China.

In 2009, an ASEAN meeting in Thailand fell into acrimony when Secretary of State Hillary Clinton compared North Korea’s leaders to “small children” when discussing their nuclear program. The country’s leadership, in turn, described Ms. Clinton as a “schoolgirl” and a “pensioner.”

During the 2017 ASEAN summit in Manila, Mr. Trump grimaced while jostling with Southeast Asian leaders for an awkward cross-armed handshake that was captured in a group photograph.

The Quadrilateral Security Dialogue is an informal partnership between Australia, India, Japan and the United States, which was formed in part to counter China. It was proposed by Prime Minister Shinzo Abe of Japan in 2007.

Beijing opposed the initial discussion, lodging formal diplomatic protests. Australia withdrew in 2008 and the dialogue was put on hold for some time. But the grouping was revived in 2017 on the sidelines of an ASEAN conference.

President Biden was the first U.S. president to convene a meeting among the leaders of the nations as a foursome. In 2024, he hosted the Quad leaders at his home in Wilmington, Del. Mr. Trump has appeared to be less enthusiastic about the partnership. Last year, he did not attend the Quad summit in India.

Reporting from the village of Manabayucan in the Philippines and the capital, Manila

In May, Roseller Rodriguez, a mayor in the Philippines, was startled to learn from news reports that U.S. officials had arrived in his district to start an artificial intelligence hub.

He saw a video of senior American and Filipino officials standing on a makeshift stage, unveiling a new initiative called Pax Silica.

Mr. Rodriguez had not been invited. Nor had he ever heard about the hub. Was it a data center? Where would the water and electricity come from? What would happen to the farmers living there?

Months later, he says he has few answers.

“I’m not concerned with the geopolitics of Pax Silica,” Mr. Rodriguez said in his office in the municipality of Capas. “My problem is Pax Silica will be built in our community.”

The United States started Pax Silica to unite partners around A.I. supply chains independent of China. The initiative is part of President Trump’s effort to counter Beijing’s economic and technological influence.

Trump administration officials have cast it as a 21st-century successor to Pax Americana, the era of relative peace among major powers after World War II.

The significance of Pax Silica, which 26 signatories have joined, has become heightened after revelations that Nvidia’s former chief executive, Jensen Huang, was targeted by Chinese intelligence, exposing the growing geopolitical stakes of the global A.I. race.

In the Philippines, America’s oldest ally in Asia, the backlash has been fierce.

Dozens of Filipino performers greeted President Trump as he descended the stairs of Air Force One onto the tarmac of Ninoy Aquino Airport in Manila, waving flags and playing “Y.M.C.A.” by the Village People on drums and trumpets.

President Vladimir V. Putin could meet with Kim Jong-un if the North Korean leader also travels to China for the APEC summit this week, Russian state news agencies reported, citing Dmitri S. Peskov, the Kremlin’s spokesman. Before his departure for China, Putin met on Monday with a secretary of North Korea’s ruling Workers’ Party. It was not yet clear whether the North Korean leader would attend the summit.

President Trump has landed in Manila, lingering on the tarmac for a welcome ceremony before walking alone to Marine One. He has several events tonight, but he will not leave his hotel for them. A meeting with quad leaders, a U.S.-Thailand trade deal signing and a dinner will all be held at the resort where the president is staying. In other words, he showed up late to this summit, and the summit will be coming to him.

Thousands of protesters have assembled in downtown Manila, chanting in Tagalog, “Trump leave the Philippines.” They are opposed to his Pax Silica A.I. supply chain initiative.

President Trump’s stopover at the Association of Southeast Asian Nations summit is part of a state visit to the Philippines, where he will be received with a 21-gun salute.

The last time he met President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines, he was full of praise for the Filipino leader.

“Great family, great family legacy and highly respected in his country,” Mr. Trump said while hosting Mr. Marcos at the White House last year.

But that legacy was long considered an obstacle to Mr. Marcos’s political ambitions. He is the son of the dictator Ferdinand E. Marcos, who ruled the Philippines for 20 years, imposed martial law and was ousted in 1986 by a nonviolent uprising. The elder Marcos died in exile in Hawaii, without facing trial on U.S. criminal charges that he had plundered the Philippine Treasury. Years later, a court found his estate liable for torture, summary executions and disappearances.

The younger Marcos spent years rehabilitating his family’s image and won election in 2022. He is hosting Mr. Trump at his official residence, Malacañang Palace, which was also his childhood home.

Mr. Marcos and his wife, Liza Marcos, will receive Mr. Trump on Wednesday with a 21-gun salute. Mr. Trump is expected to sign the palace guest book, hold closed-door meetings, pose for a handshake, then attend a state luncheon held in his honor.

It is not clear whether Mr. Trump, who has a well-known affinity for autocratic leaders, will meet with Mr. Marcos’s mother, Imelda, 97, with whom he has socialized in New York. Ms. Marcos wielded immense power when her husband was president and later came to be known for her flamboyant lifestyle, shoe collection and unexplained wealth.

Mr. Marcos has said that when he called to congratulate Mr. Trump on his election victory in November 2024, Mr. Trump asked about Imelda Marcos.

“My mother is really his friend, he knows my mother very well,” Mr. Marcos said, relaying the conversation. “He was asking about her, ‘How’s Imelda?’ I told him she sends her congratulations.”

After the pomp and circumstance at Malacañang, which traces its roots to the Spanish colonial era in the Philippines, Mr. Trump is headed to Shenzhen, China. The highlight of that leg of his trip will be a trilateral meeting with two strongmen: President Xi Jinping of China and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

For the second year in a row, President Trump is attending the annual gathering of Southeast Asian leaders, a show of alliance to a region that the United States sees as a strategic and maritime counterweight to China.

But Mr. Trump reached the gathering, held this year in Manila, only on Tuesday, just as the summit was wrapping up, and he is not expected to make any significant policies or diplomacy moves.

Mixed signals like this — to say nothing of Mr. Trump’s punishing tariffs — are adding to a widespread belief within ASEAN, the Association of Southeast Asian Nations, that the United States is not a reliable partner. That leaves the bloc balancing between Washington and Beijing, with which many of its members have deep trade partnerships.

“They’ve learned that the United States is not reliable, so a lot of the hedging has been away from the United States,” said Ted Osius, a former U.S. ambassador to Vietnam.

The Philippines, America’s oldest security ally in Asia, provides a window into this balancing act.

After taking office three years ago, President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. reversed his predecessor’s tilt toward China and pivoted back toward the United States. That stance contributed to many tense encounters between Filipino and Chinese vessels in the South China Sea, where Manila and Beijing have competing territorial claims.

As U.S. patrols in the South China Sea have dropped, Mr. Marcos has appeared to be more conciliatory toward Beijing. In August, he said that there was a “distinct possibility” that the Philippines and China could partner on oil and gas exploration in those waters despite their territorial feud.

“Marcos Jr. already has begun a shift toward China, one that a single visit by Trump is unlikely to stop,” Joshua Kurlantzick, a senior fellow for Southeast Asia and South Asia at the Council on Foreign Relations, wrote last week.

On Sunday, China and ASEAN failed to agree on a code of conduct in the South China Sea — something the Philippines, which has some of the biggest stakes in the dispute, had vowed to secure while chairing the bloc this year.

Chester Cabalza, president of International Development and Security Cooperation, a Philippine think tank, said: “Strategically, Manila opens its doors for conciliation with Beijing to stabilize normal ties. But on the ground, the real battle has yet to subside.”

Domestically, Mr. Marcos has to walk a tightrope, too.

“For Filipino fishermen and coastal communities, the South China Sea is not simply about a geopolitical dispute. It concerns livelihoods, access to maritime resources and national dignity,” said Arnaud Leveau, president of the Asia Centre, a Paris-based think tank. Any diplomatic accommodation perceived as sacrificing Filipino interests, he said, would therefore be politically risky.

The wider region has been battered by the consequences of another Trump policy: the war in Iran. Southeast Asia depends heavily on oil and gas imports from the Middle East and was roiled by soaring prices of fuel. In the Philippines, crops rotted in fields because farmers could not afford to transport them to markets. In Thailand, public servants were asked to use stairs instead of elevators to reduce energy expenses.

ASEAN comprises 11 nations — Brunei, Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, Malaysia, Myanmar, the Philippines, Singapore, Thailand, Vietnam and, since last year, East Timor — with vastly different degrees of freedom and wealth. The bloc represents almost 700 million people, or almost 10 percent of the global population.

Making decisions on a consensus-basis and pledging not to interfere in one other’s internal affairs, the bloc has often been criticized as slow and ineffective, with the situation in Myanmar often cited as an example. A coup in that country in 2021 unleashed a civil war that ranks among the world’s most violent conflicts.

Myanmar’s leaders remain barred from ASEAN summits, under an agreement that was extended on Monday. Still, almost half of the bloc’s member states have had bilateral engagements with Myanmar since the coup leader, Min Aung Hlaing, used stage-managed elections to take the post of president this year. Washington has also recently re-engaged in direct talks with Myanmar.

Mr. Trump’s attendance at the summit, which concludes with an ASEAN-U.S. meeting, is part of a state visit to the Philippines. He is expected to hold a bilateral meeting with Mr. Marcos on Wednesday before heading to China for an Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit. Mr. Marcos has said little publicly about meeting Mr. Trump; but earlier this year, he described Washington as a “steadfast partner.” His administration has hinted that the two sides are working on a military aid agreement.

In recent days, hundreds of demonstrators have gathered in Manila to protest Mr. Trump’s visit. They oppose his “Pax Silica” initiative, a move to build a supply chain for the artificial intelligence industry that bypasses China, saying it would exploit Philippine natural resources.

Renato Reyes, who led one of the protest groups on Sunday afternoon, said, “Marcos is so desperate to welcome Donald Trump that he will roll out the red carpet, even if Trump arrives late for the summit.”

Aie Balagtas See contributed reporting from Manila, and Damien Cave from Ho Chi Minh City, Vietnam.

President Trump sets off this week for meetings in Asia that will culminate in a summit with two strongmen he has often praised: President Xi Jinping of China and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

It will be the first time the three men, who command the world’s largest nuclear arsenals, will have sat down together. Mr. Trump plans to discuss the reduction of nuclear weapons in the world and the war against Iran, according to a White House spokeswoman. He is not expected to bring up Russia’s war with Ukraine.

Mr. Trump will travel first to Manila, then to southern China, and will meet with regional leaders confronting an array of uncertainties. Those include the course of the war between the United States and Iran, high energy prices and mounting concern over the risks of artificial intelligence.

For Mr. Trump, the trip will be a pivot to foreign policy and relationships after American voters delivered a swift rebuke to his party in the midterm elections. His trip also comes as his administration faces scrutiny over a scandal involving the former chief executive of Nvidia, the tech giant, which has raised questions about Chinese espionage and American tech exports.

The highlight of Mr. Trump’s trip is expected to be his meeting with Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin on Thursday in China.

The prospect of their summit has drawn comparisons to the 1945 conference in the Crimean city of Yalta, where the leaders of the United States, the Soviet Union and Britain met to decide the shape of postwar Europe.

The White House has said that Mr. Trump wants to discuss the threat of nuclear proliferation. U.S. officials have expressed concerns about China’s accelerating nuclear buildup, expected to reach 1,000 nuclear warheads by 2030.

The last nuclear arms control treaty between the United States and Russia expired in February. Trump administration officials have said that any new agreement on nuclear arms control must include China, but Beijing has shown no interest in the topic. China has an estimated 620 nuclear warheads, compared with the more than 5,000 that Russia and the United States have each, according to the Stockholm International Peace Research Institute.

U.S. administration officials said they did not expect a deal out of the meeting, which they characterized as a starting point for discussions.

The gathering has prompted concern in Ukraine that Mr. Putin might push for a deal, deciding Ukraine’s fate without Kyiv’s involvement. A U.S. administration official said Ukraine was not expected to be part of the discussion between the three leaders.

Mr. Trump will start his trip in the Philippines, the oldest American treaty ally in the Asia-Pacific region, as the United States faces questions about its support for its partners in a region where China’s military and economic might continues to grow.

In Manila, President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. plans to discuss with Mr. Trump the South China Sea, where the Philippines and China have competing claims. Philippine officials have said that they hope Mr. Trump’s visit might result in more American military assistance.

Mr. Trump is also expected to meet with leaders of ASEAN, a Southeast Asian grouping of 11 nations, as well as a gathering of the so-called Quad grouping of the United States, India, Australia and Japan.

Prime Minister Anthony Albanese of Australia said on Sunday that he expected “lots of engagement” with Mr. Trump during the meetings.

“Obviously, that’s our most important ally,” he said. “But we want to see something constructive come out of that, including we want to see a de-escalation in the conflicts that are around the world.”

Chinese analysts described the meeting of the Quad group, formed in part to counter China, as an effort by Mr. Trump to project power after his party’s defeat in the midterms. “Its symbolic value outweighs its practical value,” Xu Liping, a senior scholar at Guangdong University of Foreign Studies, told Shanghai Observer, a state-run outlet.

After Manila, Mr. Trump will travel to the Chinese tech hub of Shenzhen, where he will meet Mr. Xi on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation.

Jensen Huang, a key adviser who accompanied Mr. Trump on his last trip to China, will not be by his side. Mr. Huang helped shape the president’s views about technological competition with China and persuaded him to loosen restrictions on selling advanced chips to China.

Mr. Huang stepped down from his role at Nvidia after revelations that he had an affair with a Chinese businesswoman, Alice Huang, who U.S. intelligence officials believe was collecting intelligence on Mr. Huang for Beijing. China has rejected the allegations, calling them “smears.” Ms. Huang has not responded to requests for comment.

So far, the president has not raised the issue with Mr. Xi and is not expected to during their meeting on Thursday. But the saga is likely to cause unease in China about whether Washington will impose tighter controls on the export of technology to the country.

As a result, U.S.-based analysts speculated that Mr. Xi might try to use his personal relationship with Mr. Trump to forestall such restrictions.

Yun Sun, director of the China program at the Stimson Center in Washington, said Mr. Huang’s downfall would likely mean “there is going to be more anxiety on the Chinese side about their access to American chips.” She expects Mr. Xi to emphasize the importance of easing U.S. controls on chip exports to China during their meeting.

Cooperation between China and the United States on artificial intelligence may be more difficult after the Nvidia scandal.

Still, there may be progress on the creation of a notification system that would allow the countries to alert each other about national security issues related to artificial intelligence, which the two sides agreed to in September.

“There’s some shared concern on both sides that U.S. or Chinese models might be misused by bad actors and cause damage to the U.S., China, or other countries,” said Kyle Chan, a fellow at the Brookings Institution focusing on China’s technology development.

When Mr. Xi sits down with Mr. Trump in Shenzhen, he will be dealing with a president who will soon face a new constraint at home: a Democrat-controlled Congress.

Lawmakers may pressure Mr. Trump to tighten export controls against China or accelerate weapons sales to Taiwan, according to Ali Wyne, a senior research and advocacy adviser focusing on U.S.-China relations at the International Crisis Group. That means that “Xi will have to work much harder to keep Trump on side,” Mr. Wyne said.

Mr. Trump will be the first American president to visit China twice in one calendar year.

Analysts say the leaders’ summits, which have included state visits in Beijing and then in Washington, have been the only thing holding together a fragile détente between the two countries as they clash over A.I., export controls, Taiwan and other issues.

The two sides declared a trade truce last year when they met in Busan, South Korea. That deal, which was to expire this month, has been extended until January.

China’s restrictions on the export of crucial rare-earth metals and rare-earth magnets, which are needed to manufacture most modern technology, remain one of the most difficult unresolved issues.

Mr. Trump is likely to raise this with Mr. Xi, according to a senior U.S. administration official who said that China’s continued restrictions is one reason Washington only agreed to a two-month extension of the trade truce.

Ana Swanson contributed reporting from Washington, Aie Balagtas See from Manila, and Laura Chung from Sydney, Australia.

#161. UK will miss climate targets if it weakens EV sales rules, advisers warn

Analysts say mooted move to let more petrol and diesel vehicles be sold would wreck plan to reach net zero by 2050

Weakening the UK’s electric car sales rules would probably cause the government to miss legally binding climate targets, according to advisers tracking Britain’s progress to net zero.

The analysis by the Climate Change Committee (CCC) suggests the policy would blow a hole in the plan for achieving net zero carbon emissions by 2050 – prompting concerns that Andy Burnham’s government will have to slash the UK’s carbon emissions reduction targets, known as the carbon budgets.

In a letter published on Wednesday, the CCC said the UK is “likely to miss” its climate targets if it confirms plans to allow more sales of petrol and diesel cars and vans in the next four years.

Those changes could result in an extra 8.4m tonnes of CO2 pollution a year from the UK by next decade.

The electric vehicle rules are the single biggest contributor to those climate goals, meaning the government will have to find other major carbon emission reductions, unless it amends the budgets to allow more pollution.

Carmakers must sell a bigger proportion of electric cars each year under the rules, known as the zero emission vehicle (ZEV) mandate, or face fines of £12,000 per vehicle.

However, the industry has lobbied intensively for months to cut the headline targets from 80% electric by 2030 to as low as 50% or 60%, arguing that the mandate was putting them under too much financial pressure.

Electric cars were 26.6% of UK new car sales during the first 10 months of 2026, well below the 33% headline target. However, carmakers are expected to comply with the mandate using a series of loopholes known as “flexibilities”.

The committee’s chair, Nigel Topping, wrote to Heidi Alexander, the transport secretary, to oppose any reduction of the targets.

Such cuts would, he said, “put the government’s commitment to tackling climate change at risk, miss an opportunity to reduce costs for a typical household by about £800 a year, increase the UK’s exposure to volatile oil markets by up to 15m barrels of oil a year in 2030, and damage long-term competitiveness as our main export markets rapidly transition to electric vehicles”.

Some in government have become alarmed in recent months about the impact the government’s various changes could have on the UK’s ability to meet its climate commitments.

The government has just cut funding for installing heat pumps, the second-biggest decarbonisation policy. Burnham has also alienated some Labour MPs by waving through gas extraction from the Jackdaw gasfield, and considering approval for the massive Rosebank oilfield.

MPs voted earlier this year to approve a new set of carbon reductions for the years 2038-42.

However, the Guardian has learned that one cabinet minister in the Keir Starmer government wanted the government to go back to parliament to scrap those planned reductions and enact a more conservative target instead.

The idea did not come to fruition though, with other cabinet ministers mostly opposed.

An industry source with knowledge of discussions in government said that serious consideration was still being given to changing the carbon budgets in the run-up to the UN’s Cop31 climate conference, subject to parliamentary approval.

The source said it was not clear if the proposal was still being discussed. A government source strongly denied that it was under consideration.

The changes to the mandate also mean the UK is unlikely to meet the carbon budget covering 2033 to 2037.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Several analysts said the government will struggle to find emissions reductions elsewhere, given the scale of pollution from petrol and diesel cars and vans. The policy could also face opposition from the governments of Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland, each of which have their own carbon budgets.

Burnham on Thursday told Cop31 in Antalya, Turkey, that he was “proud to support this Cop’s focus on electrification” and urged people to choose electric cars and heat pumps.

Doug Parr, chief scientist at Greenpeace UK, said: “The Burnham government cannot talk of climate leadership on the international stage while doing a U-turn on ZEV targets at home.”

Topping added: “Weakening the zero-emission vehicle mandate would miss an opportunity to reduce the cost of living for people, undermine Britain’s competitiveness and leave us more exposed to volatile fossil fuel prices.

“People are buying electric vehicles in record numbers because they are cheaper to own than the alternative.

“The mandate gives those up and down the supply chain the certainty they need to invest, supports thousands of jobs, and keeps the UK on track to meet its climate commitments.

“British drivers deserve better than a backtrack on such critical policy.”

The car industry criticised the CCC’s analysis. Mike Hawes, chief executive of the Society for Motor Manufacturers and Traders, said: “A successful EV transition needs a strong market and a competitive industry.

“The CCC, however, ignores the damaging cost of the targets it defends: billions in unsustainable discounts and millions more in government incentives.

“Contrary to the CCC’s claims, the mandate is deterring local investment, as however much manufacturers export, without a strong local market there is no business case to build here.”

A government spokesperson said: “We remain committed to the phaseout of all new petrol and diesel cars by 2030 and to all new car and van sales being zero emission by 2035, as well as to our legally binding net zero targets.

“Our consultation on the mandate will not change this and will ensure we are taking a pragmatic and balanced approach to the ZEV transition.”

#160. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

#157. American soldier held in UK suspected of spying for Russia taken into US military custody

Lt Col Brandon Carroll Tyner was arrested by British police on 10 November over allegations related to espionage

A US soldier who was arrested in the UK on suspicion of spying for Russia has been taken into custody by the US military, police have said.

Lt Col Brandon Carroll Tyner, 41, was detained by British counter-terrorism police ‌on 10 November in Gloucestershire over allegations relating ⁠to espionage.

The arrest was part of an investigation by UK Counter Terrorism Policing, working with authorities including the US military and the FBI.

The FBI director, Kash Patel, said in a statement on Tuesday: “This FBI has zero tolerance for anyone who betrays their oath to the United States or puts our national security at risk.

“That’s been demonstrated by the 167 counterintelligence and espionage arrests we’ve made over the last 21 months, a 53% increase.

“An army officer is in custody as the result of an espionage investigation and the close cooperation between the FBI’s Philadelphia field office and Legat London and our partners at UK Counter Terrorism Policing, MI5, UK Defence Counter Intelligence, and the US Army Counterintelligence Command.

“Together we worked to identify a potential threat to national security and take swift action.”

The suspect, a senior soldier, was based at Imjin Barracks in Innsworth, Gloucestershire, which serves as the headquarters of Nato’s Allied Rapid Reaction Corps, the Guardian understands.

He was arrested on suspicion of offences under section 1 and section 3 of the National Security Act and was in custody at a London police station.

US authorities have said Tyner joined the military in 2008 as a senior human resources officer, and was deployed overseas to Iraq, Qatar and Afghanistan.

He was identified on Friday after British counter-terror police secured more time to question him.

As part of the investigation, officers carried out searches at two locations in Gloucestershire, the Metropolitan police said last week.

Assistant commissioner Laurence Taylor, head of Counter Terrorism Policing: said: “Following an extensive investigation, CTP presented our case to the CPS [Crown Prosecution Service], who in turn engaged with the attorney general’s office and American partners. Subsequently, a decision was made to pass the investigation to them to progress.

“This decision was made in line with existing international protocols.

“These are extremely serious allegations, and our collective response should send a strong message that we will act swiftly and robustly where we suspect this kind of espionage activity taking place.

“I’d like to praise the excellent work of the investigation team here in the UK to get us to this point and we will continue to support our US colleagues as they take the matter forwards.”

The investigation is not linked to any other ongoing Counter Terrorism Policing investigations.

US Brig Gen Sean Stinchon said the case “should serve as a stark warning to any service member suspected of violating the oath we all take to protect and defend the constitution”.

He added: “Whenever we suspect somebody of betraying the trust of their fellow soldiers and jeopardising our collective defence, we will identify you, and we will ensure justice is served.

“This effort is a great example of the remarkable partnership Army Counterintelligence Command has with army prosecutors, the FBI, DOJ [Department of Justice], and the UK.”

#156. Grenades, grief and a deepening political divide: five takeaways from Sonia Gandhi’s new memoir

From the assassinations of her husband and mother-in-law to press stonewalling under Modi, Belonging offers an insider’s account of India’s political evolution

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

Few love stories have been as closely entwined with a nation’s history as that of Sonia and Rajiv Gandhi. The former’s much-awaited political memoir, Belonging, offers a strikingly candid account of her journey from a small town in Italy to the highest echelons of power in India – all for love, she says.

The veteran Congress party leader has been fiercely protective of her personal world. In Belonging, she opens up for the first time about the extraordinary twists and turns of a life that has largely unfolded in full public view. (The Guardian’s Hannah Ellis-Petersen has a rare in-person interview with Gandhi, and you can read an excerpt from the book here.)

Belonging, published today, contains moving glimpses of Gandhi’s private grief. She writes about the loss of her first child, who died shortly after birth, and the chilling discovery of grenades in a room of their house after Operation Blue Star, the 1984 military operation against Sikh separatists at the Golden Temple in Amritsar. She also provides a ringside view of several turbulent chapters in Indian politics, not least the violent assassinations of her mother-in-law, Indira Gandhi, in 1984 and her husband, Rajiv, in 1991.

Her book also revisits some of the darker episodes that have dogged the Gandhi family and the Congress party, including Indira’s imposition of the Emergency in June 1975, a 21-month period during which civil liberties were suspended, political opponents were jailed and the press was gagged, which caused fear and dismay across the country. In Gandhi’s account, it also caused a rift within the family.

Ultimately, Gandhi’s memoir is a vivid record of six decades of India’s political transformation from the Congress-dominated secular republic she moved to in 1968 until today, when Hindu nationalism has become the dominant political force under Narendra Modi.

Here are five key takeaways from her memoir and what they reveal about the state of Indian politics today.

Gandhi has had no real contact with Modi for the last 12 years

Gandhi’s account offers a glimpse of how deep the political polarisation runs in Indian politics today. In one revealing passage, Gandhi writes that she has had “practically no real contact” with Modi in the 12 years he has led the nation. In fact, at times when the two have been in the same rooms, such as the speaker’s small chamber, Modi “tends to look away, avoiding eye contact”, she says. On occasion, she adds, he has asked briefly about her health after a hospital stay.

For her, this breakdown is a departure from the past, when leaders on opposing sides of the aisle “would treat one another with cordiality”. After the 2001 terror attack on parliament, Gandhi immediately called then-BJP prime minister AB Vajpayee to enquire about his safety, a gesture he appreciated.

Gandhi sees an increasingly authoritarian style of politics, in which those in power seek to “belittle and torment” opponents. For years, Gandhi’s son, Rahul, has been the subject of BJP’s ridicule, which calls him Pappu, a derisive term intended to portray him as inexperienced and naive. She adds that similar things are happening elsewhere in the world, an observation that can be read in the context of US President Donald Trump’s use of personal insults and ridicule against opponents.

It’s lonely at the top for India’s female politicians

India has an illustrious history of women occupying top leadership positions, long before many other democracies. But their rise has often come at a cost – that of loneliness in navigating a male-dominated political world.

In the book, Gandhi describes a 2012 conversation with Hillary Clinton on the countrys’ female leaders and their backgrounds. In her recollections of Indira, she shares that, despite wielding authority, Indira could feel besieged and profoundly alone, and this was particularly acute, Gandhi writes, in the months leading up to the declaration of the Emergency by her mother-in-law.

Gandhi also writes of her own isolation, including when she decided to take on the leadership of the Congress out of a sense of duty to her family’s political legacy.

Those in power are increasingly stonewalling the press

Among the many ways in which Gandhi charts the erosion of India’s democratic conventions in Belonging, one particularly telling shift is the changed relationship between the media and those in power. She recalls a time when journalists had opportunities to question prime ministers directly.

In 12 years in power, Modi has not addressed a single press conference on his own. He has sat in on one press briefing at the conclusion of an election campaign, and appeared alongside US presidents at joint press encounters after bilateral meetings, where they took questions from the press. Modi has consistently avoided facing journalists in an open forum, and his media interactions have largely been one-on-one interviews with sympathetic, pro-government news anchors or in one much publicised exchange with a Bollywood actor who asked him how he liked to eat mangoes.

The book’s publication has brought renewed attention to concerns about the space for free expression in contemporary India, and the extent to which apprehensions about repercussions may influence what can be said, written and published.

Penguin Random House India was to publish Gandhi’s memoir but withdrew from the project in August. Gandhi told the Guardian that it was “uneasy in general with the criticism of the government” and said it had dropped the book after she declined certain suggested edits. Penguin has disputed her account, saying that the suggestion it decided not to publish the book because of refused editorial changes “does not accurately reflect the circumstances”.

The book, released in the US by Alfred A Knopf, is being published in India by HarperCollins.

In June, Penguin had also pulled out of plans to distribute graphic journalist Joe Sacco’s The Once and Future Riot, about the 2013 Muzaffarnagar communal violence. The publisher cited an inaccurate map of India and unresolved citation issues. Sacco, however, blamed the decision on the “current political climate in India”.

She’s had quiet fallouts with the ‘thoroughly dishonest’

Western political memoirs, particularly by former heads of government or senior politicians, are often forthcoming in naming political adversaries, recounting private exchanges and offering candid assessments of colleagues. David Cameron’s 2019 memoir, For the Record, took aim at his former friend, Michael Gove, describing him as “mendacious” and a “foam-flecked Faragist”.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Gandhi, in contrast, is restrained. She writes warmly of the closeness between the Gandhi family and that of Bollywood superstar Amitabh Bachchan, whose parents she lived with when she first moved to India, but she makes no mention of the subsequent estrangement between the two families. In another instance, she does not name the political leader she describes as “thoroughly dishonest” and who she says she trusted in 1999, when Congress unsuccessfully attempted to form a coalition government.

Readers from India and around the world on the festival of lights and tell us how they celebrated.

double quotation markIn India, Diwali comes to you. You can see it in the streets, hear it in the neighbourhood and feel it in the atmosphere. In the UK, it is different. Life carries on around you. But traditions do not have to disappear when you move countries – they can change shape. A small light in a quiet room can carry the same meaning as an entire street illuminated for Diwali. double quotation markMy favorite part of Diwali back home was getting up before sunrise on Narak Chaturdashi and having a ceremonial bath, followed by a breakfast of traditional Marathi Diwali snacks. I have been living abroad for over 10 years now and it’s always a bittersweet time of the year where I miss my family and the general vibe of Diwali in India. But I am trying to build new traditions as one half of an Indian-Austrian couple, which includes trading the traditional boxes of mithai for Manner Schnitten and marzipan. Charu, Germany

double quotation markThere used to be more of a community spirit to Diwali. As kids, we would be tasked with going door to door, exchanging sweets and snacks with neighbours (sometimes to our great displeasure, because it made for awkward conversation with those we had annoyed with our antics). Nowadays, the celebrations are mostly private. Group celebrations are walled off within exclusive clubs, families light their own fireworks almost competitively, and people send each other sweets through a delivery service. Samyuktha, Chennai

The government has approved the sale of a 60.7% stake in IDBI bank to Canadian Fairfax Holdings for Rs 52,878 crore (£4.22bn).

It’s hard to take one’s eyes off Muhammed Riswan when he’s at work. The 23-year-old football freestyler from Areekode in Kerala’s Malappuram district conjures tricks with the ball in a delicately choreographed dance. It glides between his feet, next it is rolling on to his back right before he leaps into the air to catch it with one hand.

Riswan, who’s been freestyling for eight years, is one of the two Indians to win Instagram Rings 2026 award recognising creators. The other is Chanel model Bhavitha Mandava.

Riswan’s big break came with a video filmed at Keralamkundu waterfall, where he pulled off a freestyle move before gracefully arching the ball into the water. The clip has since clocked more than 600m views. Last year, he was invited to watch his sporting idol, Lionel Messi, play in Argentina.

#155. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#152. Disgraced former leader Sheikh Hasina plans return to Bangladesh from exile

Former prime minister faces death penalty upon return after being sentenced in absentia over deaths in 2024 uprising

Sheikh Hasina is “confident” she will return to Bangladesh in December, according to exiled members of her party, after the disgraced former prime minister applied for a new passport this week.

Hasina has been living in self-imposed exile since August 2024 when she was toppled from power by a mass uprising against her authoritarian rule. She crossed the border to India, where she has since been allowed to live freely in Delhi.

Last November, a tribunal in Bangladesh found Hasina guilty of crimes against humanity and sentenced her to death over her crackdown on a student uprising that left an estimated 1,400 people dead.

Her return to Bangladesh is likely to be disruptive for the country where Hasina’s party, Awami League, is still banned and thousands of its members are in jail. Should she be allowed to return, she is likely to be arrested immediately.

Hasina first raised the prospect of going back to Bangladesh in August. Speaking to the Guardian this week, several exiled Awami League leaders also living in India confirmed that Hasina was serious and “confident” about returning by next month. They confirmed that several other party figures had already returned in anticipation.

On Monday, a British lawyer acting on behalf of Hasina wrote to the Bangladesh high commission in Delhi requesting a new passport and national identity card to be issued “in a timely fashion”, in a letter seen by the Guardian.

“The reason our leader Sheikh Hasina has applied for a passport is that she genuinely wants to return,” said a party leader based in the Indian city of Kolkata, who spoke on condition of anonymity because he is not authorised to speak to the media on the party’s behalf.

Hasina has rejected the charges against her and claimed the tribunal that convicted her was illegitimate. “She is confident about her return, which is why she has applied for a new passport and a national identity card. If the authorities do not issue her a passport, she will apply for a travel pass to make her return to Bangladesh possible,” said the Awami League leader.

Since Hasina’s conviction, the Bangladesh government has put pressure on the Indian authorities to extradite the former prime minister. However, the Indian government, which was close to Hasina while she was in power, has ignored the demands, straining ties between the two countries.

This week, the Bangladesh government said that, as a “terrorist”, Hasina would only be allowed to return via an “acceptable prisoner exchange” under the India-Bangladesh extradition treaty.

Aminul Islam, the chief prosecutor of the tribunal that sentenced Hasina, said there was “no legal scope” for her to be issued a new passport as she was a “convicted fugitive”.

Islam emphasised that Hasina would be hanged if she returned, as the deadline for her to appeal against her conviction had passed. “Sheikh Hasina has no option but to face the execution of the verdict,” he said.

Despite the abuses and oppression that mired her 15 years in power and subsequent conviction for crimes against humanity, Hasina still commands significant popularity among the thousands of Awami League cadre still in Bangladesh, many of whom have been driven underground.

Analysts said they believed her decision to return was driven by an attempt to revive her support base and destabilise the newly elected government, led by Tarique Rahman. It came to power in February during Bangladesh’s first democratic election in decades.

The government has since struggled with inflation and protests that shut down garment factories, which the Awami League hopes to use to its advantage.

Another Awami League leader said all those in exile intended to follow Hasina back to Bangladesh, despite many of them facing serious criminal charges and having been given lengthy prison sentences in absentia.

“Some of us have been sentenced in absentia by kangaroo courts, and we know we will be jailed. But we are ready to face the consequences and return to our own people. We will fight this politically,” said the senior leader, who is based in India.

Some senior party figures have returned in recent months, including the former MP Mustaque Ahmed Ruhi. He was immediately arrested on his return in September and faces prosecution over an alleged murder during the 2024 uprising.

Exiled Awami League leaders have continued to meet in Kolkata, where many of them are living, and are routinely summoned to Delhi to meet Hasina.

Speaking over email this week, Hasina told the Associated Press: “If death is my destiny, I would rather die on the soil of my own country, among my own people.”

#149. World’s top court to rule if Myanmar committed genocide against Rohingya

International court of justice has never before found a state guilty of genocide, and ruling will have implications for case against Israel

The world’s top court will deliver a judgment this week on whether Myanmar committed genocide against its Rohingya minority, in a landmark case that will set a critical 21st-century benchmark for the gravest of all international crimes.

The closely watched judgment could become one of the most significant moments in international law since the end of the second world war.

The international court of justice (ICJ) has never found a state responsible for genocide, a crime defined after the Nazi-led Holocaust. The ruling will have implications for a separate high-profile genocide case against Israel.

Nearly a decade after a military campaign forced hundreds of thousands of Muslim Rohingya to flee across the border, the ICJ in The Hague will deliver its judgment on Thursday morning.

“The ICJ ruling will be historic for Rohingya survivors and their families around the world,” said Yanghee Lee, a former UN rights investigator to Myanmar.

“The international community failed to prevent Myanmar military atrocities against the Rohingya nine years ago. It must seize this opportunity to finally secure justice for the Rohingya and begin to reckon with these failings.”

ICJ judges have spent seven years weighing accusations by the Gambia, a Muslim-majority state, that Myanmar’s repression of the Rohingya breached the 1948 UN genocide convention.

A key issue in previous cases has been how to prove “intent” in a crime defined as acts committed with the “intent to destroy, in whole or in part” a national, ethnical, racial or religious group.

Antonia Mulvey, the founder of Legal Action Worldwide (Law), a group that represents hundreds of Rohingya survivors, said she was “cautiously optimistic” the court would hold Myanmar fully accountable.

Government actions showed “patterns of conduct” that proved clear attempts to destroy the group, she said.

“The attacks on the villages took place the same way,” she said. “They would attack the civilians, burn the villages, separate men and women, conduct sexual violence … and then as they fled, they would continue to kill them.”

Two other ICJ cases dealing with atrocities in the Balkans fell short of finding states directly responsible for genocide, although Serbia was found legally responsible for failing to prevent the genocide at Srebrenica.

Mulvey said a key part of the upcoming judgment will be how it handles the issue of rape and sexual violence as an act of genocide. Her team has collected accounts of soldiers mutilating women’s genitals and biting their faces. “That’s to mark people, to brand them … it is also to stop reproduction,” she said.

“Yes, we would say sexual violence is a critical part to show their intent to destroy the group,” she added.

“You don’t need to gang-rape people. You don’t need to kill their children in front of them. You don’t need to mutilate them. You don’t need to do it in public. What is the purpose of that? The purpose is to destroy the group.”

In January, the ICJ held closed-door sessions to hear directly from Rohingya survivors who travelled from camps in Bangladesh. One witness said her child was taken from her arms, stabbed and thrown into a fire, according to Law, while another described seeing piles of bodies in a mass grave.

During hearings, Gambian justice minister Dawda Jallow told the court the case was not “about esoteric issues of international law”.

“It is about real people, real stories and a real group of human beings,” said Jallow. “The Rohingya of Myanmar. They have been targeted for destruction.”

Myanmar, which has denied carrying out a genocide, claims its actions targeted a military uprising from insurgents.

For decades, Rohingya people have been stripped of their citizenship and targeted by increasing discrimination, amid a backdrop of communal violence with Buddhists in the country’s western Rakhine state.

The crisis reached a climax after a Rohingya militant group conducted guerrilla-style attacks in the summer of 2017. The Myanmar army responded with massive “clearance operations”.

Refugees who fled into Bangladesh in 2017 told the Guardian of a massacre in the village of Tula Toli, where survivors described systematic shooting of civilians, including the drowning of children in a river.

This testimony from Tula Toli – which satellite footage later showed had been razed – added to dozens of witness accounts collected over the years to build a case for genocide.

A ruling that Myanmar committed genocide would add international pressure on its leader Min Aung Hlaing, who headed the military during the 2016-2017 attacks and later overthrew the elected government of Aung San Suu Kyi in a 2021 coup, triggering a civil war that has left tens of thousands dead.

The former general is separately b​eing investigated by the international criminal court (ICC), which prosecutes individuals and not states like the ICJ, on allegations of crimes against humanity. He was installed as civilian president in April after elections that were widely condemned as a sham, and has made efforts to rebuild diplomatic ties.

For many onlookers, the ICJ decision will hold relevance far beyond Myanmar, as it will provide the strongest hint of how judges might assess the seminal genocide case brought by South Africa against Israel over its campaign in Gaza.

The cases have much in common, with an ethnic group targeted by a powerful military, although Israel has far stronger diplomatic backing from the US.

The judgment comes as Washington has been on the offensive against international human rights bodies, including sanctioning the ICC. Washington technically already considers the Rohingya crisis to be a genocide, based on a decision by the Biden administration.

However, Trump’s UN ambassador, Mike Waltz, warned that the ICJ “must not be allowed” to establish a standard for genocide that could be used against “allied soldiers”, a statement that will be relevant for Israeli troops.

Supporters of the Myanmar case say the judgment should compel states to block arms transfers and jet fuel supplies. While the court does not have the means to enforce its decisions, they have been respected in the past, and the UN security council could give it strength, although divisions, especially between the US and China, make this unlikely.

More than 1 million Rohingya remain in Bangladesh, in the world’s largest refugee settlement. Fewer than 600,000 are estimated to remain in Myanmar.

A displaced Rohingya living inside Myanmar told the Guardian he hoped the judgment would “help end decades of persecution and discrimination”.

“We are not asking for sympathy alone,” he said. “We are asking for justice, recognition, equal rights and a future in which our children can live safely and with dignity in their own homeland.”
